INSTRUCTION: ADD
FORMAT: add rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: In the RV64I ISA, the ADD instruction MUST add its two source registers as 64-bit integers and write the 64-bit sum to the destination register.
RULE 2: In the RV64I ISA, 32-bit integer addition is performed by the ADDW instruction, and the ADD instruction MUST NOT perform a 32-bit addition.
RULE 3: ADD computes the XLEN-bit integer sum of the values held in source registers rs1 and rs2 and produces an XLEN-bit result.
RULE 4: ADD writes the computed result into the destination register rd.
RULE 5: ADD never raises any arithmetic exception, including overflow, underflow, or other arithmetic traps.
RULE 6: If an ADD instruction produces a result that exceeds the XLEN‑bit representable range (i.e., signed or unsigned overflow occurs), then the processor does not raise an exception: mcause is unchanged and the destination register receives the low XLEN bits of the mathematically correct sum (wrap‑around).
RULE 7: In RV64I, a 32-bit signed addition can be verified by comparing the result of ADD with the result of ADDW on the same operand values.
RULE 8: ADD reads the integer values from source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the resulting value to destination register rd.
RULE 9: ADD computes the integer sum of the values read from rs1 and rs2.
RULE 10: ADD discards any overflow beyond the XLEN width and writes only the low XLEN bits of the sum into rd.
RULE 11: ADD adds the integer values from its two source registers and writes the sum to the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: ADD.UW
FORMAT: add.uw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: ADD.UW reads the 32-bit low word of the register specified by rd'/rs1' and produces a result in which bits 31-0 equal those original bits and all bits more significant than bit 31 are set to zero.
RULE 2: ADD.UW writes the zero-extended result to the same register specified by rd'/rs1', replacing the register's previous value.
RULE 3: ADD.UW does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd'/rs1'.
RULE 4: ADD.UW has an architectural effect that is identical to the effect of the c.zext.w instruction applied to the same register.
RULE 5: ADD.UW must be defined only on RV64 implementations; on RV32 the encoding is reserved and its behavior is not defined.
RULE 6: In an ADD.UW instruction the source register specified with the .uw suffix provides an unsigned 32-bit operand that is taken from the low 32 bits of that register.
RULE 7: In an ADD.UW instruction the source register that is not designated with the .uw suffix contributes its full XLEN-bit value to the operation.
RULE 8: ADD.UW adds the unsigned 32-bit operand from the .uw source register to the full-XLEN operand from the other source register using full-XLEN arithmetic and produces an XLEN-wide result.
RULE 9: ADD.UW writes the XLEN-wide result of the addition into the destination register rd.
RULE 10: ADD.UW is only available in the RV64 configuration and is not defined for RV32.
RULE 11: ADD.UW adds the unsigned word values from the two source registers and writes the resulting sum to the destination register.
RULE 12: ADD.UW computes X(rd) as the XLEN-bit sum of the value in register rs2 and the zero-extended 32-bit value taken from bits 31..0 of register rs1.
RULE 13: For ADD.UW the operand derived from rs1 is formed by filling the upper XLEN-32 bits with zeros after extracting bits 31..0 of X(rs1).
RULE 14: The addition performed by ADD.UW is modulo 2^XLEN, so any overflow is discarded and only the low XLEN bits of the sum are written to rd.
RULE 15: When rs2 is the zero register, ADD.UW writes to rd the zero-extended lower word of rs1, producing the same architectural result as a zero-extension operation.

INSTRUCTION: ADDI
FORMAT: addi rd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: ADDI writes its XLEN-bit result to the destination register rd.
RULE 2: ADDI adds the signed value in source register rs1 to the sign-extended immediate and stores the XLEN-bit sum in rd.
RULE 3: ADDI does not raise any arithmetic exception, including on overflow.
RULE 4: ADDI MUST add the sign-extended 12-bit immediate to the value in register rs1 and write the sum to register rd.
RULE 5: If the addition produces a value wider than XLEN bits, ADDI MUST ignore arithmetic overflow and write only the low XLEN bits of the sum to rd.
RULE 6: When the 12-bit immediate field of ADDI is zero, ADDI MUST copy the value of rs1 into rd, implementing the MV pseudoinstruction.
RULE 7: If a PUSH sequence adjusts the stack‑pointer with any instruction other than an ADDI that adds an immediate to the stack‑pointer, then after the sequence the stack‑pointer register will not equal the original value minus the total number of bytes pushed.
RULE 8: ADDI uses a signed immediate operand, allowing negative values such as –64.
RULE 9: ADDI adds the sign-extended immediate to the value read from the source register and writes the 32-bit sum to the destination register.
RULE 10: When the destination register specified by ADDI is identical to its source register, the register is overwritten with the sum of its original content and the sign-extended immediate.
RULE 11: ADDI adds the sign-extended immediate to the value in the source register to produce an architectural result.
RULE 12: The architectural result of an ADDI instruction is written to the destination register specified by the instruction.
RULE 13: When the immediate field of an ADDI instruction is -1, the architectural result equals the source register value minus one.

INSTRUCTION: ADDIW
FORMAT: addiw rd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: If an ADDIW instruction is executed on a processor that does not implement RV64I (e.g., an RV32I core), then it raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 2: ADDIW adds the sign-extended 12-bit immediate to the 64-bit value in rs1, discards any overflow by taking the low 32 bits of the sum, and writes to rd the sign extension of those 32 bits to 64 bits.
RULE 3: When the immediate field of ADDIW is zero, ADDIW writes to rd the sign extension of the lower 32 bits of rs1, acting as the assembler pseudoinstruction SEXT.W.

INSTRUCTION: ADDW
FORMAT: addw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: ADDW is defined only for RV64I implementations and is not defined for RV32I.
RULE 2: ADDW adds the low 32 bits of the two source registers as signed 32-bit integers and discards any overflow, keeping only the low 32 bits of the sum.
RULE 3: ADDW sign-extends the low 32-bit sum to 64 bits and writes the resulting 64-bit value to the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: AND
FORMAT: and rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The AND instruction writes the result of its operation into the integer register identified by rd.
RULE 2: The AND instruction computes the bitwise logical AND of the values read from rs1 and rs2.
RULE 3: The AND instruction MUST compute the bitwise logical AND of the values in registers rs1 and rs2 and write the resulting XLEN-bit value to register rd.
RULE 4: The AND instruction MUST NOT modify any architectural registers other than rd.
RULE 5: The AND instruction MUST not raise an exception or trap for any defined encoding of the instruction.

INSTRUCTION: ANDI
FORMAT: andi rd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: ANDI must compute the bitwise logical AND of the value in source register rs1 and the sign-extended 12-bit immediate operand.
RULE 2: ANDI must write the result of that bitwise AND operation into destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: ANDN
FORMAT: andn rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The ANDN instruction computes rd = rs1 & ~rs2 (bitwise AND of rs1 with the bitwise complement of rs2) and writes the XLEN-wide result to rd.
RULE 2: Because the ANDN mnemonic has no .b, .h, .w, or .uw suffix, the ANDN instruction operates on the full XLEN width of its source registers and produces an XLEN-wide result.
RULE 3: ANDN computes the destination register rd as the bitwise AND of the first source register rs1 and the bitwise complement of the second source register rs2.
RULE 4: ANDN treats the operation as if the rs2 operand is inverted before the logical AND is performed.
RULE 5: ANDN produces a result whose width equals the XLEN of the executing hart (32 bits on RV32 and 64 bits on RV64).
RULE 6: The ANDN instruction computes rd = rs1 & ~rs2, performing a bitwise AND of the first source operand with the bitwise complement of the second source operand.
RULE 7: The ANDN instruction operates on XLEN-bit values, where XLEN is 32 for RV32 and 64 for RV64.
RULE 8: The ANDN instruction MUST compute for every bit position *i* the logical AND of the *i*-th bit of register *rs1* and the logical NOT of the *i*-th bit of register *rs2*, and MUST write the resulting word to register *rd*.
RULE 9: The ANDN instruction MUST NOT modify the contents of registers *rs1* or *rs2*.
RULE 10: The ANDN instruction MUST NOT affect any architectural status flags or cause side effects other than updating *rd*.
RULE 11: The ANDN instruction computes rd as the bitwise logical AND of the value in rs1 and the bitwise inversion of the value in rs2.
RULE 12: ANDN computes the bitwise AND of the value in source register rs1 and the bitwise NOT of the value in source register rs2, and writes the XLEN-wide result to destination register rd.
RULE 13: ANDN operates on the full XLEN bits of the registers, where XLEN equals 32 for RV32 and 64 for RV64.

INSTRUCTION: AUIPC
FORMAT: auipc rd, uimm20
RULE 1: AUIPC uses the U-type format and its U-immediate field supplies the upper 20 bits of a 32-bit offset with the lower 12 bits forced to zero.
RULE 2: AUIPC computes the sum of the address of the AUIPC instruction and the 32-bit offset formed by concatenating the U-immediate with twelve zero bits, and writes this sum into the destination register rd.
RULE 3: AUIPC can be paired with a subsequent load or store instruction whose 12-bit immediate is added to the value produced by AUIPC to access any 32-bit PC-relative data address.
RULE 4: When the U-immediate field of AUIPC is zero, the instruction writes the current PC value (the address of the AUIPC instruction) into rd.
RULE 5: AUIPC forms a 32-bit offset by placing the 20-bit U-type immediate in bits 31:12 and setting bits 11:0 to zero.
RULE 6: AUIPC sign-extends the 32-bit offset to the processor's XLEN before using it.
RULE 7: AUIPC adds the sign-extended offset to the address of the AUIPC instruction itself.
RULE 8: AUIPC writes the result of that addition into the architectural register specified by rd.
RULE 9: AUIPC paired with JALR can form address offsets whose signed values lie in the interval [-2^31-2^11 , 2^31-2^11-1].

INSTRUCTION: BCLR
FORMAT: bclr rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: BCLR clears a single bit of the value read from register rs1.
RULE 2: BCLR selects the bit to clear using the bit index supplied by register rs2.
RULE 3: BCLR writes the resulting value, with the selected bit cleared, to destination register rd.
RULE 4: BCLR writes to destination register rd the value of source register rs1 with the bit at the position indicated by the low log2(XLEN) bits of source register rs2 cleared to zero, leaving all other bits of rs1 unchanged.
RULE 5: BCLR determines the bit index to clear by extracting the low log2(XLEN) bits from rs2 and interpreting that value as an unsigned position between 0 and XLEN-1.

INSTRUCTION: BCLRI
FORMAT: bclri rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: BCLRI is defined for both RV32 and RV64 implementations and therefore must be a legal instruction for processors with XLEN equal to 32 or 64.
RULE 2: Execution of BCLRI writes to rd the value of rs1 with the bit at position imm cleared to zero while preserving all other bits of rs1.
RULE 3: BCLRI writes to rd the value of rs1 with the single bit at the index specified by shamt cleared to zero while all other bits of rs1 remain unchanged.
RULE 4: BCLRI obtains the bit index to clear by extracting the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt immediate field.

INSTRUCTION: BEXT
FORMAT: bext rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: BEXT extracts the bit at the index given by the low-order bits of rs2 from the value in rs1 and writes a zero-extended result of either 0 or 1 to rd.
RULE 2: An RV64 implementation MUST implement the BEXT instruction.
RULE 3: When BEXT is executed, it MUST compute an index value equal to the contents of rs2 masked with the value (XLEN − 1), i.e., index = X(rs2) & (XLEN-1).
RULE 4: BEXT MUST shift the contents of rs1 right by the computed index and then mask the result with 1, i.e., X(rd) = (X(rs1) >> index) & 1.
RULE 5: The architectural result written by BEXT to rd is guaranteed to be either 0 or 1.
RULE 6: BEXT does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: BEXTI
FORMAT: bexti rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: BEXTI extracts the bit of register rs1 at the position given by the immediate imm and writes the extracted bit (zero-extended) to destination register rd.
RULE 2: BEXTI extracts the single bit from the source register rs1 at the bit position specified by the immediate imm and writes that extracted bit to the destination register rd.
RULE 3: BEXTI MUST compute the bit index as the bitwise AND of the shamt field and the value XLEN-1, thereby using the lower log2(XLEN) bits of shamt.
RULE 4: BEXTI MUST place in destination register rd the least-significant bit of the value obtained by logically right-shifting source register rs1 by the computed index.

INSTRUCTION: BINV
FORMAT: binv rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: If a BINV instruction executes with source registers rs1 and rs2 and destination register rd, then after execution rd contains rs1 with the single bit at the position specified by the low‑order bits of rs2 inverted (i.e., rd = rs1 ⊕ (1 << (rs2 mod XLEN))).
RULE 2: BINV MUST produce a result in rd that equals the value in rs1 with the single bit whose index is given by the value in rs2 inverted, leaving all other bits unchanged.
RULE 3: BINV writes to rd the result of X(rs1) XOR (1 shifted left by index), i.e., X(rd) = X(rs1) ^ (1 << index).
RULE 4: BINV does not cause any architectural side effects other than writing the destination register; it does not modify memory or any other registers.
RULE 5: BINV never raises an exception for any value of rs2; the operation is defined for all possible contents of rs2.

INSTRUCTION: BINVI
FORMAT: binvi rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: When a BINVI instruction executes, the destination register rd receives the value of source register rs1 with the single bit at the position specified by the immediate operand inverted.
RULE 2: Execution of a BINVI instruction MUST NOT modify any integer register other than the destination register rd.
RULE 3: Execution of a BINVI instruction MUST NOT modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 4: BINVI rd, rs1, shamt MUST compute index as shamt & (XLEN-1) (i.e., the lower log2(XLEN) bits of shamt) and write to integer register rd the value X(rs1) XOR (1 << index).
RULE 5: BINVI rd, rs1, shamt MUST leave all integer registers other than rd unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: BLT
FORMAT: blt rs1, rs2, label
RULE 1: BLT performs a signed two's-complement comparison of the values in its first source register and its second source register.
RULE 2: If the signed value in the first source register of a BLT instruction is less than the signed value in the second source register, BLT transfers control to the target label specified by the instruction.
RULE 3: If the signed value in the first source register of a BLT instruction is not less than the signed value in the second source register, BLT does not transfer control to the target label and execution continues with the following sequential instruction.
RULE 4: The signed comparison used by BLT treats the register contents as two's-complement integers, so the sign bit determines ordering for negative and positive values.

INSTRUCTION: BLTU
FORMAT: bltu rs1, rs2, label
RULE 1: BLTU MUST be a branch instruction that performs an unsigned-less-than comparison of the unsigned integer values in its first source register and its second source register.
RULE 2: BLTU MUST take the branch to the target address encoded in its immediate when the unsigned value in the first source register is less than the unsigned value in the second source register.
RULE 3: BLTU MUST not take the branch (execution continues with the following instruction) when the unsigned value in the first source register is greater than or equal to the unsigned value in the second source register.
RULE 4: When BLTU takes the branch, the program counter is updated to the address of the label specified by the branch immediate.
RULE 5: When BLTU does not take the branch, the program counter is incremented to the address of the instruction following BLTU.
RULE 6: Using BLTU after an unsigned addition with the addition result as the first operand and one of the addends as the second operand MUST cause the branch to be taken if unsigned overflow occurred, thereby providing a single-instruction method for overflow checking of unsigned addition.

INSTRUCTION: BNE
FORMAT: bne rs1, rs2, label
RULE 1: BNE MUST compare the value in its first source register with the value in its second source register and take the branch when the two values are not equal.
RULE 2: BNE MUST NOT take the branch when the value in its first source register is equal to the value in its second source register, and execution proceeds to the sequential next instruction.
RULE 3: When BNE takes the branch, it MUST sign-extend the immediate offset field, add the sign-extended offset to the address of the BNE instruction (the current pc), and use the resulting sum as the branch-target address.

INSTRUCTION: BREV8
FORMAT: brev8 rd, rs1
RULE 1: If a BREV8 instruction executes, then the destination register rd contains the byte‑reversed value of the source register rs.
RULE 2: Executing BREV8 reverses the order of bits within each byte of the value held in rs and writes the concatenation of the reversed bytes to rd.
RULE 3: In an RV64 implementation, BREV8 processes the full 64 bits of rs, reverses the bits in each of the eight bytes, and writes the resulting 64-bit value to rd.
RULE 4: The BREV8 instruction reverses the bits in each byte of the source operand and writes the resulting value to the destination register.
RULE 5: BREV8 reverses the order of the bits within each 8-bit byte of the value read from the source integer register rs1.
RULE 6: BREV8 writes each bit-reversed source byte into the same byte position of the destination integer register rd, leaving the relative ordering of bytes unchanged.
RULE 7: BREV8 updates the architectural integer register rd with the xlen-bit result that consists of the concatenation of all bit-reversed bytes of rs1, so that every bit of rd is defined after the instruction executes.
RULE 8: BREV8 rd, rs MUST set X(rd) to an XLEN-bit value whose each byte i (for i = 0 … XLEN/8-1) equals the bits of byte i of X(rs) reversed in order.
RULE 9: BREV8 rd, rs reads the architectural register specified as rs and writes the architectural register specified as rd, and MUST NOT modify any other architectural state.
RULE 10: BREV8 rd, rs reverses the order of the eight bits in each byte of the source register so that bit 0 becomes bit 7, bit 1 becomes bit 6, …, and bit 7 becomes bit 0.
RULE 11: The result produced by BREV8 rd, rs has a width equal to the implementation's XLEN, consisting of the reversed-bit bytes concatenated to form an XLEN-bit word.
RULE 12: The BREV8 instruction MUST reverse the order of bits within each byte of the source operand.
RULE 13: The BREV8 instruction MUST write the byte-wise bit-reversed result to the destination register, preserving the register width of the executing ISA (32 bits for RV32, 64 bits for RV64).
RULE 14: BREV8 MUST treat each active element as SEW bits partitioned into bytes and replace each byte with the reversal of its bit order.
RULE 15: BREV8 MUST not alter any architectural state other than the contents of vd and the normal program-counter advancement.
RULE 16: If BREV8 does not process all active elements, then it fails to retire successfully (the RETIRE_SUCCESS flag is not set).
RULE 17: BREV8 must produce an output value in which, for every byte of the input, bit 0 is placed in bit 7, bit 1 in bit 6, …, and bit 7 in bit 0 of the corresponding output byte.
RULE 18: BREV8 must unconditionally swap each bit with its opposite position within each byte, independent of the data values.

INSTRUCTION: BSET
FORMAT: bset rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: If a BSET instruction executes, then after execution the architectural state shows that rs1 and rs2 retain their pre‑instruction values and rd contains the result of applying the BSET operation to the values of rs1 and rs2.
RULE 2: BSET rd, rs1, rs2 sets the bit in the value read from rs1 at the index given by the integer value in rs2 to 1 and writes the resulting word to rd.
RULE 3: BSET rd, rs1, rs2 leaves all bits of the rs1 value unchanged except the bit at the index specified by rs2, which is forced to 1.
RULE 4: BSET rd, rs1, rs2 does not alter any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 5: BSET must compute the bit index by masking the XLEN-bit value of rs2 with the constant XLEN-1, thereby using only the lower log2(XLEN) bits of rs2.
RULE 6: BSET must create a mask value equal to the integer 1 shifted left by the computed index, using XLEN-bit logical left-shift semantics.
RULE 7: BSET must set the destination register rd to the bitwise OR of the XLEN-bit value of rs1 and the mask value produced from the shift.
RULE 8: BSET must produce a result in rd that is identical to the value of rs1 with exactly one bit set at the position indicated by the lower log2(XLEN) bits of rs2.

INSTRUCTION: BSETI
FORMAT: bseti rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: Execution of the BSETI instruction performs a single-bit set where the bit position is specified by the immediate operand.
RULE 2: BSETI sets the bit in the source register rs1 whose index is specified by the immediate operand imm.
RULE 3: BSETI writes the value produced after setting the specified bit to the destination register rd.
RULE 4: BSETI preserves all bits of rs1 other than the bit indexed by imm unchanged in the result written to rd.
RULE 5: BSETI reads the bit index from the lower log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt operand.
RULE 6: BSETI produces a result equal to the value in rs1 with the bit at the index read from shamt set to one, and writes this result to rd.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADD
FORMAT: c.add rd, rs2
RULE 1: When a trap is taken on the target instruction, an implementation should ignore the C.ADD-encoded NTL hint for the first instruction in the trap handler.
RULE 2: If an interrupt occurs between the execution of a C.ADD-encoded NTL hint and its target instruction, execution resumes at the target instruction without re-executing the hint and without changing program semantics.
RULE 3: C.ADD adds the integer values in registers rd and rs2 and writes the resulting sum to register rd.
RULE 4: C.ADD has the same architectural effect as executing the 32-bit ADD instruction with operands rd, rd, rs2.
RULE 5: C.ADD MUST NOT be considered valid when the encoded rs2 field equals x0; such encodings correspond to C.JALR or C.EBREAK instead.
RULE 6: C.ADD encodings with rs2 ≠ x0 and rd = x0 are defined as HINTs and do not perform the addition operation.
RULE 7: C.ADD x0, rs2 is defined as an RVC hint and therefore does not modify any architectural state other than advancing the program counter and updating any applicable performance counters.
RULE 8: C.ADD must have a non-zero second source register; encodings with rs2 = 0 are not C.ADD.
RULE 9: If the destination register field rd of a decoded C.ADD instruction is zero, the implementation must treat the instruction as a hint and must not produce any architectural effect.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDI4SPN
FORMAT: c.addi4spn rd, sp, c_nzuimm10
RULE 1: C.ADDI4SPN adds the zero-extended immediate, multiplied by 4, to the stack pointer register x2 and writes the resulting value to the destination register rd′.
RULE 2: An encoding of C.ADDI4SPN with uimm equal to 0 is reserved and must be treated as an illegal instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDI16SP
FORMAT: c.addi16sp sp, c_nzimm10
RULE 1: When the register field of a C.MOP.n encoding contains x2, the instruction must be decoded as C.ADDI16SP.
RULE 2: C.ADDI16SP writes its result to register x2 (the stack pointer).
RULE 3: C.ADDI16SP adds a sign-extended 6-bit immediate that must be non-zero to the current value of register x2.
RULE 4: C.ADDI16SP scales its immediate by 16, so the addition uses multiples of 16.
RULE 5: C.ADDI16SP produces a result that must lie in the range –512 to 496 inclusive.
RULE 6: C.ADDI16SP has the same architectural effect as executing the 32-bit instruction addi x2, x2, nzimm[9:4].

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDI
FORMAT: c.addi rd, c_nzimm6
RULE 1: C.ADDI adds the sign-extended 6-bit immediate to the current value of register rd and writes the result back to rd for encodings where rd ≠ x0 and the immediate is non-zero.
RULE 2: C.ADDI has the same architectural effect as an addi rd, rd, imm instruction.
RULE 3: When a C.ADDI encoding has rd ≠ x0 and the immediate field equal to zero, the instruction is defined as a HINT and must not modify any architectural state.
RULE 4: When a C.ADDI encoding has rd = x0 and the immediate field equal to zero, the instruction is interpreted as C.NOP and performs no architectural effect.
RULE 5: When a C.ADDI encoding has rd = x0 and the immediate field not equal to zero, the instruction is defined as a HINT and must not modify any architectural state.
RULE 6: Executing C.ADDI with immediate zero acts as a hint that does not modify any architectural state except that the program counter is advanced and any applicable performance counters are updated.
RULE 7: Executing C.ADDI with immediate zero overwrites the destination register with a copy of its original value, resulting in no change to architectural state.
RULE 8: C.ADDI with an assembled immediate value of zero is classified as a microarchitectural hint (HINT) and does not perform an addition operation.
RULE 9: Execution of C.ADDI with an immediate of 0 overwrites its destination register with the register's current value.
RULE 10: C.ADDI with an immediate of 0 must write the destination register with its current value, leaving the register unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDIW
FORMAT: c.addiw rd, c_imm6
RULE 1: C.ADDIW is defined only for RV64C implementations.
RULE 2: The immediate field of C.ADDIW is sign-extended from 6 bits to the operand width before being used in the addition.
RULE 3: When the sign-extended immediate of C.ADDIW is zero, the instruction produces the same architectural effect as a sign-extend-word (sext.w) of the destination register.
RULE 4: C.ADDIW adds the sign-extended immediate to the current value of the destination register, computes the result modulo 2^32, and then sign-extends that 32-bit result to 64 bits before writing it back to the destination register.
RULE 5: The architectural result of C.ADDIW is equivalent to executing the 32-bit-operand instruction addiw rd, rd, imm.
RULE 6: C.ADDIW must not be used with destination register x0; encodings where rd = x0 are reserved.
RULE 7: C.ADDIW MUST NOT have a zero register specifier; the rs1/rd field must be a non-zero register.
RULE 8: C.ADDIW implements the same architectural effect as the 32-bit ADDIW instruction from the application-class profile.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDW
FORMAT: c.addw rd, rs2
RULE 1: C.ADDW adds the values in registers rd′ and rs2′, takes the lower 32 bits of the sum, sign-extends that 32-bit result to 64 bits, and writes the sign-extended value to register rd′.
RULE 2: C.ADDW expands architecturally to the 32-bit-add instruction addw rd′, rd′, rs2′.
RULE 3: The encoding that identifies C.ADDW is defined only for the RV64 base ISA and is reserved in RV32.
RULE 4: C.ADDW uses the register specified by rd′/rs1′ as both the destination register and the first source register.

INSTRUCTION: C.AND
FORMAT: c.and rd, rs2
RULE 1: C.AND MUST compute the bitwise AND of the architectural values in the registers specified by rd′ and rs2′.
RULE 2: C.AND MUST write the result of that bitwise AND into the register specified by rd′, replacing its previous architectural value.
RULE 3: C.AND MUST have the same architectural effect as an AND instruction that uses rd′ as both the destination and first source register and rs2′ as the second source register.

INSTRUCTION: C.ANDI
FORMAT: c.andi rd, c_imm6
RULE 1: C.ANDI MUST compute the bitwise AND of the value in register rd′ and a sign-extended 6-bit immediate.
RULE 2: C.ANDI MUST write the result of the bitwise AND operation back to register rd′.
RULE 3: C.ANDI MUST treat its 6-bit immediate field as a signed value that is sign-extended to the XLEN before the AND operation.
RULE 4: C.ANDI MUST be architecturally equivalent to executing the base ISA instruction andi rd′, rd′, imm where imm is the sign-extended immediate.
RULE 5: C.ANDI MUST use the same register rd′ as both its source operand and its destination operand.
RULE 6: When the 2-bit opcode field inst[1:0] is 01 and the 3-bit field inst[15:13] is 100, the instruction MUST be decoded as C.ANDI.
RULE 7: The most-significant immediate bit (imm[5]) of a C.ANDI instruction MUST be taken from the encoding bit labeled imm[5].
RULE 8: The register specifier of a C.ANDI instruction MUST select the compressed register rs1′, which also serves as the destination register rd′.
RULE 9: C.ANDI MUST follow the same criteria as the uncompressed ANDI instruction defined in the RVI ISA.

INSTRUCTION: C.BEQZ
FORMAT: c.beqz rs1, label
RULE 1: C.BEQZ must transfer control to the branch target address if the value in register rs1′ equals zero.
RULE 2: C.BEQZ must continue execution at the sequential next instruction if the value in register rs1′ is not zero.
RULE 3: C.BEQZ computes its branch target address by sign-extending the immediate offset and adding the result to the address of the C.BEQZ instruction.
RULE 4: C.BEQZ may branch to any address within ±256 bytes of the address of the C.BEQZ instruction.
RULE 5: C.BEQZ has the same architectural effect as the 32-bit instruction beq rs1′, x0, offset, where offset is the sign-extended immediate used by C.BEQZ.

INSTRUCTION: C.BNEZ
FORMAT: c.bnez rs1, label
RULE 1: C.BNEZ's offset field is sign-extended and added to the current PC to produce the branch target address.
RULE 2: C.BNEZ can target a branch target address that lies within ±256 bytes of the PC.
RULE 3: C.BNEZ takes the branch when the value in rs1′ is not zero.

INSTRUCTION: C.EBREAK
FORMAT: c.ebreak
RULE 1: The encoding of C.EBREAK requires that the rs2 field of the compressed instruction be set to x0.
RULE 2: C.EBREAK causes a breakpoint exception that transfers control to the debugging environment.
RULE 3: The RVC Quadrant 2 encoding `100 | 1 | 0 | 0 | 10` MUST be decoded as the C.EBREAK instruction.
RULE 4: When a breakpoint exception is raised by a C.EBREAK instruction, the mtval CSR MUST be written with either zero or the virtual address of the faulting instruction.
RULE 5: When a breakpoint exception is raised by a C.EBREAK instruction, the mepc CSR MUST be written with the virtual address of the faulting instruction.
RULE 6: C.EBREAK performs the same architectural operation as the EBREAK instruction.
RULE 7: C.EBREAK raises a breakpoint exception and performs no other operation unless overridden by an external debug environment.
RULE 8: C.EBREAK sets the epc register of the receiving privilege mode to the address of the C.EBREAK instruction itself.
RULE 9: C.EBREAK causes a synchronous exception.
RULE 10: C.EBREAK is not considered retired.
RULE 11: C.EBREAK should not increment the minstret CSR.
RULE 12: C.EBREAK generates a breakpoint exception whose cause value is Breakpoint (3).
RULE 13: The breakpoint exception generated by C.EBREAK is synchronous.
RULE 14: C.EBREAK does not cause entry into Debug Mode.
RULE 15: If the breakpoint exception generated by C.EBREAK traps to M-mode or S-mode and sctrctl.BPFRZ=1, the breakpoint exception is not recorded.
RULE 16: C.EBREAK MUST raise a breakpoint exception.
RULE 17: When a breakpoint exception is raised by a C.EBREAK instruction, the architectural register stval is written with either zero or the virtual address of that C.EBREAK instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLD
FORMAT: c.fld fd, c_uimm8(rs1)
RULE 1: C.FLD loads a double-precision floating-point value from the memory address computed by its effective address into the floating-point register rd′.
RULE 2: C.FLD computes its effective address by zero-extending the immediate offset, multiplying that offset by 8, and adding the result to the contents of the base register rs1′.
RULE 3: C.FLD has the same architectural effect as executing the uncompressed instruction fld rd′, offset(rs1′) with the same rd′, rs1′, and offset fields.
RULE 4: The C.FLD instruction is only legal when the implementation also supports the Zca extension and the D extension.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLDSP
FORMAT: c.fldsp fd, c_uimm9(sp)
RULE 1: C.FLDSP loads a double-precision floating-point value from memory into the destination floating-point register rd.
RULE 2: C.FLDSP computes its effective address by adding the zero-extended offset, scaled by eight, to the stack pointer register x2.
RULE 3: C.FLDSP expands to the 32-bit instruction fld rd, offset(x2).
RULE 4: C.FLDSP writes the floating-point register specified by the rd field of its encoding.
RULE 5: If the effective address computed for C.FLDSP has any of the low three bits set, the hardware masks those bits to zero, so the load reads from the aligned address and the destination register contains the value at that masked address.
RULE 6: The address used by C.FLDSP is transformed by the pointer-masking mechanism in the same way as for any other explicit load instruction.
RULE 7: When MXR is in effect at the effective privilege mode for the explicit memory access, pointer masking does not apply to the address used by C.FLDSP.
RULE 8: When MPRV or SPVP are in effect, pointer masking for C.FLDSP is performed according to the pointer-masking settings of the effective privilege mode.
RULE 9: C.FLDSP permits misaligned memory accesses, subject to the same limitations that apply when pointer masking is not in effect.
RULE 10: If a memory access is generated by a page‑table walk, an IOMMU, a device, or a DMA controller, then pointer masking is not applied and the effective address used for the access equals the original unmasked address, which can be observed by the access succeeding to a location that would be masked away for a CPU‑generated address.
RULE 11: C.FLDSP inherits the permission checks, exception conditions, and architectural effects of the base FLD instruction for explicit memory accesses.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLW
FORMAT: c.flw fd, c_uimm7(rs1)
RULE 1: C.FLW loads a 32-bit single-precision floating-point value from memory into the floating-point register rd′.
RULE 2: C.FLW computes its effective address by zero-extending the encoded offset, multiplying the zero-extended offset by 4, and adding the result to the contents of base register rs1′.
RULE 3: C.FLW performs the same architectural effect as executing the FLW instruction with the same destination register rd′, base register rs1′, and offset.
RULE 4: C.FLW must be decoded when the fetched instruction has bits [15:13] equal to 011 and bits [1:0] equal to 00.
RULE 5: In the encoding of C.FLW, the destination register specifier rd' is taken from bits 4-2 of the instruction.
RULE 6: A processor that implements the Zca extension must not define C.FLW as a valid instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLWSP
FORMAT: c.flwsp fd, c_uimm8(sp)
RULE 1: C.FLWSP loads a 32-bit single-precision floating-point value from memory into floating-point register rd.
RULE 2: C.FLWSP computes its effective address by adding the stack pointer x2 to the zero-extended offset field of the instruction after scaling the offset by 4 (i.e., left-shifting the offset by two bits).
RULE 3: C.FLWSP has the same architectural effect as executing the base ISA instruction flw rd, offset(x2).
RULE 4: C.FLWSP writes the 32-bit word loaded from memory into the floating-point register specified by the rd field.
RULE 5: C.FLWSP is defined only for RV32 implementations, and its encoding is reserved in RV64 or RV128 implementations.
RULE 6: C.FLWSP imposes no explicit privilege-level restrictions, generates no defined exceptions, and has no side-effects beyond performing the floating-point load.
RULE 7: C.FLWSP is defined only for RV32 implementations and is not defined for RV64.
RULE 8: C.FLWSP must load a 32-bit floating-point value from the memory address computed by its address operand into the destination floating-point register, exactly as the FLW instruction does.
RULE 9: C.FLWSP is an explicit memory access and therefore pointer masking applies to the effective address used by C.FLWSP.
RULE 10: C.FLWSP must support misaligned memory accesses, and the result of a misaligned access must be equivalent to applying the pointer-masking transformation to each aligned sub-access that composes the original misaligned request.
RULE 11: C.FLWSP must enforce the same permission checks and raise the same exceptions as the FLW instruction for any memory access.
RULE 12: C.FLWSP must not permit a misaligned access that would be illegal for an unmasked FLW; such an access must cause the same exception as defined for FLW.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSD
FORMAT: c.fsd fs2, c_uimm8(rs1)
RULE 1: C.FSD stores the double-precision floating-point value contained in floating-point register rs2′ to the memory location whose effective address is calculated by the instruction.
RULE 2: C.FSD computes its effective address by zero-extending the offset, multiplying the zero-extended offset by 8, and adding the result to the base address in register rs1′.
RULE 3: C.FSD is semantically equivalent to the standard instruction fsd rs2′, offset(rs1′).
RULE 4: C.FSD is a 16-bit instruction that stores a double-precision floating-point value from an F register to memory.
RULE 5: C.FSD is only defined when an implementation provides the C extension together with both the D and F extensions.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSDSP
FORMAT: c.fsdsp fs2, c_uimm9(sp)
RULE 1: If C.FSDSP is executed on a processor that does not implement the RV32DC or RV64DC extension, then an illegal‑instruction exception is raised (mcause = 2).
RULE 2: C.FSDSP stores the 64-bit double-precision floating-point value contained in floating-point source register rs2 to memory.
RULE 3: C.FSDSP computes the effective memory address by adding the stack pointer register x2 to the zero-extended immediate offset multiplied by 8 (i.e., shifted left three bits).
RULE 4: C.FSDSP has the same architectural effect as executing the 64-bit store instruction fsd rs2, offset(x2).
RULE 5: If the D extension or the F extension is not implemented, then executing a C.FSDSP instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 6: C.FSDSP MUST be subject to pointer masking because pointer masking applies to all explicit memory accesses.
RULE 7: C.FSDSP MUST support misaligned accesses, and the architectural effect is identical to applying the pointer masking transformation to each constituent aligned memory access.
RULE 8: If C.FSDSP fails to mask the effective address before the store, then the store writes to the unmasked address (observable by reading memory at the masked address versus the unmasked address).

INSTRUCTION: C.FSW
FORMAT: c.fsw fs2, c_uimm7(rs1)
RULE 1: C.FSW stores the single-precision floating-point value contained in floating-point register rs2′ to the memory location whose effective address is computed by the instruction.
RULE 2: C.FSW computes its effective address by adding a zero-extended immediate offset, multiplied by 4, to the base address held in integer register rs1′.
RULE 3: C.FSW has the same architectural effect as executing the 32-bit instruction fsw rs2′, offset(rs1′).
RULE 4: C.FSW MUST store a single-precision (32-bit) floating-point value to memory using the compressed instruction encoding.
RULE 5: The Zcf extension MUST provide C.FSW as the compressed form of a single-precision floating-point store operation.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSWSP
FORMAT: c.fswsp fs2, c_uimm8(sp)
RULE 1: C.FSWSP MUST store the single-precision floating-point value in floating-point register rs2 to the memory location whose effective address is the stack pointer (x2) plus the zero-extended offset field multiplied by 4.
RULE 2: C.FSWSP computes its effective address by zero-extending the offset field, scaling the result by 4, and adding that value to the current contents of the stack pointer register x2.
RULE 3: C.FSWSP behaves as if it were expanded to the 32-bit instruction fsw rs2, offset(x2).
RULE 4: C.FSWSP uses an immediate value formed by concatenating the bits labelled uimm[5:2] and uimm[7:6] from its encoding.
RULE 5: If executing C.FSWSP causes any exception, permission fault, or architectural state change other than storing the source register to memory at the address (sp + imm), then an illegal‑instruction exception is raised.
RULE 6: C.FSWSP stores a single-precision floating-point value from a floating-point register to memory.
RULE 7: C.FSWSP is a compressed (16-bit) instruction that performs a single-precision floating-point store operation.
RULE 8: If the Zcf extension is not implemented, then executing a C.FSWSP instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 9: C.FSWSP is defined only for the RV32 architecture.
RULE 10: C.FSWSP stores a single-precision floating-point value from a floating-point register to memory.
RULE 11: When the Zcf extension is not implemented, the encoding for C.FSWSP is reserved.
RULE 12: C.FSWSP maps to an explicit memory access that stores a 32-bit floating-point value to the effective address, equivalent in architectural effect to the base-ISA FSW instruction.
RULE 13: The effective address used by C.FSWSP is transformed by the pointer-masking operation that is in effect for the current privilege mode.
RULE 14: C.FSWSP supports misaligned memory accesses.
RULE 15: When C.FSWSP performs a misaligned store, the architectural result is identical to applying the pointer-masking transformation to each constituent aligned memory access that would result from splitting the misaligned store.

INSTRUCTION: C.J
FORMAT: c.j label
RULE 1: C.J performs an unconditional control transfer.
RULE 2: C.J sign-extends its immediate offset and adds the result to the current pc to compute the jump target address.
RULE 3: If the C.J immediate offset is not a multiple of 2 bytes, then executing the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 4: C.J may jump to any address within plus or minus 2 KiB of the current pc.
RULE 5: C.J expands to the base instruction jal x0 with the same offset.
RULE 6: If the transfer‑type filter bit for encoding 11 is implemented and set by software, then executing a C.J instruction must leave the CTR CSR unchanged (the CTR readback value is the same before and after the C.J).

INSTRUCTION: C.JAL
FORMAT: c.jal label
RULE 1: C.JAL computes its jump target address by sign-extending the encoded immediate and adding the result to the current pc.
RULE 2: C.JAL's sign-extended offset permits jumps to any address within ±2 KiB of the current pc.
RULE 3: C.JAL's immediate offset is required to be a multiple of 2 bytes.
RULE 4: C.JAL writes the address of the instruction following the jump (pc + 2) into the link register x1.
RULE 5: C.JAL has the same architectural effect as executing the base instruction jal x1, offset.
RULE 6: If a 16‑bit C.JAL instruction does not write the address of the next sequential instruction (original PC + 2) into register x1 or does not update the program counter to PC + (sign‑extended immediate << 1), then the architectural state is incorrect (x1 contains a value other than PC + 2 or PC does not equal the target address).

INSTRUCTION: C.JALR
FORMAT: c.jalr rs1
RULE 1: C.JALR must perform an indirect jump to the address contained in the source register rs1 and must write the address of the following instruction (pc + 2) into the link register x1.
RULE 2: C.JALR is only architecturally valid when the source register rs1 is not x0; encodings with rs1 = x0 are defined as the C.EBREAK instruction.
RULE 3: C.JALR must compute the link address by adding an offset of 2 bytes to the current pc, not the 4-byte offset used by the base-ISA jalr instruction.
RULE 4: C.JALR is defined only when the register source field rs1 encoded in the instruction is not zero.
RULE 5: C.JALR MUST perform an unconditional control transfer to the address contained in register rs1.
RULE 6: C.JALR MUST write the address of the instruction following the jump (pc+2) into the link register x1.
RULE 7: If the Zicfilp extension is enabled and a C.JALR instruction executes with rs1 not equal to x1, x5, or x7, then the hart MUST update the ELP to LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 8: For any C.JALR instruction the jump target is taken from the value in the specified rs1 register and is not encoded in the instruction opcode.
RULE 9: When a software-check exception caused by Zicfilp occurs in conjunction with C.JALR, its priority is higher than that of an illegal-instruction exception and lower than that of an instruction access-fault.

INSTRUCTION: C.JR
FORMAT: c.jr rs1
RULE 1: C.JR performs an unconditional control transfer to the address contained in register rs1.
RULE 2: C.JR expands to the base instruction jalr x0, 0(rs1).
RULE 3: C.JR is only defined when rs1 is not x0; encodings with rs1 equal to x0 are reserved.
RULE 4: The rs1 field of C.JR must be zero.
RULE 5: C.JR must set the program counter to the address contained in register rs1, performing an unconditional control transfer.
RULE 6: C.JR x5 must set the program counter to the value contained in register x5, which is the return address.
RULE 7: C.JR is defined as an indirect jump without linkage when its rs1 operand is neither x1 nor x5.

INSTRUCTION: C.LBU
FORMAT: c.lbu rd, c_uimm2(rs1)
RULE 1: C.LBU loads a single byte from memory at the effective address.
RULE 2: C.LBU zero-extends the loaded byte to the XLEN width of the executing hart.
RULE 3: C.LBU writes the zero-extended result to destination register rd′.
RULE 4: C.LBU forms its immediate uimm by setting bits 31:2 to zero, assigning bit 1 from instruction bit 5, assigning bit 0 from instruction bit 6, and then zero-extending uimm to XLEN bits for address calculation.
RULE 5: C.LBU adds the zero-extended immediate uimm to the value in source register rs1' (which is one of x8-x15) to compute the effective memory address.
RULE 6: C.LBU zero-extends the loaded byte to XLEN bits before any further processing.
RULE 7: C.LBU writes the zero-extended byte to destination register rd' (which is one of x8-x15).
RULE 8: In the abstract operation of C.LBU, the value written to rd' equals EXTZ(mem[X(rs1c) + EXTZ(uimm)][7..0]).
RULE 9: C.LBU uses rd' and rs1' from the standard compressed register set x8-x15.

INSTRUCTION: C.LD
FORMAT: c.ld rd, c_uimm8(rs1)
RULE 1: C.LD loads a 64-bit value from memory into the destination register rd′.
RULE 2: C.LD computes its effective address by adding to the base address in register rs1′ the zero-extended offset multiplied by 8.
RULE 3: C.LD has the same architectural effect as the 64-bit load instruction ld rd′, offset(rs1′).
RULE 4: C.LD forms its unsigned immediate offset by concatenating uimm[7:6] as the high bits with uimm[5:3] as the low bits, producing a zero-extended offset.
RULE 5: C.LD computes the effective memory address by adding the zero-extended offset to the value held in rs1′.
RULE 6: C.LD loads a doubleword from memory at the effective address and writes the loaded value into the destination register rd′.
RULE 7: C.LD is defined only for RV64 implementations and has no defined behavior on RV32 implementations.
RULE 8: C.LD reads a doubleword from the memory location whose address equals the current value in register sp.
RULE 9: C.LD writes the doubleword read from memory into register x5.
RULE 10: C.LD must be treated as an illegal instruction on RV64 implementations.
RULE 11: C.LD must be available only when the Zclsd extension is implemented, and the Zclsd extension requires that both the Zilsd and Zca extensions also be implemented.
RULE 12: C.LD must specify both its destination register and its base register using registers x8 through x15.
RULE 13: C.LD computes its effective address by adding the contents of base register rs1' to the zero-extended offset field multiplied by 8.
RULE 14: C.LD treats the offset field as an unsigned value that is zero-extended before being scaled by 8 for address calculation.

INSTRUCTION: C.LDSP
FORMAT: c.ldsp rd, c_uimm9(sp)
RULE 1: C.LDSP is an RV64C-only instruction that loads a 64-bit value from memory into the destination register rd.
RULE 2: C.LDSP computes its effective address by adding the stack pointer x2 to a zero-extended immediate offset multiplied by eight.
RULE 3: C.LDSP has the same architectural effect as executing an ld rd, offset(x2) instruction with the same offset.
RULE 4: C.LDSP is valid only when the destination register rd is not x0.
RULE 5: Encodings of C.LDSP where rd = x0 are reserved and have no defined architectural effect.
RULE 6: C.LDSP requires the destination register rd to be non-zero; an encoding with rd = 0 is reserved.
RULE 7: C.LDSP computes its effective memory address by sign-extending the instruction's offset field and adding the result to the current value of the stack pointer.
RULE 8: For C.LDSP, using register x0 as the destination register is reserved.
RULE 9: C.LDSP computes its effective address by zero-extending the immediate offset, multiplying the result by eight, and adding that value to the stack pointer register x2.
RULE 10: C.LDSP produces the same architectural effect as executing the standard instruction ld rd, offset(x2) with the same offset, loading the identical 64-bit value from the same address.
RULE 11: If a C.LDSP instruction encodes rd = x0, then the instruction is reserved and its effect is undefined (no guaranteed architectural state change).
RULE 12: C.LDSP, when executed as a CPU instruction, MUST apply the pointer-masking transformation to its effective memory address as defined for explicit memory accesses.
RULE 13: When MPRV or SPVP are set, the pointer-masking settings of the effective privilege mode MUST be applied to the effective address used by C.LDSP.
RULE 14: C.LDSP must support misaligned memory accesses, and the architectural effect of a misaligned access MUST be identical to applying the pointer-masking transformation to each constituent aligned access that would result from splitting the misaligned operation.

INSTRUCTION: C.LH
FORMAT: c.lh rd, c_uimm1(rs1)
RULE 1: C.LH loads a signed halfword from the memory address formed by adding the unsigned immediate offset to the value in source register rs1', sign-extends the 16-bit data, and writes the result to destination register rd'.
RULE 2: C.LH does not introduce any additional architectural permission checks or exception behavior beyond those defined for standard load instructions.
RULE 3: C.LH forms the immediate uimm by setting bits 31-2 to zero, bit 1 to the value of encoding bit 5, and bit 0 to zero, and then zero-extends this value to XLEN bits.
RULE 4: C.LH reads a 16-bit halfword from memory at the effective address.
RULE 5: C.LH sign-extends the 16-bit halfword loaded from memory to the processor's XLEN width.
RULE 6: C.LH writes the sign-extended XLEN-wide result into destination register rd'.
RULE 7: C.LH uses rd' and rs1' registers that are selected from the compressed register set x8-x15.

INSTRUCTION: C.LHU
FORMAT: c.lhu rd, c_uimm1(rs1)
RULE 1: C.LHU loads an unsigned halfword from the memory address formed by adding the unsigned immediate to the value in rs1' and writes the zero-extended result to rd'.
RULE 2: C.LHU forms its unsigned immediate uimm such that uimm[31:2] are zero, uimm[1] equals the instruction's bit 5, and uimm[0] is zero.
RULE 3: C.LHU computes the effective address by adding the zero-extended uimm to the value in rs1', where rs1' must be one of registers x8-x15.
RULE 4: C.LHU loads a 16-bit halfword from memory at the effective address computed from rs1' plus uimm.
RULE 5: C.LHU zero-extends the loaded 16-bit halfword to XLEN bits and writes the result to rd', where rd' must be one of registers x8-x15.
RULE 6: If a C.LHU instruction is executed, then it loads the 16‑bit value from the effective address, zero‑extends it into the destination register, and no exception is raised regardless of the current processor state.

INSTRUCTION: C.LI
FORMAT: c.li rd, c_imm6
RULE 1: Execution of C.LI writes the sign-extended 6-bit immediate imm into the architectural register identified by rd.
RULE 2: The architectural effect of C.LI is defined to be identical to the effect of the 32-bit instruction addi rd, x0, imm.
RULE 3: When the destination register field rd of C.LI is x0, the instruction is defined as a HINT and MUST NOT produce any architectural state change.
RULE 4: C.LI MUST have its destination register field set to x0.
RULE 5: Execution of C.LI MUST NOT modify any architectural state other than advancing the program counter and updating any applicable performance counters.

INSTRUCTION: C.LUI
FORMAT: c.lui rd, c_nzimm18
RULE 1: If a C.LUI instruction’s immediate field is zero, then executing it raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 2: C.LUI encodings with destination register x2 and a non-zero immediate are interpreted as the C.ADDI16SP instruction rather than as C.LUI.
RULE 3: C.LUI encodings with destination register x0 and a non-zero immediate are defined as hint instructions that produce no architectural state change.
RULE 4: C.LUI execution must advance the program counter to the address of the following instruction.
RULE 5: C.LUI encodings must have the destination register field rd equal to x0.
RULE 6: C.LUI MUST have its destination register rd distinct from registers x0 and x2.
RULE 7: C.LUI is a reserved instruction when its immediate field imm is zero, and such an encoding is reserved for future standard extensions.
RULE 8: C.LUI is treated as a hint when its destination register rd is register x0.

INSTRUCTION: C.LW
FORMAT: c.lw rd, c_uimm7(rs1)
RULE 1: C.LW must load a 32-bit value from memory into register rd′.
RULE 2: C.LW must compute its effective address by adding the value in register rs1′ to four times the zero-extended offset field encoded in the instruction.
RULE 3: C.LW must have the same architectural effect as the 32-bit lw instruction with the computed offset and base register rs1′ (i.e., lw rd′, offset(rs1′)).
RULE 4: C.LW MUST be a 16-bit compressed instruction that resides in quadrant 0, meaning its two least-significant opcode bits are 00.
RULE 5: C.LW MUST use the rs1′ field encoded in bits [9:7] as the base register for address calculation.
RULE 6: C.LW MUST use the rd′ field encoded in bits [4:2] as the destination register that receives the loaded word.
RULE 7: C.LW MUST be defined and non-reserved for the base ISA in both RV32 and RV64 implementations.
RULE 8: C.LW MUST have no additional operand restrictions beyond those implicit in its encoding fields.

INSTRUCTION: C.LWSP
FORMAT: c.lwsp rd, c_uimm8(sp)
RULE 1: C.LWSP loads a 32-bit value from memory into the destination register rd.
RULE 2: C.LWSP computes its effective address by adding to the stack pointer x2 a zero-extended offset that is multiplied by 4.
RULE 3: C.LWSP has the same architectural effect as executing the 32-bit load instruction lw rd, offset(x2).
RULE 4: C.LWSP encodes a destination register field rd that must satisfy rd ≠ 0.
RULE 5: If the rd field of a C.LWSP encoding is zero, then executing the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 6: In a C.LWSP encoding, the bit named uimm[5] provides the most-significant bit of the unsigned immediate used by the instruction.
RULE 7: In a C.LWSP encoding, the bits named uimm[4:2] and uimm[7:6] together supply the remaining lower bits of the unsigned immediate according to RVC conventions.
RULE 8: The unsigned immediate for C.LWSP is formed by concatenating uimm[5] as the high bit with the lower bits supplied by uimm[4:2] and uimm[7:6] in the order defined by the RVC encoding.
RULE 9: The effective address used by C.LWSP MUST be masked according to the pointer-masking rules that apply to all explicit memory accesses.
RULE 10: If executing C.LWSP on a masked address causes a permission check or exception that would not be raised by an equivalent LW to the same masked address, then the processor takes an unexpected exception (e.g., raises an illegal‑instruction exception).

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.1
FORMAT: c.mop.1
RULE 1: C.MOP.1 must not write any architectural register.
RULE 2: If a C.MOP.1 instruction attempts to read any architectural register, then it raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: The store performed by C.MOP.1 is identical to existing store instructions except that the base address is implicitly the shadow-stack pointer (ssp).
RULE 4: The store performed by C.MOP.1 is identical to existing store instructions except that the store width is implicitly XLEN.
RULE 5: When xSSE equals 1, C.MOP.1 stores the source register value to memory at address ssp-(XLEN/8).
RULE 6: The ssp register is decremented only if the store performed by C.MOP.1 completes successfully.
RULE 7: If xSSE ≠ 1 when C.MOP.1 is executed, then the store does not occur (the targeted memory location retains its pre‑execution value) and the ssp register remains unchanged (its post‑execution value equals its pre‑execution value).

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.3
FORMAT: c.mop.3
RULE 1: C.MOP.3 MUST NOT write any architectural register.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.5
FORMAT: c.mop.5
RULE 1: C.MOP.5 must not write any integer register.
RULE 2: If a C.MOP.5 instruction executes, then the XLEN‑bit value at the memory address held in ssp is loaded into its destination register.
RULE 3: C.MOP.5 performs its load, comparison, possible exception, and ssp increment only when the architectural flag xSSE is set to 1; if xSSE is 0 the instruction has no architectural effect.
RULE 4: C.MOP.5 may be used only with rs1 equal to x5; encodings that specify any other source register are reserved.
RULE 5: C.MOP.5 compares the loaded value to the value held in rs1 (x5) and raises a software-check exception with cause 18 and xtval set to "shadow stack fault (code 3)" if the values are not bitwise equal.
RULE 6: C.MOP.5 does not raise the software-check exception when the loaded value equals the value in rs1.
RULE 7: C.MOP.5 increments the shadow-stack pointer ssp by XLEN/8 bytes only if the load completes without an access-fault exception and without raising the software-check exception.
RULE 8: C.MOP.5 raises a software-check exception that has lower priority than any load/store/AMO access-fault exception that may be generated by the same memory access.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.7
FORMAT: c.mop.7
RULE 1: C.MOP.7 MUST NOT write any architectural register.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.9
FORMAT: c.mop.9
RULE 1: Execution of C.MOP.9 MUST NOT modify any integer register.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.11
FORMAT: c.mop.11
RULE 1: C.MOP.11 MUST NOT write any architectural register.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.13
FORMAT: c.mop.13
RULE 1: C.MOP.13 MUST NOT modify any architectural register file entry when it is executed.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.15
FORMAT: c.mop.15
RULE 1: C.MOP.15 MUST NOT write any architectural register.

INSTRUCTION: C.MUL
FORMAT: c.mul rd, rs2
RULE 1: C.MUL computes the product of the values in rsd' and rs2' and writes the lower XLEN bits of the product to rd' (the same register as rsd').
RULE 2: When a C.MUL instruction executes, the destination register (rsd') is written with the low XLEN bits of the signed 2 × XLEN‑bit product of the signed XLEN‑bit values held in registers rsd' and rs2'.
RULE 3: C.MUL writes the lowest XLEN bits of the signed 2*XLEN-bit product back into register rsd'.
RULE 4: In C.MUL the destination register rsd' and the source register rs2' must be selected from the compressed register set x8-x15.
RULE 5: C.MUL treats the register field rsd' as both the destination (rd') and the first source operand (rs1').
RULE 6: C.MUL does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rsd'.
RULE 7: If a C.MUL instruction is executed, then the destination register contains the lower XLEN bits of the product of the two source registers.

INSTRUCTION: C.MV
FORMAT: c.mv rd, rs2
RULE 1: C.MV copies the architectural value held in register rs2 into register rd.
RULE 2: C.MV produces the same architectural result as an add instruction with rd as the destination, x0 as the first source, and rs2 as the second source.
RULE 3: C.MV is defined only for encodings in which rs2 is not x0; encodings with rs2 equal to x0 are defined as the C.JR instruction instead of C.MV.
RULE 4: For encodings where rs2 is not x0 and rd is x0, the encoding is defined as a HINT rather than as a C.MV operation.
RULE 5: C.MV MUST be defined only when the destination register rd is x0 and the source register rs2 is not x0; encodings that do not satisfy these constraints are reserved.
RULE 6: Execution of C.MV MUST advance the program counter to the address of the following instruction.
RULE 7: Execution of C.MV MUST NOT modify any architectural integer register state, including x0.
RULE 8: Execution of C.MV MUST NOT modify any architectural memory state.
RULE 9: Implementations that ignore hint instructions MUST treat C.MV as a no-op, causing only the program-counter advancement and any optional performance-counter update.
RULE 10: When C.MV is decoded with rd equal to zero, it MUST be treated as a hint that produces no architectural effect.
RULE 11: Encodings of C.MV with rs2 equal to zero are reserved.

INSTRUCTION: C.NOP
FORMAT: c.nop
RULE 1: C.NOP must not modify any architecturally visible state other than advancing the program counter and incrementing any applicable performance counters.
RULE 2: C.NOP has the same architectural effect as the 32-bit NOP instruction.
RULE 3: C.NOP must advance the program counter to the address of the following instruction.
RULE 4: C.NOP must not modify any architectural state other than the program counter and any applicable performance counters.
RULE 5: C.NOP has no defined architectural side-effects, permissions, or exception conditions.
RULE 6: C.NOP must not alter any architectural register, memory, or program-counter state.
RULE 7: C.NOP MUST be recognized as a valid compressed instruction on RV64 implementations.

INSTRUCTION: C.NOT
FORMAT: c.not rd
RULE 1: C.NOT performs a bitwise NOT on the value in the source register and writes the result to the destination register.
RULE 2: C.NOT rd'/rs1' MUST compute the one's complement of the value currently held in rd'/rs1' and write the resulting value back to the same register.
RULE 3: If C.NOT is executed in any privilege mode, then it executes normally (does not raise an illegal‑instruction exception) and writes the bitwise NOT of the source register to the destination register, regardless of any architectural state.
RULE 4: C.NOT rd'/rs1' MUST specify a register that is one of the standard eight registers x8-x15.
RULE 5: C.NOT MUST produce an architectural result identical to that of the 32-bit instruction xori rd'/rs1', rd'/rs1', -1.

INSTRUCTION: C.NTL.ALL
FORMAT: c.ntl.all
RULE 1: Execution of C.NTL.ALL must not modify any architectural state nor alter the architecturally visible effects of its following target instruction.
RULE 2: If a trap is taken on the target instruction, implementations should ignore C.NTL.ALL rather than applying its effect to the first instruction of the trap handler.
RULE 3: If an interrupt is taken after C.NTL.ALL has completed but before its target instruction is fetched, then on return the PC is set to the target address, C.NTL.ALL is not reexecuted, and the architectural state is exactly as if C.NTL.ALL had executed normally.
RULE 4: If an instruction that does not explicitly access memory is executed while C.NTL.ALL is in effect, then the architectural state (registers, CSRs, and memory) remains unchanged and no exception is raised.
RULE 5: C.NTL.ALL must not modify any architectural state other than advancing the program counter and updating any applicable performance counters.
RULE 6: Executing C.NTL.ALL must advance the program counter to the address of the following instruction.
RULE 7: Implementations that ignore HINT instructions must execute C.NTL.ALL as a no-op.

INSTRUCTION: C.NTL.P1
FORMAT: c.ntl.p1
RULE 1: C.NTL.P1 MUST NOT change architectural state nor alter the architecturally visible effects of its target instruction.
RULE 2: If a trap is taken on an instruction that includes the C.NTL.P1 hint, then the C.NTL.P1 hint must be ignored for the first instruction of the trap handler, and that first handler instruction must produce its normal architectural result (e.g., write the expected value to its destination register) without any effect from the hint.
RULE 3: If an interrupt occurs between the execution of C.NTL.P1 and its target instruction, execution shall resume at the target instruction without reexecuting C.NTL.P1, and this does not change the program's architectural semantics.
RULE 4: Execution of C.NTL.P1 must not modify any architectural state other than advancing the program counter.
RULE 5: Execution of C.NTL.P1 must not modify any architectural state other than updating any applicable performance counters.
RULE 6: An implementation that chooses to ignore HINT instructions must execute C.NTL.P1 as a no-op, performing only the required program-counter advancement.

INSTRUCTION: C.NTL.PALL
FORMAT: c.ntl.pall
RULE 1: C.NTL.PALL must not change any architectural state and must not alter the architecturally visible effects of its target instruction.
RULE 2: If an interrupt occurs between execution of C.NTL.PALL and its target instruction, execution resumes at the target instruction without reexecuting C.NTL.PALL, and program semantics remain unchanged.
RULE 3: Execution of C.NTL.PALL does not modify any architectural state except for advancing the program counter and updating any applicable performance counters.

INSTRUCTION: C.NTL.S1
FORMAT: c.ntl.s1
RULE 1: C.NTL.S1 must not change any architectural state and must not alter the architecturally visible effects of the immediately subsequent instruction.
RULE 2: Following a C.NTL.S1 with a non-memory-access instruction may reduce performance but must not produce any architecturally visible effect.
RULE 3: If a trap is taken on the target instruction of a C.NTL.S1 hint, then the first instruction in the trap handler must execute as a normal instruction with the C.NTL.S1 hint ignored, observable by the instruction producing its usual architectural effects (e.g., the destination register contains the expected value).
RULE 4: If an interrupt is taken after a C.NTL.S1 instruction completes but before its target instruction is executed, then after the interrupt handler returns (mret) the next PC equals the C.NTL.S1 target address and all architectural registers and memory hold the same values they would have held had the interrupt not occurred.
RULE 5: C.NTL.S1 MUST advance the program counter to the address of the next instruction.
RULE 6: C.NTL.S1 MUST NOT modify any architectural state other than the program counter and performance counters.
RULE 7: If an implementation does not treat C.NTL.S1 as a no-operation, it executes the instruction as a regular computational instruction that does not mutate architectural state.

INSTRUCTION: C.SB
FORMAT: c.sb rs2, c_uimm2(rs1)
RULE 1: C.SB stores the least-significant byte of the source register to the memory address computed from the base register and the unsigned immediate, having the same architectural effect as the 32-bit store-byte instruction.
RULE 2: C.SB forms a 32-bit unsigned immediate uimm where bits 31 down to 2 are zero, bit 1 is taken from instruction bit 5, and bit 0 is taken from instruction bit 6.
RULE 3: C.SB zero-extends the immediate uimm to the XLEN width before adding it to the base address.
RULE 4: C.SB computes the effective memory address by adding the zero-extended uimm to the value held in the base register rs1'.
RULE 5: C.SB uses the compressed source register rs2' (which maps to one of the architectural registers x8-x15) as the data source for the store.
RULE 6: C.SB stores the least significant byte of the value in rs2' to the memory location whose address is the sum of the value in rs1' and the zero-extended uimm.
RULE 7: C.SB writes only bits 7 down to 0 of the addressed byte, performing a byte-store to memory.
RULE 8: C.SB selects its rs1' and rs2' operands exclusively from the standard compressed register set x8-x15.
RULE 9: C.SB imposes no architectural prerequisites and may be executed in any processor state.

INSTRUCTION: C.SD
FORMAT: c.sd rs2, c_uimm8(rs1)
RULE 1: If the RV64C extension is not present and a C.SD instruction is executed, then the processor raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 2: C.SD stores the 64-bit value held in register rs2′ to memory.
RULE 3: C.SD computes its effective address by adding to the base address in register rs1′ the zero-extended offset field multiplied by 8.
RULE 4: C.SD has the same architectural effect as the 64-bit store instruction sd with operands rs2′ and offset(rs1′).
RULE 5: C.SD is defined only for implementations that support the RV64 base ISA and is not defined for RV32 implementations.
RULE 6: In the architectural effect of C.SD, the rs1′ field supplies the base address register whose value is added to the unsigned immediate to form the effective store address.
RULE 7: In the architectural effect of C.SD, the rs2′ field supplies the double-word value that is written to memory at the effective store address.
RULE 8: In C.SD, the unsigned immediate is formed by concatenating uimm[5:3] as the low part and uimm[7:6] as the high part to produce the offset that is added to the base address.
RULE 9: C.SD must store the 64-bit value from the indicated source register into memory.
RULE 10: C.SD must compute the target memory address by adding an implicit offset of zero to the value in the indicated base register.
RULE 11: C.SD must be available only on RV32 implementations and must not be available on RV64 implementations.
RULE 12: C.SD stores a doubleword from the register pair identified by rs2' to the memory address formed by adding the sign-extended offset to the address in rs1'.
RULE 13: If a C.SD instruction is executed on a processor that implements the Zcf extension, then the processor raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 14: C.SD MUST use only registers x8-x15 for both its source and base register operands.
RULE 15: When the source register field of a C.SD instruction is x0, the 64-bit value stored to memory by the instruction must be zero.
RULE 16: C.SD computes the effective memory address by adding to the contents of base register rs1' the product of eight and the zero-extended offset field encoded in the instruction.
RULE 17: C.SD writes the 64-bit value from rs2' and rs2'+1 to the computed effective address.
RULE 18: In C.SD the offset field is zero-extended to XLEN bits prior to being multiplied by eight.

INSTRUCTION: C.SDSP
FORMAT: c.sdsp rs2, c_uimm9(sp)
RULE 1: C.SDSP stores the 64-bit value contained in register rs2 to the memory location addressed by its effective address.
RULE 2: C.SDSP computes its effective address by adding to the stack pointer register x2 the zero-extended offset field multiplied by 8.
RULE 3: C.SDSP has the same architectural effect as executing the base ISA instruction sd rs2, offset(x2).
RULE 4: C.SDSP derives its low-order immediate field from the same instruction bits designated uimm[5:3] and uimm[8:6].
RULE 5: C.SDSP stores a doubleword to memory at the address obtained by adding a sign-extended offset encoded in the instruction to the current value of the stack pointer (sp).
RULE 6: If the source register field of a C.SDSP instruction encodes x0, the instruction MUST store a 64-bit value of zero.
RULE 7: C.SDSP computes the effective memory address by adding to the stack pointer register x2 the product of eight and the zero-extended offset field encoded in the instruction.
RULE 8: C.SDSP has the same architectural effect as executing the non-compressed instruction sd rs2, offset(x2) with the same rs2 and offset values.
RULE 9: If a C.SDSP instruction is executed, then a memory write occurs to the effective address (sp + sign‑extended immediate) and the memory location contains the value from the source register.
RULE 10: C.SDSP must be subject to the pointer-masking mechanism, so its effective address is transformed according to the current privilege mode's pointer-masking settings unless the MXR setting disables masking.

INSTRUCTION: C.SEXT.B
FORMAT: c.sext.b rd
RULE 1: C.SEXT.B has a 16-bit encoding and sign-extends the low-order byte of the source register into the destination register.
RULE 2: C.SEXT.B must sign-extend the least-significant byte of the source register to XLEN bits by copying bit 7 of that byte into all more-significant bits of the destination register.
RULE 3: C.SEXT.B must use a single register operand that serves as both the source and the destination of the sign-extension operation.
RULE 4: C.SEXT.B is only defined when the Zbb extension is implemented in the processor.

INSTRUCTION: C.SEXT.H
FORMAT: c.sext.h rd
RULE 1: An implementation that includes the Zcb extension MUST support the compressed instruction C.SEXT.H.
RULE 2: C.SEXT.H must sign-extend the least-significant halfword of the source operand to XLEN bits by copying bit 15 into all more-significant bits of the destination register.
RULE 3: C.SEXT.H must treat its single operand rd′/rs1′ as both the source and the destination register.
RULE 4: C.SEXT.H must restrict rd′/rs1′ to be one of the registers x8, x9, x10, x11, x12, x13, x14, or x15.
RULE 5: C.SEXT.H is only defined when the Zbb extension is implemented.

INSTRUCTION: C.SH
FORMAT: c.sh rs2, c_uimm1(rs1)
RULE 1: C.SH stores a halfword (16 bits) from the source register rs2′ to memory.
RULE 2: The halfword written by C.SH is the low-order 16 bits of rs2′.
RULE 3: C.SH computes the effective memory address by adding the zero-extended immediate uimm to the base register rs1′.
RULE 4: If a C.SH instruction is executed on a processor that does not implement the Zcb extension, then it raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 5: In the CSH format, bit 9 of the 16-bit encoding of C.SH selects the rs1' register field.
RULE 6: In the CSH format, bit 8 of the 16-bit encoding of C.SH is reserved and has no architectural effect for C.SH.
RULE 7: In the CSH format, bit 7 of the 16-bit encoding of C.SH is reserved and has no architectural effect for C.SH.
RULE 8: In the CSH format, bits 3 and 2 of the 16-bit encoding of C.SH are reserved and have no architectural effect for C.SH.
RULE 9: C.SH stores the least-significant halfword (bits 15..0) of the source register rs2' into memory.
RULE 10: C.SH computes the effective memory address by adding the contents of the base register rs1' to the zero-extended immediate uimm.
RULE 11: C.SH forms the immediate uimm by setting bits 31:2 to zero, setting bit 1 to the instruction encoding bit 5, and setting bit 0 to zero.
RULE 12: C.SH zero-extends the immediate uimm to XLEN before adding it to the value in rs1'.
RULE 13: C.SH has no architectural prerequisites and may be executed in any privilege mode.
RULE 14: C.SH updates only the 16-bit halfword at the effective address, leaving surrounding memory bytes unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: C.SLLI
FORMAT: c.slli rd, shamt
RULE 1: C.SLLI performs a logical left shift of the value in register rd by the amount specified in the shamt field and writes the shifted result back to rd.
RULE 2: C.SLLI encodings with shamt = 0 are treated as HINTs.
RULE 3: C.SLLI encodings with rd = x0 are treated as HINTs.
RULE 4: C.SLLI does not modify any architectural state except that it advances the program counter and may update applicable performance counters.
RULE 5: If a C.SLLI instruction is ignored as a hint, then the destination register retains its pre‑instruction value, the program counter advances normally, and no exception is raised.
RULE 6: When rd is x0, C.SLLI does not modify any architectural state.
RULE 7: When imm is zero, C.SLLI does not modify any architectural state because the destination register is overwritten with a copy of its original value.
RULE 8: C.SLLI's rs1/rd field must not be zero (rs1/rd ≠ 0).
RULE 9: If a C.SLLI instruction is executed with the destination register field equal to zero or the immediate (shamt) field equal to zero, then the instruction is treated as a hint and leaves all architectural state unchanged, i.e., the destination register retains its previous value and no exception is raised.

INSTRUCTION: C.SRAI
FORMAT: c.srai rd, shamt
RULE 1: C.SRAI performs an arithmetic right shift of the value in register rd' and writes the resulting value back to register rd'.
RULE 2: When the shamt field of C.SRAI is zero, the encoding is defined as a HINT.
RULE 3: C.SRAI MUST have its immediate field equal to zero (imm = 0).
RULE 4: When executed as a HINT, C.SRAI MUST NOT modify any architectural state except for advancing the program counter and updating any applicable performance counters.
RULE 5: On implementations that ignore HINTs, C.SRAI MUST behave as a no-op, affecting no architectural state other than advancing the program counter.
RULE 6: In any implementation that treats C.SRAI as a HINT, the instruction MUST satisfy that its destination register is x0 or that the destination register is the same as its source register, ensuring no architectural state change.
RULE 7: If the uimm field of a C.SRAI instruction is non‑zero, then the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 8: C.SRAI does NOT cause any architectural state change.
RULE 9: C.SRAI does NOT perform permission checks, raise exceptions, or define any execution semantics beyond being a hint.
RULE 10: C.SRAI MUST obey the same architectural criteria and semantics as defined for the corresponding shift-right-arithmetic-immediate operation in the base integer ISA (RVI).

INSTRUCTION: C.SRLI
FORMAT: c.srli rd, shamt
RULE 1: C.SRLI logically right shifts the value in register rd' by the amount specified in the shamt field and writes the shifted result back to register rd'.
RULE 2: C.SRLI obtains its shift amount from the shamt field of the instruction encoding.
RULE 3: C.SRLI is architecturally equivalent to the 32-bit instruction srli rd', rd', shamt.
RULE 4: C.SRLI with a shamt field equal to zero is defined as a HINT instruction.
RULE 5: The immediate field of C.SRLI must be zero.
RULE 6: Execution of C.SRLI does not modify any architectural state except that it advances the program counter to the next instruction and may update any applicable performance counters.
RULE 7: C.SRLI with an unsigned immediate value equal to zero is a hint and has no defined architectural effect.

INSTRUCTION: C.SUB
FORMAT: c.sub rd, rs2
RULE 1: C.SUB MUST subtract the integer value in register rs2′ from the integer value in register rd′ and write the resulting integer value back to register rd′.
RULE 2: C.SUB MUST produce the same architectural result as executing the integer register-register subtraction instruction sub rd′, rd′, rs2′.

INSTRUCTION: C.SUBW
FORMAT: c.subw rd, rs2
