RULE 1: C.SUBW subtracts the value in register rs2′ from the value in register rd′, sign-extends the lower 32 bits of the difference, and writes the result to register rd′.
RULE 2: C.SUBW has the same architectural effect as executing the subw rd′, rd′, rs2′ instruction.
RULE 3: An RV64 implementation must treat the execution of C.SUBW as defined behavior and must not raise an illegal-instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: C.SW
FORMAT: c.sw rs2, c_uimm7(rs1)
RULE 1: C.SW stores the 32-bit value held in register rs2' to the memory location addressed by its effective address.
RULE 2: C.SW computes its effective address by zero-extending the immediate, multiplying the result by 4, and adding it to the contents of register rs1'.
RULE 3: C.SW has the same architectural effect as executing the 32-bit store instruction sw rs2', offset(rs1') with the same register values and immediate.

INSTRUCTION: C.SWSP
FORMAT: c.swsp rs2, c_uimm8(sp)
RULE 1: C.SWSP MUST store the 32-bit value from register rs2 to the memory location whose address is the effective address computed for the instruction.
RULE 2: C.SWSP MUST compute its effective address by adding to the stack pointer register x2 the zero-extended offset field multiplied by 4.
RULE 3: C.SWSP MUST be treated as the 32-bit store instruction sw rs2, offset(x2).
RULE 4: C.SWSP uses the uimm[5:2|7:6] field as the unsigned immediate offset for the store address.
RULE 5: If a C.SWSP instruction is executed, then the effective store address is the computed address after applying the current pointer mask, observable by the memory write occurring at the masked address.
RULE 6: Pointer masking MUST be applied to the address generated by C.SWSP.
RULE 7: Misaligned C.SWSP stores are supported and their behavior is identical to applying the pointer masking transformation to each constituent aligned memory access that composes the misaligned store.
RULE 8: Because C.SWSP is not an SFENCE.*, HFENCE.*, SINVAL.*, or HINVAL.* operation, pointer masking MUST be applied to the address used by C.SWSP.

INSTRUCTION: C.XOR
FORMAT: c.xor rd, rs2
RULE 1: C.XOR must compute the bitwise exclusive-OR of the architectural values in registers rd′ and rs2′ and write the resulting value to register rd′.
RULE 2: C.XOR must have the same architectural effect as executing the integer instruction xor rd′, rd′, rs2′.

INSTRUCTION: C.ZEXT.B
FORMAT: c.zext.b rd
RULE 1: C.ZEXT.B reads the least-significant byte of the source register, zero-extends it to the register width, and writes the result to the destination register.
RULE 2: If C.ZEXT.B is not encoded with exactly one register operand that is used as both source and destination, then execution raises an illegal‑instruction exception; otherwise, after execution the destination register contains the zero‑extended value of the low byte of that register.
RULE 3: C.ZEXT.B zero-extends the least-significant byte of the specified register to XLEN bits by setting all bits more significant than bit 7 to zero.
RULE 4: C.ZEXT.B has no architectural prerequisites and may be executed in any privilege mode.
RULE 5: The architectural effect of C.ZEXT.B is equivalent to executing the 32-bit instruction andi rd, rd, 0xff on the same register.
RULE 6: The C.ZEXT.B instruction performs a zero-extend of a byte.
RULE 7: Implementations that support the RV64 ISA must include the C.ZEXT.B instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.ZEXT.H
FORMAT: c.zext.h rd
RULE 1: C.ZEXT.H is a 16-bit compressed instruction that reads the low 16 bits of the register rsd', zero-extends that halfword to XLEN bits, and writes the result back to rsd'.
RULE 2: C.ZEXT.H causes no architectural side effects other than updating rsd' with the zero-extended value.
RULE 3: C.ZEXT.H does not perform any permission checks and does not raise any exceptions.
RULE 4: C.ZEXT.H MUST be available only when the Zbb extension is implemented.
RULE 5: C.ZEXT.H MUST use a register from the standard set x8-x15 as both its source and destination operand.
RULE 6: C.ZEXT.H reads the XLEN-bit value from the specified register, extracts bits 15 down to 0, and writes back a value whose bits 15-0 equal the extracted bits and whose bits XLEN-1 down to 16 are zeros.
RULE 7: C.ZEXT.H updates the same architectural register that provides the operand, performing the zero-extension in place.

INSTRUCTION: C.ZEXT.W
FORMAT: c.zext.w rd
RULE 1: C.ZEXT.W MUST produce a 64-bit result in which bits 31:0 equal bits 31:0 of the source register rsd′ and bits 63:32 are cleared to zero.
RULE 2: C.ZEXT.W performs the same architectural operation as the 32-bit zero-extend-word instruction from the application-class profile.
RULE 3: C.ZEXT.W MUST write its result back to the same architectural register rd'/rs1'.
RULE 4: C.ZEXT.W MUST set bits 0-31 of the destination register to the corresponding bits 0-31 of the source register value.
RULE 5: C.ZEXT.W MUST set bits 32 through XLEN-1 of the destination register to zero.
RULE 6: C.ZEXT.W is only defined on implementations that support the Zba extension; on implementations without Zba the encoding is reserved.
RULE 7: C.ZEXT.W MUST update the architectural register file entry corresponding to rd'/rs1' with the zero-extended 32-bit value of its prior contents.
RULE 8: C.ZEXT.W has an architectural effect equivalent to executing add.uw rd'/rs1', rd'/rs1', zero on a 32-bit implementation.

INSTRUCTION: CBO.CLEAN
FORMAT: cbo.clean (rs1)
RULE 1: CBO.CLEAN MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception if the current privilege mode is not Machine and the menvcfg.CBCFE bit is clear.
RULE 2: CBO.CLEAN MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception if the current privilege mode is User and the senvcfg.CBCFE bit is clear.
RULE 3: CBO.CLEAN's illegal-instruction and virtual-instruction exception conditions are identical to the corresponding exception conditions for CBO.FLUSH.
RULE 4: If a CBO.CLEAN instruction is encoded with a non‑zero offset operand, then it raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 5: CBO.CLEAN execution in any privilege mode less privileged than M MUST NOT raise an illegal-instruction exception when the CBCFE field in the menvcfg CSR is set to 1.
RULE 6: CBO.CLEAN execution in any privilege mode less privileged than M MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception when the CBCFE field in the menvcfg CSR is 0.
RULE 7: CBO.CLEAN execution in U-mode MUST be permitted only if the instruction is enabled for use in S-mode and the CBCFE field in senvcfg is set to 1.
RULE 8: If CBO.CLEAN is executed in U-mode when either the instruction is not enabled for S-mode use or the CBCFE field is not 1, an illegal-instruction exception MUST be raised.

INSTRUCTION: CBO.FLUSH
FORMAT: cbo.flush (rs1)
RULE 1: CBO.FLUSH MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception if ((priv_mode != M) && !menvcfg.CBCFE) or ((priv_mode == U) && !senvcfg.CBCFE).
RULE 2: CBO.FLUSH MUST execute when neither the illegal-instruction nor the virtual-instruction exception conditions are true.
RULE 3: CBO.FLUSH does NOT require rs1 to be aligned to the size of a cache block.
RULE 4: If a fault occurs during execution of CBO.FLUSH, the faulting virtual address is defined to be the value in rs1.
RULE 5: If the Zicbom extension is implemented and the CBCFE field in menvcfg is 1, then execution of CBO.FLUSH in any privilege mode less privileged than M MUST NOT raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 6: If the Zicbom extension is implemented and the CBCFE field in menvcfg is 0, then execution of CBO.FLUSH in any privilege mode less privileged than M MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 7: CBO.FLUSH execution in U-mode MUST be enabled only if the instruction is enabled for S-mode and CBCFE is set to 1; otherwise it MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 8: When the Zicbom extension is implemented, the henvcfg.CBCFE field is 1, and the CBO.FLUSH instruction is HS-qualified, the CBO.FLUSH instruction MUST be enabled for execution.
RULE 9: When the Zicbom extension is implemented, the henvcfg.CBCFE field is 0, and the CBO.FLUSH instruction is HS-qualified, the CBO.FLUSH instruction MUST raise a virtual-instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: CBO.INVAL
FORMAT: cbo.inval (rs1)
RULE 1: CBO.INVAL MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception if the current privilege mode is not M and menvcfg.CBIE = 00, or if the current privilege mode is U and senvcfg.CBIE = 00.
RULE 2: CBO.INVAL does not require the value in rs1 to be aligned to the size of a cache block.
RULE 3: If a fault occurs during execution of CBO.INVAL, the faulting virtual address is defined as the value in rs1.
RULE 4: If CBIE is set to 00b, executing CBO.INVAL in any mode less privileged than M MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 5: CBO.INVAL execution in U-mode is enabled only if the instruction is also enabled for use in S-mode and the CBIE field in senvcfg is set to 01b or 11b; otherwise an illegal-instruction exception is raised.

INSTRUCTION: CBO.ZERO
FORMAT: cbo.zero (rs1)
RULE 1: If a CBO.ZERO instruction executes with a given base address and size, then after execution every byte in the specified memory range contains 0.
RULE 2: When CBO.ZERO is performed as a single memory operation while the PMP access-control bits differ across the cache block, a store access-fault exception must be raised.
RULE 3: If CBO.ZERO access is not permitted because address translation does not allow write access, a store page-fault or store guest-page-fault exception is raised.
RULE 4: If a CBO.ZERO instruction is executed while the access is not permitted for any reason other than a disallowed write access, then a store access‑fault exception is raised (mcause = Store/AMO access‑fault).
RULE 5: When a page-fault, guest-page-fault, or access-fault exception is taken due to CBO.ZERO, the tval CSR is written with the faulting effective address supplied in rs1.
RULE 6: If the effective address of a CBO.ZERO instruction is denied by the privilege checks using the MPRV, MPV, and MPP bits in mstatus and the SUM and MXR bits in mstatus, sstatus, or vsstatus, then executing CBO.ZERO raises a load/store access‑fault exception (mcause set to the corresponding load/store access‑fault code); otherwise the cache block is zeroed as defined.
RULE 7: Implementations must process exceptions generated by CBO.ZERO as store or AMO exceptions, regardless of the specific permission checks that caused the exception.
RULE 8: CBO.ZERO MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception when the current privilege mode is not M and the menvcfg.CBZE bit is clear.
RULE 9: CBO.ZERO MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception when the current privilege mode is U and the senvcfg.CBZE bit is clear.
RULE 10: CBO.ZERO MUST execute normally when none of the illegal-instruction or virtual-instruction exception conditions are true.
RULE 11: If CBO.ZERO does not store zeros to every byte of the cache block whose effective address is in rs1, then a subsequent load from any address within that block can return a non‑zero value instead of zero.
RULE 12: If CBO.ZERO is executed on any address (cacheable or non‑cacheable) and after execution a load from any byte of the affected cache block does not return zero, then the instruction has failed to store zeros.
RULE 13: If the effective address in rs1 is used as the operand for CBO.ZERO, then after the instruction executes every byte in the cache block (and the corresponding memory locations) that contains that address reads back as zero.
RULE 14: CBO.ZERO MUST store zeros to every byte of the cache block that contains the address specified in rs1.
RULE 15: CBO.ZERO does NOT require the address in rs1 to be aligned to the size of a cache block.
RULE 16: If a fault occurs during execution of CBO.ZERO, the faulting virtual address is defined to be the value in rs1 rather than the base address of the cache block.
RULE 17: In any privilege mode less privileged than M, if the CBZE field is set to 1, CBO.ZERO must execute without raising an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 18: In any privilege mode less privileged than M, if the CBZE field is not set to 1, CBO.ZERO must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 19: CBO.ZERO execution in U-mode is permitted only when the instruction is enabled for S-mode use and the CBZE field is set to 1; otherwise an illegal-instruction exception must be raised.
RULE 20: If a CBO.ZERO instruction is not HS-qualified, it MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 21: If a CBO.ZERO instruction is HS-qualified and the CBZE field is set to 1, the instruction MUST be enabled for execution.
RULE 22: If CBO.ZERO computes its effective address without applying the required pointer mask, then a subsequent load from the correctly masked address returns the original (non‑zero) value instead of zero, showing that the cache line at the masked address was not zeroed.
RULE 23: CBO.ZERO must not perform a store when pointer masking would block the access.

INSTRUCTION: CLMUL
FORMAT: clmul rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The CLMUL instruction computes the carry-less product of the values in rs1 and rs2 and writes the low-order part of the product to rd.
RULE 2: CLMUL computes the lower XLEN bits of the 2 × XLEN carry-less product of the values held in registers rs1 and rs2.
RULE 3: CLMUL writes the lower XLEN bits of the carry-less product into the destination register rd.
RULE 4: CLMUL performs carry-less multiplication, which is defined as multiplication in the polynomial ring over GF(2).
RULE 5: CLMUL computes the carry-less product of the values in source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the low-order XLEN bits of that product into the destination register rd.
RULE 6: CLMUL MUST write the final accumulator value to the destination register rd, producing the lower XLEN bits of the carry-less product of the rs1 and rs2 values.
RULE 7: The CLMUL instruction computes a carry-less product of the two XLEN-bit operands by iterating over each bit position i from 0 to XLEN-1 and, when the i-th bit of the rs2 operand is 1, XOR-ing the rs1 operand left-shifted by i into an accumulator initialized to zero.
RULE 8: The CLMUL instruction produces as its architectural result the lower XLEN bits of the full 2·XLEN-bit carry-less product.
RULE 9: After the computation, the CLMUL instruction writes the XLEN-bit accumulator value to the destination register rd.
RULE 10: The CLMUL instruction computes the low XLEN bits of the carry-less product of the two source registers, treating each operand as a binary polynomial over GF(2).
RULE 11: An RV64 implementation must provide the CLMUL instruction.
RULE 12: An implementation that includes the Zbkb extension must implement the CLMUL instruction.
RULE 13: An implementation that includes the Zbkx extension must implement the CLMUL instruction.
RULE 14: The CLMUL operation computes a 64-bit product by initializing a 64-bit result to zero and, for each bit position j from 0 to 63 of op2, XOR-ing the result with op1 shifted left by j positions when op2[j] is 1, discarding any bits shifted beyond the 64-bit width.
RULE 15: If a CLMUL instruction raises an exception or fails to retire with RETIRE_SUCCESS, then it has not processed all active elements.
RULE 16: The 64-bit value produced by the CLMUL operation represents the low half (least-significant 64 bits) of the full 128-bit carry-less product of the two 64-bit operands.
RULE 17: CLMUL retires successfully after completing the element-wise operations without raising an architectural exception.

INSTRUCTION: CLMULH
FORMAT: clmulh rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The CLMULH instruction takes three integer registers rd, rs1, and rs2 and writes a result only to rd.
RULE 2: The CLMULH instruction computes the high XLEN bits of the carry-less product of the XLEN-bit values in rs1 and rs2 and stores that high part into rd.
RULE 3: CLMULH MUST produce the upper XLEN bits of the 2 × XLEN-bit carry-less product of the values in rs1 and rs2 and write that result to rd.
RULE 4: The CLMULH instruction computes the carry-less multiplication of the values in source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the high half of the infinite-precision product into destination register rd.
RULE 5: Execution of the CLMULH instruction updates only the destination register rd; the architectural state of rs1, rs2, and all other registers remains unchanged.
RULE 6: CLMULH must, for each integer i from 1 through XLEN inclusive, test bit i of rs2_val and, if that bit is 1, replace output with the bitwise XOR of the current output and rs1_val shifted right by (XLEN − i) bits; otherwise output remains unchanged.
RULE 7: CLMULH must write the final value of output to the architectural XLEN-bit destination register rd after the loop completes.
RULE 8: CLMULH must produce as its result the upper XLEN bits of the 2·XLEN-bit carry-less product of the original rs1 and rs2 values.
RULE 9: If the tested bit of rs2 is one, CLMULH updates the accumulator by XOR-ing it with the rs1 value right-shifted by (XLEN − i) bits.
RULE 10: After completing all XLEN iterations, CLMULH writes the final accumulator value to the destination register rd.
RULE 11: The value written by CLMULH to rd is the upper XLEN bits of the 2·XLEN-bit carry-less product of the original rs1 and rs2 values.

INSTRUCTION: CLMULR
FORMAT: clmulr rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: CLMULR rd, rs1, rs2 MUST write to rd the bits numbered (2 × XLEN-2) down to (XLEN-1) of the 2 × XLEN-bit carry-less product computed from rs1 and rs2.
RULE 2: CLMULR produces bits 2·XLEN-2 down to XLEN-1 of the 2·XLEN-bit carry-less product of its source operands.
RULE 3: CLMULR computes an XLEN-bit output by initializing the output to zero and, for each integer i from 0 to XLEN-1, XOR-ing the output with rs1 shifted right by (XLEN-i-1) bits whenever bit i of rs2 is 1.
RULE 4: CLMULR writes the resulting XLEN-bit value to the destination register rd, replacing its previous architectural contents.
RULE 5: CLMULR is architecturally equivalent to bit-reversing both rs1 and rs2, performing a carry-less multiplication (CLMUL), bit-reversing the 2·XLEN-bit product, and then extracting bits 2·XLEN-2:XLEN-1 as the final result.
RULE 6: CLMULR's mnemonic suffix "r" indicates that the instruction operates on reversed-bit inputs and yields a reversed-bit output relative to the standard CLMUL operation.

INSTRUCTION: CLZ
FORMAT: clz rd, rs1
RULE 1: The CLZ instruction counts the number of leading zero bits in the XLEN-bit value held in the source register rs and writes that count to the destination register rd.
RULE 2: The CLZ instruction reads the XLEN-bit value from source register rs, counts the number of leading zero bits in that value, and writes the resulting count to destination register rd.
RULE 3: The CLZ instruction counts the number of contiguous zero bits starting from the most-significant bit of the source register and writes that count to the destination register.
RULE 4: If the value read from the source register for a CLZ instruction is zero, the CLZ instruction writes the architectural integer register width XLEN to the destination register.
RULE 5: If the most-significant bit of the source register value for a CLZ instruction is one, the CLZ instruction writes zero to the destination register.
RULE 6: For any non-zero source register value where the most-significant one-bit is at bit position i (0 <= i <= XLEN-1), the CLZ instruction writes (XLEN-1-i) to the destination register.
RULE 7: The CLZ instruction obtains the index i of the highest set bit in the source register, computes (XLEN-1-i), and stores that result in the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: CLZW
FORMAT: clzw rd, rs1
RULE 1: When executing CLZW rd, rs, the processor ignores the upper 32 bits of the source register rs and uses only the least-significant 32 bits as the input value.
RULE 2: CLZW computes the number of leading zero bits in the 32-bit signed representation of the input value and produces a 32-bit signed count result.
RULE 3: CLZW sign-extends the 32-bit count result to the processor's XLEN before writing it to the destination register rd.
RULE 4: CLZW updates only the architectural state of the destination register rd; no other architectural state is modified by the execution of CLZW.
RULE 5: CLZW MUST be defined only for implementations that support the RV64 ISA and is not defined for RV32 implementations.
RULE 6: CLZW counts the number of consecutive zero bits starting from the most-significant bit of the low-order 32-bit word taken from the source register rs.
RULE 7: CLZW writes the resulting count, a value between 0 and 32 inclusive, into the destination register rd and zero-extends the result to the full XLEN width.
RULE 8: CLZW rd, rs sets rd to the number of leading zero bits in the 32-bit value contained in rs, counting from bit 31 down to bit 0.
RULE 9: CLZW rd, rs computes the result as 31 minus the index of the most-significant bit that is set to 1 in rs.
RULE 10: CLZW rd, rs writes a result in the range 0 to 31 inclusive to rd when rs contains at least one bit set to 1.
RULE 11: CLZW rd, rs writes the value 32 to rd when the value in rs is zero.
RULE 12: CLZW rd, rs writes the value 0 to rd when bit 31 of rs is 1.
RULE 13: CLZW rd, rs does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: CM.JALT
FORMAT: cm.jalt index
RULE 1: Execution of CM.JALT causes a table-based jump to the target address obtained from the jvt CSR and writes the address of the following instruction to the link register (x1) as the return address.
RULE 2: If the address of the table used by a CM.JALT instruction is not aligned to a 64‑byte boundary, then executing that CM.JALT raises an instruction‑address‑misaligned exception (mcause = 0).
RULE 3: The entries in the table accessed by CM.JALT follow the current data endianness rather than the little-endian format used for normal instruction fetch.
RULE 4: If a CM.JALT instruction is executed, then the processor uses the instruction’s 8‑bit index to select entry i of the 256‑entry jump table and sets the program counter to the XLEN‑wide address stored in that entry.
RULE 5: Execution of a CM.JALT instruction performs a table jump.
RULE 6: Both implicit reads performed by a CM.JALT instruction require execute permission and do not consider read permission.
RULE 7: If an exception occurs on either instruction fetch of a CM.JALT instruction, xEPC is set to the PC of that CM.JALT instruction.
RULE 8: If an exception occurs on either instruction fetch of a CM.JALT instruction, xCAUSE is set to the cause appropriate for the fault type.
RULE 9: If an exception occurs on either instruction fetch of a CM.JALT instruction and xTVAL is not zero, xTVAL contains the address of the fetch that caused the fault.
RULE 10: If the index field of the encoding is less than 32, the instruction must decode as CM.JT and therefore must not decode as CM.JALT.
RULE 11: If the index field of the encoding is 32 or greater, the instruction must decode as CM.JALT.
RULE 12: CM.JALT must be decoded only when the index field of the instruction is greater than or equal to 32; if the index field is less than 32 the instruction must decode as CM.JT.
RULE 13: When XLEN is 64, CM.JALT must compute the table address as jvt.base plus the index field shifted left by 3 bits.
RULE 14: CM.JALT must read an XLEN-bit entry from memory at the computed table address.
RULE 15: CM.JALT must write pc+2 into register ra, thereby linking to the return address.
RULE 16: CM.JALT must set the program counter to the fetched XLEN-bit entry with its least-significant bit cleared, causing a jump to that aligned address.
RULE 17: When all transfer-type filter bits in the CTR control register are unimplemented or cleared, the default CTR configuration records a CM.JALT instruction as a control transfer when it executes in an enabled privileged mode.

INSTRUCTION: CM.JT
FORMAT: cm.jt index
RULE 1: CM.JT obtains a jump target address by reading an entry from a 256-entry table that resides in instruction memory, where each entry is XLEN bits wide.
RULE 2: When CM.JT reads a table entry, the entry is interpreted according to the processor's current data endianness rather than the little-endian format used for normal instruction fetch.
RULE 3: The index encoded in a CM.JT instruction selects one of the 256 table entries, and the resulting jump target may be any address representable in the XLEN-wide address space.
RULE 4: When an exception occurs on either instruction fetch of a CM.JT instruction, xEPC is set to the address of the CM.JT instruction.
RULE 5: When an exception occurs on either instruction fetch of a CM.JT instruction, xCAUSE is set to the code corresponding to the fault type.
RULE 6: When an exception occurs on either instruction fetch of a CM.JT instruction and xTVAL is non-zero, xTVAL contains the faulting fetch address.
RULE 7: When the jvt.mode field equals 0, CM.JT reads an entry from the jump vector table and jumps to the address obtained from that entry.
RULE 8: If the CM.JT instruction is fetched and executed, then no illegal‑instruction exception is raised and execution proceeds normally in any privilege mode.
RULE 9: If XLEN = 64 and a CM.JT instruction is executed, then the next PC must equal jvt.base + (index << 3); if the next PC differs from that value, the implementation violates the rule.
RULE 10: CM.JT fetches XLEN bits from memory at the computed table address, clears the least-significant bit of the fetched value, and loads the resulting address into the program counter.

INSTRUCTION: CM.MVA01S
FORMAT: cm.mva01s r1s, r2s
RULE 1: If a CM.MVA01S instruction is executed on an implementation with XLEN < 64, then it raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 2: CM.MVA01S updates the architectural register a0 (x10) with the value contained in the source s-register identified as r1s'.
RULE 3: CM.MVA01S updates the architectural register a1 (x11) with the value contained in the source s-register identified as r2s'.
RULE 4: If CM.MVA01S does not update a0 and a1 atomically, then after the instruction either a0 or a1 will retain its pre‑instruction value while the other contains the new value.

INSTRUCTION: CM.MVSA01
FORMAT: cm.mvsa01 r1s, r2s
RULE 1: The two destination registers encoded by CM.MVSA01 must be distinct registers selected from the set s0-s7.
RULE 2: Execution of CM.MVSA01 copies the architectural value of register a0 into the first destination s register and copies the architectural value of register a1 into the second destination s register, leaving all other architectural state unchanged.
RULE 3: CM.MVSA01 moves the architectural register a0 into the destination register identified by the first s-register operand and moves the architectural register a1 into the destination register identified by the second s-register operand.
RULE 4: For a CM.MVSA01 encoding to be legal, the two s-register specifiers MUST be different; r1s' and r2s' MUST not be equal.
RULE 5: If a CM.MVSA01 instruction executes, then the three‑bit value written to xreg1 is the concatenation of (r1sc[2:1] > 0) as the most‑significant bit, (r1sc[2:1] == 0) as the middle bit, and r1sc[2:0] as the least‑significant bit; and the three‑bit value written to xreg2 is the concatenation of (r2sc[2:1] > 0) as the most‑significant bit, (r2sc[2:1] == 0) as the middle bit, and r2sc[2:0] as the least‑significant bit.

INSTRUCTION: CM.POP
FORMAT: cm.pop {reg_list}, stack_adj
RULE 1: The reg_list operand of CM.POP MUST contain between one and thirteen registers selected from ra and the s0-s12 registers.
RULE 2: Valid reg_list values for CM.POP are exactly the sets {ra}, {ra, s0}, {ra, s0-s1}, {ra, s0-s2}, {ra, s0-s3}, {ra, s0-s4}, {ra, s0-s5}, {ra, s0-s6}, {ra, s0-s7}, {ra, s0-s8}, {ra, s0-s9}, and {ra, s0-s11}.
RULE 3: The reg_list value {ra, s0-s10} is NOT valid for CM.POP.
RULE 4: If the Zca extension is not implemented and a CM.POP instruction is executed, then the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 5: If the processor implements both the C and D extensions (Zcd is present) and a CM.POP instruction is executed, then an illegal‑instruction exception is raised (mcause set to the illegal‑instruction code).
RULE 6: When a CM.POP instruction executes, it must read a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 12 to the current value of the stack pointer register sp and write that word into the destination register named in the instruction.
RULE 7: After the load performed by a CM.POP instruction, the stack pointer register sp must be incremented by the immediate value 16.
RULE 8: CM.POP {ra, s0-s3},48 MUST load the 32-bit value from the memory address formed by adding 44 to the current stack pointer into register s3.
RULE 9: CM.POP {ra, s0-s3},48 MUST load the 32-bit value from the memory address formed by adding 40 to the current stack pointer into register s2.
RULE 10: CM.POP {ra, s0-s3},48 MUST load the 32-bit value from the memory address formed by adding 36 to the current stack pointer into register s1.
RULE 11: CM.POP {ra, s0-s3},48 MUST load the 32-bit value from the memory address formed by adding 32 to the current stack pointer into register s0.
RULE 12: CM.POP {ra, s0-s3},48 MUST load the 32-bit value from the memory address formed by adding 28 to the current stack pointer into register ra.
RULE 13: CM.POP {ra, s0-s3},48 MUST increment the stack pointer by 48 after all loads have been performed.
RULE 14: If CM.POP updates the stack pointer before completing all five loads or loads the registers in any order other than s3, s2, s1, s0, then ra, then after execution at least one of s0‑s3 or ra will contain a value that does not match the word stored at its original stack address (i.e., the destination register readback is incorrect).
RULE 15: CM.POP {ra, s0-s4}, 64 must have the encoding fields rlist equal to 9 and spimm equal to 2.
RULE 16: CM.POP {ra, s0-s4}, 64 must load the 32-bit value from memory address sp+60 into register s4.
RULE 17: CM.POP {ra, s0-s4}, 64 must load the 32-bit value from memory address sp+56 into register s3.
RULE 18: CM.POP {ra, s0-s4}, 64 must load the 32-bit value from memory address sp+52 into register s2.
RULE 19: CM.POP {ra, s0-s4}, 64 must load the 32-bit value from memory address sp+48 into register s1.
RULE 20: CM.POP {ra, s0-s4}, 64 must load the 32-bit value from memory address sp+44 into register s0.
RULE 21: CM.POP {ra, s0-s4}, 64 must add the immediate 64 to register sp, updating sp to sp+64.
RULE 22: CM.POP {ra, s0-s4}, 64 must not modify any architectural state other than registers s4, s3, s2, s1, s0, ra and sp.
RULE 23: CM.POP loads each register specified in the xreg_list from memory locations starting at address sp + stack_adj − bytes, where bytes is 4 when XLEN = 32 and 8 when XLEN = 64, and after loading a register the address is decremented by bytes.
RULE 24: CM.POP updates the stack pointer by adding stack_adj to sp atomically only if the load phase completes without any exception or interrupt.
RULE 25: For RV64, CM.POP determines stack_adj_base as 16 bytes when rlist ∈ [4, 5], 32 bytes when rlist ∈ [6, 7], 48 bytes when rlist ∈ [8, 9], 64 bytes when rlist ∈ [10, 11], 80 bytes when rlist ∈ [12, 13], 96 bytes when rlist = 14, and 112 bytes when rlist = 15.
RULE 26: For RV64, the allowed stack_adj values for CM.POP are 16, 32, 48, or 64 bytes when rlist ∈ [4, 5]; 32, 48, 64, or 80 bytes when rlist ∈ [6, 7]; 48, 64, 80, or 96 bytes when rlist ∈ [8, 9]; 64, 80, 96, or 112 bytes when rlist ∈ [10, 11]; 80, 96, 112, or 128 bytes when rlist ∈ [12, 13]; 96, 112, 128, or 144 bytes when rlist = 14; and 112, 128, 144, or 160 bytes when rlist = 15.
RULE 27: For RV32I and RV64, a rlist value of 4 encodes the register list containing x1 (ra); 5 encodes x1 and x8 (ra, s0); 6 encodes x1, x8, x9 (ra, s0-s1); 7 encodes x1, x8-x9, x18 (ra, s0-s2); 8 encodes x1, x8-x9, x18-x19 (ra, s0-s3); 9 encodes x1, x8-x9, x18-x20 (ra, s0-s4); 10 encodes x1, x8-x9, x18-x21 (ra, s0-s5); 11 encodes x1, x8-x9, x18-x22 (ra, s0-s6); 12 encodes x1, x8-x9, x18-x23 (ra, s0-s7); 13 encodes x1, x8-x9, x18-x24 (ra, s0-s8); 14 encodes x1, x8-x9, x18-x25 (ra, s0-s9); and 15 encodes x1, x8-x9, x18-x27 (ra, s0-s11).
RULE 28: The stack_adj operand of CM.POP is calculated as stack_adj_base plus spimm multiplied by 16, where spimm is the number of additional 16-byte increments allocated for the stack frame.

INSTRUCTION: CM.POPRET
FORMAT: cm.popret {reg_list}, stack_adj
RULE 1: The reg_list operand of CM.POPRET must contain the register ra and may include zero or more consecutive registers from s0 up to s12, with the total number of registers ranging from one to thirteen.
RULE 2: The specific register list {ra, s0-s10} is not a valid reg_list operand for CM.POPRET.
RULE 3: Executing CM.POPRET must load ra from the memory address sp+92, must load s0 from sp+88, must load s1 from sp+84, must load s2 from sp+80, must load s3 from sp+76, must load s4 from sp+72, must load s5 from sp+68, must load s6 from sp+64, must load s7 from sp+60, must load s8 from sp+56, must load s9 from sp+52, must load s10 from sp+48, must load s11 from sp+44, must then add the immediate 96 to the sp register, and must finally transfer control to the address contained in ra (return).
RULE 4: The architectural effect of the 16-instruction sequence that loads ra and s0-s11 from the stack, increments sp by 96, and executes a ret instruction is defined to be identical to a single execution of CM.POPRET.
RULE 5: CM.POPRET includes a stack-pointer adjustment that forms part of its architectural effect.
RULE 6: If the load operations generated by CM.POPRET are not re‑executed after a trap handler completes, then the registers that CM.POPRET should restore retain their pre‑return values instead of being overwritten with the saved values.
RULE 7: If a CM.POPRET instruction updates any destination register before the trap is taken, then the architectural state at trap entry shows those registers already containing the updated values.
RULE 8: If a CM.POPRET instruction is executed, then the architectural state (register file, program counter, CSR values, and memory) after execution must be identical to the state produced by executing the same sequence of instructions that CM.POP expands to.
RULE 9: In RV32I and RV64 implementations, a CM.POPRET encoding with rlist = 4 selects ra; rlist = 5 selects ra and s0; rlist = 6 selects ra and s0-s1; rlist = 7 selects ra and s0-s2; rlist = 8 selects ra and s0-s3; rlist = 9 selects ra and s0-s4; rlist = 10 selects ra and s0-s5; rlist = 11 selects ra and s0-s6; rlist = 12 selects ra and s0-s7; rlist = 13 selects ra and s0-s8; rlist = 14 selects ra and s0-s9; rlist = 15 selects ra and s0-s11; any other rlist value is reserved.
RULE 10: For RV64, CM.POPRET uses stack_adj_base = 16 bytes for rlist = 4-5, 32 bytes for rlist = 6-7, 48 bytes for rlist = 8-9, 64 bytes for rlist = 10-11, 80 bytes for rlist = 12-13, 96 bytes for rlist = 14, and 112 bytes for rlist = 15; the legal stack_adj values are the four values obtained by adding a multiple of 16 bytes (0, 16, 32, 48) to the respective base.
RULE 11: The effective stack adjustment used by CM.POPRET is computed as stack_adj = stack_adj_base + spimm × 16, where spimm is an unsigned field encoded in the instruction.
RULE 12: CM.POPRET determines the data size of each load as 4 bytes when XLEN = 32 and as 8 bytes when XLEN = 64.
RULE 13: CM.POPRET computes the initial memory address for the first load as addr = sp + stack_adj − bytes, where bytes is the load size defined by XLEN.
RULE 14: CM.POPRET iterates over the registers with indices 27, 26, 25, 24, 23, 22, 21, 20, 19, 18, 9, 8, and 1 in that order, and for each register that appears in the xreg_list selected by the rlist field, it loads the value from address 0(addr) into the register (using lw for 4-byte loads or ld for 8-byte loads) and then decrements addr by bytes.
RULE 15: Upon successful completion of all loads, CM.POPRET atomically adds stack_adj to the stack pointer (sp += stack_adj).
RULE 16: After updating sp, CM.POPRET executes a ret operation, which jumps to the address contained in ra.
RULE 17: The final return section of CM.POPRET (the sp update and ret) executes atomically and only if the preceding load section terminated without any exceptions or interrupts.
RULE 18: If CM.POPRET(Z) is executed while the processor is in an enabled privileged mode and the default CTR configuration has all transfer‑type‑filter bits cleared, then the architectural record of the instruction marks it as a function‑return transfer (e.g., the transfer‑type field in the relevant CSR is set to function‑return).

INSTRUCTION: CM.POPRETZ
FORMAT: cm.popretz {reg_list}, stack_adj
RULE 1: CM.POPRETZ reg_list operand must contain between one and thirteen registers selected from ra and s0-s12 inclusive.
RULE 2: If the reg_list operand of CM.POPRETZ is not exactly one of the allowed sets {ra}, {ra, s0}, {ra, s0‑s1}, {ra, s0‑s2}, {ra, s0‑s3}, {ra, s0‑s4}, {ra, s0‑s5}, {ra, s0‑s6}, {ra, s0‑s7}, {ra, s0‑s8}, {ra, s0‑s9}, or {ra, s0‑s11}, then the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 3: CM.POPRETZ reg_list operand value {ra, s0-s10} is not a valid encoding.
RULE 4: CM.POPRETZ MUST load each register specified in its register list from the current stack frame.
RULE 5: CM.POPRETZ MUST write the value zero into register a0 as the function return value.
RULE 6: CM.POPRETZ MUST adjust the stack pointer to destroy the current stack frame.
RULE 7: CM.POPRETZ MUST execute a ret operation to return from the function.
RULE 8: When CM.POPRETZ is executed with a register list that contains ra and an immediate value I, it loads ra from the memory address computed as sp + (I-4).
RULE 9: When CM.POPRETZ is executed with a register list that contains s0-s11 and an immediate value I, it loads s0 from sp + (I-8), s1 from sp + (I-12), …, and s11 from sp + (I-48).
RULE 10: CM.POPRETZ writes the constant 0 into register a0.
RULE 11: CM.POPRETZ increments the stack pointer by the immediate value I, thereby discarding the stack frame that held the saved registers.
RULE 12: After performing the loads and updating sp, CM.POPRETZ transfers control to the address now contained in ra (i.e., it executes a return).
RULE 13: The architectural actions of CM.POPRETZ occur in the order: load the registers in the list, write 0 to a0, adjust sp, then return.
RULE 14: If CM.POPRETZ completes the loads but does not adjust the stack pointer, then the architectural register x2 (sp) will not be incremented by the total size of the popped stack frame.
RULE 15: If a trap is taken while executing CM.POPRETZ, then any load that completed before the trap will be re‑executed after the trap handler returns, and the destination register will contain the value from this re‑execution, overwriting any value written before the trap.
RULE 16: CM.POPRETZ MUST commit the optional `li a0, 0`, the stack pointer adjustment, and the optional `ret` only when it is certain that the entire CM.POPRETZ instruction will commit.
RULE 17: For CM.POPRETZ, once the stack pointer adjustment has been committed, any included `ret` instruction MUST execute.
RULE 18: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 4 on RV32I or RV64 loads register ra (x1) from the stack.
RULE 19: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 5 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1) and s0 (x8) from the stack.
RULE 20: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 6 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1), s0 (x8), and s1 (x9) from the stack.
RULE 21: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 7 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1), s0 (x8), s1 (x9), and s2 (x18) from the stack.
RULE 22: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 8 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1), s0-s3 (x8-x9, x18-x19) from the stack.
RULE 23: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 9 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1), s0-s4 (x8-x9, x18-x20) from the stack.
RULE 24: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 10 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1), s0-s5 (x8-x9, x18-x21) from the stack.
RULE 25: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 11 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1), s0-s6 (x8-x9, x18-x22) from the stack.
RULE 26: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 12 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1), s0-s7 (x8-x9, x18-x23) from the stack.
RULE 27: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 13 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1), s0-s8 (x8-x9, x18-x24) from the stack.
RULE 28: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 14 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1), s0-s9 (x8-x9, x18-x25) from the stack.
RULE 29: CM.POPRETZ with rlist = 15 on RV32I or RV64 loads registers ra (x1), s0-s11 (x8-x9, x18-x27) from the stack.
RULE 30: CM.POPRETZ on RV64 with rlist = 4‥5 uses a stack_adj_base of 16 bytes; with rlist = 6‥7 uses 32 bytes; with rlist = 8‥9 uses 48 bytes; with rlist = 10‥11 uses 64 bytes; with rlist = 12‥13 uses 80 bytes; with rlist = 14 uses 96 bytes; and with rlist = 15 uses 112 bytes.
RULE 31: CM.POPRETZ on RV64 permits a total stack_adj of 16, 32, 48, or 64 bytes when rlist = 4‥5; 32, 48, 64, or 80 bytes when rlist = 6‥7; 48, 64, 80, or 96 bytes when rlist = 8‥9; 64, 80, 96, or 112 bytes when rlist = 10‥11; 80, 96, 112, or 128 bytes when rlist = 12‥13; 96, 112, 128, or 144 bytes when rlist = 14; and 112, 128, 144, or 160 bytes when rlist = 15.
RULE 32: CM.POPRETZ computes the total stack adjustment as stack_adj = stack_adj_base + spimm × 16, where spimm is the number of additional 16-byte increments allocated for the stack frame.
RULE 33: CM.POPRETZ determines the byte size of each register load as 4 bytes when XLEN = 32 and 8 bytes when XLEN = 64.
RULE 34: CM.POPRETZ calculates the initial load address as sp + stack_adj − bytes and then loads each register in the xreg_list in descending register order, decrementing the address by bytes after each successful load.
RULE 35: CM.POPRETZ sets integer register a0 to zero after all specified registers have been successfully loaded from the stack.
RULE 36: CM.POPRETZ increments the stack pointer sp by the total stack_adj after all specified registers have been successfully loaded.
RULE 37: CM.POPRETZ performs a return to the address contained in ra after loading the registers and updating sp and a0.
RULE 38: CM.POPRETZ executes the final phase that writes a0, updates sp, and returns atomically and only if the register-load phase completes without any exception or interrupt.

INSTRUCTION: CM.PUSH
FORMAT: cm.push {reg_list}, -stack_adj
RULE 1: If CM.PUSH is executed, then the stack pointer (sp) is decremented by the size of the pushed data and the memory at the new sp address contains the value from the source register.
RULE 2: If after executing CM.PUSH the two destination registers do not contain the original values of the two source registers, then the double‑move operation was not performed correctly.
RULE 3: If the register‑list operand of CM.PUSH contains fewer than one or more than thirteen registers, or includes any register not among ra or s0‑s12, then executing the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 4: Valid register-list values for CM.PUSH are exactly the sets `{ra}`, `{ra, s0}`, `{ra, s0-s1}`, `{ra, s0-s2}`, `{ra, s0-s3}`, `{ra, s0-s4}`, `{ra, s0-s5}`, `{ra, s0-s6}`, `{ra, s0-s7}`, `{ra, s0-s8}`, `{ra, s0-s9}`, and `{ra, s0-s11}`.
RULE 5: If a CM.PUSH instruction is encoded with the register‑list {ra, s0‑s10}, then the processor raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 6: If the Zca extension is not implemented, executing a CM.PUSH instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 7: If CM.PUSH does not write the required bytes to every address from sp‑1 down to sp‑28, then reading those memory locations after the instruction returns values that differ from the expected stored bytes.
RULE 8: CM.PUSH MUST perform its stack-pointer adjustment only after it is certain that the entire CM.PUSH instruction will commit.
RULE 9: The final stack-pointer update performed by CM.PUSH executes only once and only after all stores have completed without any precise faults, and therefore becomes visible to interrupt or exception handlers only after CM.PUSH has completed.
RULE 10: CM.PUSH {ra, s0-s2}, -64 must store the value of register s2 to the memory address obtained by adding –4 to the current value of sp.
RULE 11: If CM.PUSH {ra, s0‑s2}, ‑64 does not store the pre‑instruction value of register s1 at the address (sp − 8), then a read of memory at (sp − 8) will not return the original s1 value.
RULE 12: CM.PUSH {ra, s0-s2}, -64 must store the value of register s0 to the memory address obtained by adding –12 to the current value of sp.
RULE 13: CM.PUSH {ra, s0-s2}, -64 must store the value of register ra to the memory address obtained by adding –16 to the current value of sp.
RULE 14: CM.PUSH {ra, s0-s2}, -64 must update sp by adding –64 to its original value, resulting in sp = sp – 64 after the stores.
RULE 15: CM.PUSH MUST store each register in its register list to memory using a 32-bit store-word at an address equal to the original value of sp minus 4 bytes for the first register, minus 8 bytes for the second register, and so on, decreasing by 4 bytes for each subsequent register.
RULE 16: CM.PUSH MUST perform the stores in order of decreasing register number within the list, with the register having the highest number stored at sp-4 and the register with the lowest number stored at the most negative offset.
RULE 17: CM.PUSH MUST update the stack pointer register (sp) by adding the signed immediate operand (spimm) to its original value after all stores have been completed.
RULE 18: CM.PUSH with the operands {ra, s0-s11} and an immediate of -112 MUST be architecturally equivalent to the sequence of store-word instructions that store s11 at sp-4, s10 at sp-8, s9 at sp-12, s8 at sp-16, s7 at sp-20, s6 at sp-24, s5 at sp-28, s4 at sp-32, s3 at sp-36, s2 at sp-40, s1 at sp-44, s0 at sp-48, ra at sp-52, followed by an add-immediate that sets sp = sp + (-112).
RULE 19: CM.PUSH stores the registers specified by the reg_list field to consecutive memory locations below the current stack pointer, using a word store when XLEN=32 and a doubleword store when XLEN=64.
RULE 20: CM.PUSH decrements the stack pointer by the total stack adjustment value stack_adj only after all specified registers have been successfully stored without any exception or interrupt.
RULE 21: The total stack adjustment value used by CM.PUSH is computed as stack_adj = stack_adj_base + spimm * 16, where spimm is the immediate field specifying additional 16-byte increments.
RULE 22: For CM.PUSH, stack_adj_base is the smallest multiple of 16 bytes that can contain the storage required for all registers in the reg_list.
RULE 23: In RV64 implementations of CM.PUSH, stack_adj_base values are assigned as follows: 16 bytes for rlist 4-5, 32 bytes for rlist 6-7, 48 bytes for rlist 8-9, 64 bytes for rlist 10-11, 80 bytes for rlist 12-13, 96 bytes for rlist 14, and 112 bytes for rlist 15.
RULE 24: In RV64, when rlist is 4 or 5, CM.PUSH permits stack_adj values of 16, 32, 48, or 64 bytes.
RULE 25: If rlist is 6 or 7 and a CM.PUSH instruction specifies a stack_adj value other than 32, 48, 64, or 80 bytes, then the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause is set to the illegal‑instruction code).
RULE 26: In RV64, when rlist is 8 or 9, CM.PUSH permits stack_adj values of 48, 64, 80, or 96 bytes.
RULE 27: In RV64, when rlist is 10 or 11, CM.PUSH permits stack_adj values of 64, 80, 96, or 112 bytes.
RULE 28: In RV64, when rlist is 14, CM.PUSH permits stack_adj values of 96, 112, 128, or 144 bytes.
RULE 29: If a CM.PUSH instruction on RV64 has rlist = 15 and the stack_adj field is not 112, 128, 144, or 160 bytes, then the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 30: The pseudocode for CM.PUSH determines the byte size per store as 4 bytes if XLEN equals 32 and 8 bytes if XLEN equals 64, and uses this size for each store operation.
RULE 31: If CM.PUSH fails to decrement the address by the register byte size after storing a register, then the next register’s value will be written to the same address as the previous one, and reading memory at the expected decremented address will not contain that register’s value.

INSTRUCTION: CPOP
FORMAT: cpop rd, rs1
RULE 1: The CPOP instruction counts the number of bits set to 1 in the source register rs and writes the resulting count to the destination register rd.
RULE 2: The CPOP instruction is defined for RV32 implementations, so the instruction is present when XLEN = 32.
RULE 3: CPOP rd, rs counts the number of set bits in the full architectural value of source register rs and writes the resulting count to destination register rd.
RULE 4: In RV64 implementations, the CPOP instruction is defined and must perform the population-count operation as described in RULE 1.
RULE 5: The result written by CPOP to rd is guaranteed to be an integer between 0 and XLEN inclusive.
RULE 6: CPOP rd, rs counts the number of bits set to 1 in the XLEN-bit value held in integer register rs and writes that count to integer register rd.
RULE 7: CPOP examines each bit position i from 0 through XLEN-1 of the source register rs, adding one to an internal counter for every bit that equals 1.
RULE 8: After CPOP executes, the architectural register rd contains a non-negative integer equal to the population count of rs, with a maximum possible value of XLEN.
RULE 9: CPOP does not modify any architectural registers other than the destination rd; the source register rs retains its original value.

INSTRUCTION: CPOPW
FORMAT: cpopw rd, rs1
RULE 1: CPOPW must ignore the upper 32 bits of the source register and operate only on bits 31-0 of that register.
RULE 2: CPOPW must produce a 32-bit signed count of the set bits and sign-extend that 32-bit result to the processor's XLEN before writing it to the destination register.
RULE 3: CPOPW rd, rs MUST count the number of set bits (1-bits) in the low 32 bits of source register rs.
RULE 4: CPOPW rd, rs MUST write the resulting count into the low 32 bits of destination register rd and MUST set the high 32 bits of rd to zero.
RULE 5: CPOPW rd, rs MUST not alter any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 6: The CPOPW instruction computes the population count of the lower 32 bits of its source register and writes the zero-extended 32-bit result into the destination register.
RULE 7: CPOPW counts the number of bits that are 1 in the least-significant 32-bit word of the source integer register rs.
RULE 8: CPOPW produces a result that is the integer count of those set bits, which is always in the range 0 to 32 inclusive.
RULE 9: CPOPW writes the count value to the destination integer register rd, placing the count in the low-order bits of rd and zero-extending it to the full XLEN width.
RULE 10: CPOPW does not alter any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 11: CPOPW executes without raising an exception for any valid source register value.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRC
FORMAT: csrrc rd, csr, rs1
RULE 1: CSRRC MUST read the current value of the addressed CSR, zero-extend it to XLEN bits, and write the resulting value to integer register rd.
RULE 2: CSRRC MUST treat the value in integer register rs1 as a bit mask and clear each CSR bit that corresponds to a high bit in rs1, provided that the CSR bit is writable.
RULE 3: CSRRC MUST write the CSR with the value obtained after applying the mask-clearing operation, thereby committing any changes to underlying bits that were read.
RULE 4: If rs1 equals x0, CSRRC MUST NOT write to the CSR, MUST NOT cause any side effects that would occur on a CSR write, and MUST NOT raise an illegal-instruction exception when the CSR is read-only.
RULE 5: CSRRC MUST always read the addressed CSR and MUST cause any read side effects regardless of the values of rs1 and rd.
RULE 6: If rs1 is not x0 and the value held in rs1 is zero, CSRRC MUST NOT trigger any per-field side effects but MUST still trigger side effects that result from writing the entire CSR.
RULE 7: CSRRC MUST write to the CSR if rs1 is not x0, and MUST NOT write to the CSR if rs1 is x0.
RULE 8: CSRRC executed with the source register x0 reads the addressed CSR, writes the original CSR value to the destination register, and leaves the CSR unchanged, providing a legal read-only way to read counter CSRs.
RULE 9: When XLEN ≥ 64, CSRRC may access and modify the full 64-bit width of a CSR in a single instruction.
RULE 10: CSRRC reads the CSR identified by its csr field as a source operand, independent of the destination register rd.
RULE 11: CSRRC writes a new value to the CSR identified by its csr field, making the csr field a destination register, unless the rs1 register is x0.
RULE 12: If the rs1 register of a CSRRC instruction is x0, the CSR identified by the csr field is not written.
RULE 13: An execution of CSRRC that targets the seed CSR with rs1 = x0 must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 14: When CSRRC accesses the seed CSR, the bits supplied in rs1 (or uimm) are ignored and must not modify the stored value of the seed CSR.
RULE 15: Executing CSRRC on the seed CSR with rs1 ≠ x0 is permitted and must not raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 16: If CSRRC attempts to read or write the seed CSR while the current privilege mode is not M and the implementation has not explicitly granted access, an illegal-instruction exception must be raised.
RULE 17: In a CSRRC instruction that accesses the mip CSR, only the software-writable SEIP bit participates in the read-modify-write sequence.
RULE 18: When a CSRRC instruction reads the mip CSR, the value placed in the destination register bit 9 is the logical OR of the software-writable SEIP bit (B) and the external interrupt controller signal (E).
RULE 19: During a CSRRC instruction on mip, the external interrupt controller signal (E) must not be used to compute the value written back to the software-writable SEIP bit; only the original software-writable value (B) is used.
RULE 20: After the read phase of a CSRRC instruction on mip, the software-writable SEIP bit is updated to B AND NOT rs1[9], where rs1[9] is bit 9 of the source register.
RULE 21: A CSRRC instruction on mip must not modify the read-only hardware-driven portion of the SEIP field; that portion remains unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRCI
FORMAT: csrrci rd, csr, zimm5
RULE 1: CSRRCI updates a CSR by applying an XLEN-bit value obtained by zero-extending the 5-bit unsigned immediate field (uimm[4:0]) encoded in the rs1 position, instead of using a value from an integer register.
RULE 2: CSRRCI always reads the targeted CSR and triggers any read-side effects regardless of the values in the rd and rs1 fields.
RULE 3: If the uimm[4:0] field of a CSRRCI instruction is zero, the instruction must not write to the CSR, must not cause any write-side effects, and must not raise an illegal-instruction exception when the CSR is read-only.
RULE 4: If the uimm[4:0] field of a CSRRCI instruction is non-zero, the instruction must write the CSR and therefore may cause the write-side effects associated with CSR writes.
RULE 5: CSRRCI only triggers side effects for those CSR fields whose corresponding bit positions are set to one in the zero-extended immediate value.
RULE 6: Executing CSRRCI provides a legal method to read a CSR value.
RULE 7: CSRRCI MUST treat its CSR operand as a source register.
RULE 8: CSRRCI MUST treat its CSR operand as a destination register when the immediate field uimm[4:0] is not zero.
RULE 9: CSRRCI MUST NOT treat its CSR operand as a destination register when the immediate field uimm[4:0] is zero.
RULE 10: If CSRRCI is executed with uimm = 0 and the target CSR is the seed CSR, then an illegal-instruction exception must be raised.
RULE 11: When CSRRCI is executed, the implementation must ignore the write value encoded in its uimm field.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRS
FORMAT: csrrs rd, csr, rs1
RULE 1: CSRRS atomically reads the addressed CSR, zero-extends the read value to XLEN bits, and writes the zero-extended value to integer register rd.
RULE 2: In a CSRRS execution, each bit that is high in the value held in integer register rs1 acts as a mask that sets the corresponding writable bit in the CSR.
RULE 3: When rs1 is x0, CSRRS does not write to the CSR, does not cause any side effects that would result from a CSR write, and does not raise an illegal-instruction exception on a read-only CSR.
RULE 4: CSRRS always reads the addressed CSR and always triggers any read side effects of that CSR, regardless of the values in rs1 or rd.
RULE 5: If rs1 specifies a register other than x0 and the register contains zero, CSRRS does not trigger per-field side effects but still triggers any side effects that result from writing the whole CSR.
RULE 6: CSRRS only triggers side effects for CSR fields for which the rs1 mask has at least one bit set that corresponds to that field.
RULE 7: If rs1 is not x0, CSRRS reads the CSR and also writes the CSR after applying the mask.
RULE 8: The assembler pseudoinstruction CSRR rd, csr is implemented by encoding CSRRS rd, csr, x0, and therefore exhibits the same read-only, no-write behavior as CSRRS with rs1 = x0.
RULE 9: Counter pseudoinstructions shall be implemented as the read-only CSRRS instruction with the destination register rd, the counter CSR as the CSR address, and rs1 set to x0.
RULE 10: A CSRRS instruction with rs1 equal to x0 is a legal read-only access to the specified CSR, and the other read-only CSR instruction forms based on CSRRC, CSRRSI, and CSRRCI are also legal ways to read these CSRs.
RULE 11: CSRRS treats the CSR identified by the csr field as a source register for the instruction, except when the CSR address is x0.
RULE 12: The exception that makes the CSR a source register for CSRRW or CSRRWI when rd is x0 does not apply to CSRRS.
RULE 13: CSRRS treats the CSR identified by the csr field as a destination register for the instruction, unless the source register rs1 is set to x0.
RULE 14: When a CSRRS instruction attempts to access a seed CSR and the source register rs1 is x0, the instruction must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 15: A CSRRS instruction must ignore the value supplied in rs1, so that no bits are set in the destination CSR regardless of the contents of rs1.
RULE 16: Only the software-writable SEIP bit must participate in the read-modify-write sequence of a CSRRS instruction.
RULE 17: When a CSRRS instruction reads the mip CSR, the value placed in the destination register's SEIP field must be the logical OR of the current software-writable SEIP bit and the external interrupt signal.
RULE 18: When a CSRRS instruction writes to the mip CSR, the software-writable SEIP bit must be updated to the logical OR of its previous value and the corresponding source register bit.
RULE 19: The external interrupt signal must not affect the value written back to the software-writable SEIP bit during a CSRRS instruction.
RULE 20: In a CSRRS rd, mip, rs1 instruction, the destination register bit rd[9] must receive B OR E, where B is the current software-writable SEIP bit and E is the external interrupt signal.
RULE 21: In a CSRRS rd, mip, rs1 instruction, the SEIP bit B must be updated to B OR rs1[9], where rs1[9] is the source register bit corresponding to SEIP.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRSI
FORMAT: csrrsi rd, csr, zimm5
RULE 1: CSRRSI uses a 5-bit unsigned immediate field (uimm[4:0]) taken from the rs1 field, which is zero-extended to XLEN bits and used as a bit-mask.
RULE 2: CSRRSI atomically reads the addressed CSR, zero-extends the read value to XLEN bits, and writes that value to integer register rd.
RULE 3: After reading the CSR, CSRRSI updates the CSR by setting to 1 each bit position where the zero-extended immediate mask has a 1, provided the corresponding CSR bit is writable.
RULE 4: CSRRSI MUST always read the CSR and MUST cause any read side effects, regardless of the values of rd or the immediate.
RULE 5: If the immediate value is zero, CSRRSI MUST NOT write to the CSR.
RULE 6: If the immediate value is zero, CSRRSI MUST NOT cause any side effects that would occur on a CSR write.
RULE 7: If the immediate value is zero, CSRRSI MUST NOT raise an illegal-instruction exception when the addressed CSR is read-only.
RULE 8: If the immediate value is non-zero, CSRRSI MUST write to the CSR.
RULE 9: If the immediate value is non-zero, CSRRSI MUST cause write side effects only for CSR fields for which the immediate mask has at least one bit set corresponding to that field.
RULE 10: If the immediate value is non-zero and the addressed CSR is read-only, CSRRSI MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 11: CSRRSI is a legal instruction that can be used to read the value of a control-and-status register (CSR).
RULE 12: If CSRRSI is executed with uimm[4:0] ≠ 0 and the destination register rd is x0, then the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception; if uimm[4:0] = 0, rd may be x0 without causing an exception.
RULE 13: If CSRRSI targets the seed CSR and the immediate field uimm is zero, the instruction must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 14: CSRRSI must ignore the write value supplied in its immediate field; the uimm does not affect any architectural state.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRW
FORMAT: csrrw rd, csr, rs1
RULE 1: CSRRW shall atomically read-modify-write a single CSR identified by the 12-bit csr field in bits 31-20 of the instruction encoding.
RULE 2: When the destination register rd of a CSRRW is not x0, CSRRW shall read the old value of the specified CSR, zero-extend it to XLEN bits, and write the resulting value to integer register rd.
RULE 3: When the destination register rd of a CSRRW is not x0, CSRRW shall write the initial value held in integer register rs1 to the specified CSR.
RULE 4: When the destination register rd of a CSRRW is x0, CSRRW shall not read the specified CSR and shall not cause any side effects that might occur on a CSR read.
RULE 5: When the destination register rd of a CSRRW is x0, CSRRW shall still write the initial value held in integer register rs1 to the specified CSR.
RULE 6: When the source register rs1 of a CSRRW is x0, CSRRW shall attempt to write the value zero to the specified CSR.
RULE 7: If a CSRRW instruction fails to apply the defined side effects of any field in the CSR it writes, then a subsequent read of that CSR returns the original (pre‑instruction) value for those fields, indicating the side effects were not performed.
RULE 8: CSRRW shall always perform a write to the specified CSR regardless of the values of rd or rs1.
RULE 9: In a CSRRW instruction, when the destination register rd is x0, the CSR operand is not treated as a source register.
RULE 10: In a CSRRW instruction, the architectural register specified by rd, when rd is a register other than x0, is treated as a destination register.
RULE 11: CSRRW targeting the seed CSR must copy the current value of the seed CSR into the destination register rd.
RULE 12: CSRRW targeting the seed CSR must ignore the write value supplied in rs1 (or uimm) and must not modify the seed CSR.
RULE 13: CSRRW is permitted to access the seed CSR and therefore does not raise an illegal-instruction exception solely because it is not a read-only CSR instruction.
RULE 14: CSRRW that attempts to read or write the seed CSR while the processor is in a privilege mode other than M and access has not been explicitly granted must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 15: Execution of CSRRW ra, ssp, ra writes the previous value of register ra into the ssp CSR.
RULE 16: When a CSRRW instruction is executed on the mip CSR, the destination register receives the value of the CSR as read, and for the SEIP field this read value equals the logical-OR of the software-writable bit B and the external-interrupt signal E (B || E).
RULE 17: When a CSRRW instruction writes the mip CSR, only the software-writable bit B of the SEIP field is updated, and it is set to the value of source-register bit t1[9].
RULE 18: The write performed by a CSRRW instruction to the SEIP field of the mip CSR does not depend on the external-interrupt signal E.
RULE 19: CSRRW shall read the current value of the specified CSR into the destination integer register and shall write the value contained in the source integer register into that CSR, thereby swapping the two values.
RULE 20: CSRRW is permitted to be performed on the sscratch CSR because sscratch is defined as an SXLEN-bit read/write register.

INSTRUCTION: CSRRWI
FORMAT: csrrwi rd, csr, zimm5
RULE 1: CSRRWI obtains the XLEN-bit value to write to the CSR by zero-extending the 5-bit unsigned immediate field (uimm[4:0]) encoded in the rs1 field.
RULE 2: If the destination register rd of a CSRRWI instruction is x0, the instruction shall not read the CSR and shall not cause any side effects that could occur on a CSR read.
RULE 3: When rd of a CSRRWI instruction is x0, the instruction shall write the CSR with the zero-extended immediate value.
RULE 4: When rd of a CSRRWI instruction is not x0, the instruction shall read the current value of the CSR, write the CSR with the zero-extended immediate value, and place the original CSR value into rd.

INSTRUCTION: CTZ
FORMAT: ctz rd, rs1
RULE 1: The CTZ instruction MUST count the number of trailing zero bits in the value held in source register rs and write that count to destination register rd.
RULE 2: The CTZ instruction computes the number of consecutive zero bits starting from the least-significant bit of the value in rs and writes that count to rd.
RULE 3: The CTZ instruction counts the number of consecutive zero bits starting at bit position 0 (the least-significant bit) of the value read from rs.
RULE 4: The CTZ instruction writes to the architectural register identified by rd the count produced by its trailing-zero calculation.
RULE 5: If the value read from rs is zero, the CTZ instruction produces the count XLEN and writes XLEN to rd.
RULE 6: If the least-significant bit of the value read from rs is one, the CTZ instruction produces the count 0 and writes 0 to rd.
RULE 7: For any non-zero value read from rs whose least-significant set bit is at position i (where 0 ≤ i < XLEN), the CTZ instruction writes the integer i to rd.
RULE 8: CTZ must produce a result of 0 in rd when bit 0 of the least-significant word of rs is 1.

INSTRUCTION: CTZW
FORMAT: ctzw rd, rs1
RULE 1: When executing CTZW, the upper 32 bits of the source register are ignored and only the least-significant 32 bits are used.
RULE 2: CTZW counts the number of trailing zero bits in the least-significant 32-bit word of the source register.
RULE 3: CTZW writes the count as a 32-bit signed integer into the destination register.
RULE 4: CTZW sign-extends the 32-bit signed result to the processor's XLEN before writing it to the destination register.
RULE 5: CTZW MUST be defined only for implementations that support the RV64 base ISA and is not defined for the RV32 base ISA.
RULE 6: CTZW reads the value in source register rs and writes the computed result to destination register rd.
RULE 7: CTZW computes the number of consecutive zero bits starting at the least-significant bit of the low-order 32-bit word of rs and writes that count (a value between 0 and 32 inclusive) to rd.
RULE 8: CTZW counts the number of consecutive zero bits starting at the least-significant bit and stopping before the first one-bit within the least-significant 32-bit word of the source register.
RULE 9: CTZW produces the result value 32 when every bit of the least-significant 32-bit word of the source register is zero.
RULE 10: CTZW produces the result value 0 when the least-significant bit of the source register is one.
RULE 11: CTZW writes to the destination register the integer equal to the index of the first one-bit found in the least-significant 32-bit word of the source register, where index 0 corresponds to bit 0.
RULE 12: CTZW ignores all bits of the source register above bit 31; only the least-significant 32-bit word influences the computed result.
RULE 13: CTZW stores the computed result in the destination register with zero-extension to the full XLEN width.

INSTRUCTION: CZERO.EQZ
FORMAT: czero.eqz rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: When CZERO.EQZ is executed and the evaluated condition is equal to zero, CZERO.EQZ writes the integer value zero to the destination register rd.
RULE 2: When CZERO.EQZ is executed and the evaluated condition is not equal to zero, CZERO.EQZ leaves the destination register rd unchanged.
RULE 3: CZERO.EQZ does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 4: CZERO.EQZ does not cause any memory accesses, CSR updates, or changes to the program counter other than the normal sequential advance.
RULE 5: CZERO.EQZ MUST write the zero value to rd when the value read from rs2 is zero.
RULE 6: CZERO.EQZ MUST copy the value read from rs1 to rd when the value read from rs2 is not zero.
RULE 7: CZERO.EQZ establishes a predicate that causes the integer instruction that immediately follows CZERO.EQZ to be architecturally executed only when the third operand register of CZERO.EQZ contains a non-zero value.
RULE 8: CZERO.EQZ does not modify any architectural integer register; its only architectural effect is the creation of the predicate for the following instruction.
RULE 9: The predicate created by CZERO.EQZ applies exclusively to the single instruction that directly follows CZERO.EQZ and has no effect on any later instructions.
RULE 10: CZERO.EQZ can be paired with an integer add, subtract, bitwise-or, or bitwise-xor instruction to form a two-instruction conditional operation where the arithmetic result is written to the destination register only if the condition register operand of CZERO.EQZ is non-zero.
RULE 11: In a three-instruction sequence implementing a conditional bitwise-and, CZERO.EQZ controls execution of the final move of the temporary register to the destination register, and this final move occurs only if the condition register operand of CZERO.EQZ is non-zero.
RULE 12: In a three-instruction sequence implementing a conditional select, CZERO.EQZ controls execution of the final addition that combines the temporary register holding rs2 with the destination register, and this addition occurs only if the condition register operand of CZERO.EQZ is non-zero.

INSTRUCTION: CZERO.NEZ
FORMAT: czero.nez rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: When a CZERO.NEZ instruction executes and the condition operand rs2 is nonzero, the architectural result written to the destination register rd is zero.
RULE 2: CZERO.NEZ MUST write the architectural zero value to destination register rd when the architectural value read from source register rs2 is not zero.
RULE 3: The CZERO.NEZ add operation produces rd = rs1 + rs2 when rc equals zero; otherwise rd is left unchanged.
RULE 4: The CZERO.NEZ xor operation produces rd = rs1 ^ rs2 when rc equals zero; otherwise rd is left unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: DIV
FORMAT: div rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: DIV must compute the signed integer division of the XLEN-bit dividend in rs1 by the XLEN-bit divisor in rs2, producing a quotient that is rounded toward zero.
RULE 2: DIV must ensure that, when the divisor is non-zero and the operation does not overflow, the computed quotient and remainder satisfy dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder.
RULE 3: DIV must produce a quotient whose bits are all set (i.e., -1) when the divisor supplied to the instruction is zero.
RULE 4: DIV experiences signed-division overflow only when the dividend equals the most-negative signed XLEN-bit integer (-2^{XLEN-1}) and the divisor equals -1.
RULE 5: When signed-division overflow occurs for DIV (i.e., dividend = -2^{XLEN-1} and divisor = -1), DIV must return a quotient equal to the dividend (-2^{XLEN-1}) and a remainder of zero.

INSTRUCTION: DIVU
FORMAT: divu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: DIVU must compute an XLEN-bit unsigned integer quotient by dividing the value in rs1 (the dividend) by the value in rs2 (the divisor) and must round the result toward zero.
RULE 2: For any DIVU execution where the divisor in rs2 is non-zero, the produced quotient Q must satisfy the identity dividend = divisor × Q + remainder, where the remainder is the unsigned remainder defined by the corresponding REMU operation.
RULE 3: If a DIVU instruction raises an overflow exception, then the core takes an illegal‑instruction exception and mcause is set to the illegal‑instruction exception code.
RULE 4: When the divisor in rs2 is zero, DIVU must return a quotient whose all XLEN bits are set (value 2^XLEN-1).

INSTRUCTION: DIVUW
FORMAT: divuw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: DIVUW divides the lower 32 bits of rs1 by the lower 32 bits of rs2 as unsigned integers and writes the 32-bit quotient into rd with sign-extension to 64 bits.
RULE 2: DIVUW rounds the computed quotient toward zero.
RULE 3: If the lower 32 bits of rs2 are zero, DIVUW produces the 32-bit value 2^32-1 and sign-extends it to 64 bits as the result.
RULE 4: DIVUW never generates an overflow because unsigned division overflow cannot occur.
RULE 5: DIVUW does not cause an architectural trap or exception when a divide-by-zero occurs; the all-1s result is returned instead.

INSTRUCTION: DIVW
FORMAT: divw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: DIVW computes the signed division of the lower 32 bits of rs1 by the lower 32 bits of rs2, places the 32-bit quotient into rd, and sign-extends the result to 64 bits.
RULE 2: DIVW produces a result of -1 (all 32 low bits set) sign-extended to 64 bits when the lower 32 bits of rs2 (the divisor) are zero.
RULE 3: DIVW experiences signed-division overflow only when the lower 32 bits of rs1 equal -2^31 and the lower 32 bits of rs2 equal -1, and in that case the result written to rd is the sign-extension of -2^31.
RULE 4: DIVW defines that, in the signed-division overflow case, the architectural quotient equals the dividend and the corresponding remainder would be zero.

INSTRUCTION: FADD.Q
FORMAT: fadd.q fd, fs1, fs2, rm
RULE 1: FADD.Q must compute the sum of the quad-precision floating-point value in source register rs1 and the quad-precision floating-point value in source register rs2 and produce a quad-precision floating-point result.
RULE 2: FADD.Q must write the quad-precision result of the addition to destination register rd.
RULE 3: FADD.Q must apply the rounding mode specified by the rm field of the instruction to the result.
RULE 4: FADD.Q must designate the destination register for the result using the rd field.

INSTRUCTION: FADD.S
FORMAT: fadd.s fd, fs1, fs2, rm
RULE 1: FADD.S performs single-precision floating-point addition of the values held in source registers rs1 and rs2.
RULE 2: FADD.S writes the result of the floating-point addition to the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: FCLASS.D
FORMAT: fclass.d rd, fs1
RULE 1: FCLASS.D MUST classify a double-precision floating-point operand using the same classification categories defined for the single-precision FCLASS.S instruction.

INSTRUCTION: FCLASS.H
FORMAT: fclass.h rd, fs1
RULE 1: FCLASS.H classifies a half-precision floating-point operand and produces a classification result.
RULE 2: The classification performed by FCLASS.H follows the same definition as the single-precision floating-point classify instruction, applied to half-precision operands.

INSTRUCTION: FCLASS.S
FORMAT: fclass.s rd, fs1
RULE 1: FCLASS.S MUST write to destination integer register rd a 10-bit mask in which each bit position 0 through 9 corresponds to a distinct class of the rs1 value as defined in the specification.
RULE 2: For each class, FCLASS.S MUST set the corresponding rd bit if the rs1 value belongs to that class and MUST clear that bit if the rs1 value does not belong to that class.
RULE 3: FCLASS.S MUST clear all bits of rd that are not among bits 0 through 9 of the class mask.
RULE 4: FCLASS.S MUST ensure that exactly one of bits 0 through 9 in rd is set after execution.
RULE 5: FCLASS.S MUST not modify any floating-point exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.BF16.S
FORMAT: fcvt.bf16.s fd, fs1, rm
RULE 1: FCVT.BF16.S MUST convert a 32-bit single-precision floating-point (FP32) value to a BF16 value.
RULE 2: FCVT.BF16.S is only defined when the single-precision floating-point extension F is implemented; if extension F is absent, the FCVT.BF16.S instruction is not defined.
RULE 3: FCVT.BF16.S rd, rs1 converts the 32-bit floating-point value in floating-point register rs1 to a bfloat16 value and writes the result to floating-point register rd.
RULE 4: FCVT.BF16.S must round the conversion result according to the current rounding-mode (RM) field.
RULE 5: FCVT.BF16.S may raise the overflow exception if the converted BF16 result is too large to be represented.
RULE 6: FCVT.BF16.S may raise the underflow exception if the converted BF16 result is too small to be represented as a normalized BF16 value.
RULE 7: FCVT.BF16.S may raise the inexact exception when any non-zero bits of the FP32 mantissa are discarded during conversion.
RULE 8: FCVT.BF16.S may raise the invalid exception when the source FP32 value is a NaN or otherwise not representable as a BF16 value.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.H
FORMAT: fcvt.d.h fd, fs1
RULE 1: FCVT.D.H reads a half-precision floating-point value from the source floating-point register rs1 and writes the resulting double-precision floating-point value to the destination floating-point register rd.
RULE 2: FCVT.D.H performs the conversion using the rounding mode indicated by the rm field, applying the rounding rules defined for floating-point-to-floating-point conversions.
RULE 3: The FCVT.D.H instruction MUST be defined only when the Zdinx extension is implemented; if Zdinx is not implemented, the FCVT.D.H instruction is not defined by the architecture.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.L
FORMAT: fcvt.d.l fd, rs1, rm
RULE 1: FCVT.D.L MUST convert the 64-bit signed integer value held in integer register rs1 to a double-precision floating-point value written to floating-point register rd.
RULE 2: For FCVT.D.L the range of integer values that are considered valid inputs and the architectural behavior for inputs outside that range are identical to those specified for the FCVT.int.S instruction.
RULE 3: FCVT.D.L MUST round the conversion result according to the rounding mode indicated by the rm field.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.LU
FORMAT: fcvt.d.lu fd, rs1, rm
RULE 1: FCVT.D.LU reads the unsigned integer value from integer register rs1 and writes the resulting double-precision floating-point value to floating-point register rd.
RULE 2: If FCVT.D.LU is executed on a non‑RV64 implementation, then it raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 3: FCVT.D.LU rounds the conversion result according to the rounding mode specified in the rm field of the instruction.
RULE 4: FCVT.D.LU must apply the same valid-input range limits and raise the same floating-point exceptions for out-of-range inputs as the corresponding single-precision conversion instructions FCVT.int.S.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.Q
FORMAT: fcvt.d.q fd, fs1, rm
RULE 1: FCVT.D.Q converts a quad-precision floating-point number to a double-precision floating-point number.
RULE 2: FCVT.D.Q applies the rounding mode indicated by the rm field in the same manner as other double-precision floating-point-to-floating-point conversion instructions.
RULE 3: The rm field of the FCVT.D.Q instruction encoding selects the rounding mode used for the conversion.
RULE 4: The rd field of the FCVT.D.Q instruction encoding selects the destination register that receives the resulting double-precision floating-point value.

INSTRUCTION: FCVT.D.W
FORMAT: fcvt.d.w fd, rs1, rm
RULE 1: FCVT.D.W must read a signed 32-bit integer from integer register rs1 and write the corresponding double-precision floating-point value to floating-point register rd.
