RULE 2: Executing HFENCE.GVMA in U-mode always raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: Attempting to execute HFENCE.GVMA in U-mode causes an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 4: Attempting to execute HFENCE.GVMA in HS-mode when mstatus.TVM=1 causes an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 5: If TVM=1 and the current privilege mode is HS, an attempted execution of HFENCE.GVMA MUST cause an illegal-instruction trap.
RULE 6: The TVM setting has no effect on the execution of HFENCE.GVMA in any privilege mode other than HS.

INSTRUCTION: HFENCE.VVMA
FORMAT: hfence.vvma rs1, rs2
RULE 1: Executing the HFENCE.VVMA instruction in U-mode must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 2: The HFENCE.VVMA instruction is not subject to any additional execution restrictions in S-mode, HS-mode, or M-mode.
RULE 3: If rs2 is not x0, bits XLEN-1 through ASIDMAX of the rs2 value are reserved for future standard use and current implementations of HFENCE.VVMA must ignore them.
RULE 4: The settings of mstatus.TVM and hstatus.VTVM do not cause HFENCE.VVMA to trap.
RULE 5: Executing HFENCE.VVMA in U-mode triggers an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 6: If the TVM bit in mstatus is set to 1, the HFENCE.VVMA instruction is not prohibited and may be executed in HS-mode.
RULE 7: If the TVM bit in mstatus is set to 1, execution of the HFENCE.VVMA instruction must not raise a trap.

INSTRUCTION: HINVAL.GVMA
FORMAT: hinval.gvma rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HINVAL.GVMA requires the same permissions as HFENCE.GVMA and raises the same exceptions as HFENCE.GVMA.
RULE 2: Executing HINVAL.GVMA in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: Executing HINVAL.GVMA in S-mode or HS-mode while mstatus.TVM=1 raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 4: In HS-mode, if the TVM bit in mstatus is 1, execution of the HINVAL.GVMA instruction must be prevented.
RULE 5: HINVAL.GVMA is defined as a privileged instruction.

INSTRUCTION: HINVAL.VVMA
FORMAT: hinval.vvma rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HINVAL.VVMA requires the same permissions as HFENCE.VVMA and raises the same exceptions as HFENCE.VVMA.
RULE 2: Executing HINVAL.VVMA in U-mode always raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: HINVAL.VVMA is not subject to the mstatus.TVM restriction.
RULE 4: When the TVM field in mstatus is 1, the TVM setting MUST NOT prevent execution of the HINVAL.VVMA instruction.
RULE 5: When the TVM field in mstatus is 1, the TVM setting MUST NOT cause any trap on execution of the HINVAL.VVMA instruction.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.B
FORMAT: hlv.b rd, rs1
RULE 1: If the V bit in hstatus is 1, executing HLV.B raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 2: If HLV.B is executed in U-mode while hstatus.HU = 0, an illegal-instruction exception is raised.
RULE 3: The rs2 field of an HLV.B encoding must be zero, and any non-zero rs2 value makes the encoding reserved.
RULE 4: The funct7 field of an HLV.B encoding must be 0110000, and any other funct7 value makes the encoding reserved.
RULE 5: The funct3 field of an HLV.B encoding must be 100, and any other funct3 value makes the encoding reserved.
RULE 6: In an HLV.B instruction the rd field specifies the destination integer register that receives the loaded byte.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.BU
FORMAT: hlv.bu rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.BU MUST be executed only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or when the current mode is U-mode and the hstatus.HU field equals 1.
RULE 2: HLV.BU reads an unsigned byte from the effective address, zero-extends it to XLEN, and returns the result, mirroring the architectural effect of the base-ISA LBU instruction.
RULE 3: If HLV.BU is executed in U-mode while hstatus.HU equals 0, the instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.D
FORMAT: hlv.d rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.D MUST be executed only in M-mode or HS-mode, or in U-mode when hstatus.HU=1.
RULE 2: If execution is in U-mode and hstatus.HU=0, executing HLV.D MUST cause an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: HLV.D uses the rd field to specify the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.H
FORMAT: hlv.h rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.H MUST be executed only in M-mode, HS-mode, or in U-mode when hstatus.HU=1.
RULE 2: Execution of HLV.H from U-mode when hstatus.HU=0 causes an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: HLV.H loads a signed half-word from memory, sign-extends it to the hart's XLEN, and writes the result to the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.HU
FORMAT: hlv.hu rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.HU loads a 16-bit unsigned value from the effective memory address and writes the zero-extended result to the destination integer register.
RULE 2: HLV.HU MUST be executed only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or when the current privilege mode is U-mode and hstatus.HU=1.
RULE 3: If HLV.HU is executed in U-mode while hstatus.HU=0, an illegal-instruction exception MUST be raised.
RULE 4: HLV.HU raises the same architectural exceptions as other load instructions.
RULE 5: HLV.HU loads a 16-bit halfword from the memory address computed by adding the sign-extended 12-bit immediate to the value in rs1 and writes the zero-extended result to rd.
RULE 6: HLV.HU treats the loaded halfword as unsigned and zero-extends it to the XLEN-wide destination register.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.W
FORMAT: hlv.w rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.W MUST be executed only in M-mode or HS-mode, or in U-mode when hstatus.HU=1.
RULE 2: HLV.W raises the same exceptions as other load instructions.
RULE 3: If HLV.W is executed in U-mode while hstatus.HU=0, it causes an illegal-instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.WU
FORMAT: hlv.wu rd, rs1
RULE 1: When the HS-level sstatus.MXR field is 1, HLV.WU treats execute-only pages as readable for both the VS-stage and the G-stage of address translation.
RULE 2: HLV.WU is defined only for RV64 implementations and is not a valid instruction on RV32 implementations.
RULE 3: HLV.WU requires read permission during address translation and does not require execute permission, matching the permission requirements of the corresponding RV64I LWU instruction.
RULE 4: HLV.WU raises the same architectural exceptions as a regular load instruction, including access-fault exceptions on PMP violations.
RULE 5: If the processor is in U-mode and the hstatus.HU field is 0, execution of HLV.WU causes an illegal-instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: HLVX.HU
FORMAT: hlvx.hu rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLVX.HU instruction must be executed only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or U-mode with hstatus.HU=1; otherwise an illegal-instruction exception is raised.
RULE 2: For HLVX.HU the memory being read must be executable in both stages of address translation, and read permission is not required for translation.
RULE 3: HLVX.HU cannot override machine-level physical memory protection, and attempting to read memory that PMP marks as execute-only causes an access-fault exception.
RULE 4: HLVX.HU raises the same load-instruction exceptions as ordinary load instructions for faults, rather than fetch-type exceptions.
RULE 5: In an HLVX.HU instruction, the rd field specifies the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: HLVX.WU
FORMAT: hlvx.wu rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLVX.WU must be executed only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or when the current mode is U-mode and hstatus.HU=1; otherwise an illegal-instruction exception is raised.
RULE 2: During address translation for HLVX.WU, the accessed memory must have execute permission in both translation stages; read permission is not required.
RULE 3: HLVX.WU cannot bypass machine-level PMP, and if PMP designates the target as execute-only, HLVX.WU generates an access-fault exception.
RULE 4: HLVX.WU raises the same load-type exceptions as ordinary load instructions, rather than fetch exceptions, even though it requires execute permission.
RULE 5: Memory accesses performed by HLVX.WU are not subject to pointer masking, and pointer masking does not apply even when MXR is set.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.B
FORMAT: hsv.b rs1, rs2
RULE 1: When hstatus.SPVP = 1, the explicit memory access performed by HSV.B uses an effective privilege mode of VS.
RULE 2: HSV.B produces the same architectural effect as the RV32I/RV64I SB store instruction.
RULE 3: If the current mode is U-mode and hstatus.HU = 0, executing HSV.B raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 4: HSV.B uses rs2 as the source register that provides the byte value to be stored.
RULE 5: HSV.B uses rs1 as the base address register to compute the effective memory address.
RULE 6: HSV.B stores the low-order byte of rs2 to the memory location whose address is the value held in rs1.
RULE 7: HSV.B ignores the rd field because store instructions do not produce a destination register result.
RULE 8: HSV.B has no specified permission checks, exception conditions, or side effects beyond performing the byte store.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.D
FORMAT: hsv.d rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HSV.D MUST be executed only in M-mode, HS-mode, or in U-mode when hstatus.HU = 1; execution in any other privilege mode is not valid.
RULE 2: HSV.D is not valid for RV32 and is defined only for RV64 implementations.
RULE 3: If an HSV.D instruction completes without generating a load or store to the address it computes, then no memory transaction occurs (e.g., a memory‑mapped I/O location does not observe the expected access).
RULE 4: The HS-level sstatus.SUM field is ignored for the address translation performed by HSV.D.
RULE 5: The HS-level sstatus.MXR field makes execute-only pages readable by explicit loads for both translation stages of the memory access performed by HSV.D.
RULE 6: Executing HSV.D from U-mode when hstatus.HU = 0 causes an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 7: HSV.D must be decoded only when the 7-bit opcode field equals 1110011, the 7-bit funct7 field equals 0110111, the 3-bit funct3 field equals 100, and the rd field equals 00000.
RULE 8: HSV.D is defined only for RV64 implementations and is not defined for RV32 or any other base ISA variant.
RULE 9: In an HSV.D instruction the rs2 register provides the 64-bit value that is to be stored to memory.
RULE 10: The rd field of HSV.D is architecturally ignored and must be zero in the encoding.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.H
FORMAT: hsv.h rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HSV.H is valid only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or when the current mode is U-mode and the hstatus.HU field is 1.
RULE 2: If HSV.H is executed from U-mode while hstatus.HU is 0, an illegal-instruction exception is raised.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.W
FORMAT: hsv.w rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HSV.W is only valid when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or when the current privilege mode is U-mode and the hstatus.HU field is 1.
RULE 2: If HSV.W is executed in U-mode while hstatus.HU is 0, an illegal-instruction exception is raised.
RULE 3: The funct7 field of HSV.W MUST be the binary pattern 0110101, which identifies the Hypervisor Store class.
RULE 4: The funct3 field of HSV.W MUST be the binary pattern 100, which selects the word-size store variant.
RULE 5: The rd field of HSV.W is hard-wired to the value 00000 because store instructions do not write a destination register.

INSTRUCTION: JAL
FORMAT: jal rd, label
RULE 1: JAL forms its jump target address by adding a signed offset, represented as a multiple of 2 bytes in the sign-extended J-immediate field, to the address of the JAL instruction.
RULE 2: The jump target address produced by JAL is confined to a range of ±1 MiB relative to the address of the JAL instruction.
RULE 3: JAL stores the address of the following instruction (pc + 4) into the architectural register specified by its rd field.
RULE 4: When the rd field of JAL specifies register x0, the write of pc + 4 has no architectural effect because x0 is hardwired to zero.
RULE 5: JAL generates an instruction-address-misaligned exception if the computed target address is not aligned to a four-byte boundary.
RULE 6: The JAL instruction computes its target address by adding a sign-extended immediate offset to the address of the current instruction (PC-relative).
RULE 7: The JAL instruction supports an immediate offset range that is larger than the offset range supported by conditional branch instructions.
RULE 8: A JAL instruction with destination register x0 discards the link address and unconditionally transfers control to the target address.
RULE 9: A JAL instruction with destination register x1 writes the address of the instruction following the JAL (pc+2 when the JAL originates from a compressed 2-byte instruction) into register x1 before transferring control to the target address.
RULE 10: The target address of a JAL instruction is formed by sign-extending the immediate offset and adding the result to the current program counter.
RULE 11: JAL MUST transfer control to a target address that lies within ±1 MiB of the current PC.

INSTRUCTION: JALR
FORMAT: jalr rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: The target address produced by a JALR instruction is calculated by adding the sign-extended 12-bit immediate to the value in register rs1 and then setting the least-significant bit of the sum to zero.
RULE 2: A JALR instruction writes the address of the following instruction (pc + 4) to the architectural register specified by rd.
RULE 3: When rd is register x0 in a JALR instruction, the computed return address is discarded and no link is created.
RULE 4: A JALR instruction that uses rd = x0, rs1 = x1 and an immediate of zero implements the standard calling-convention return (RET) behavior.
RULE 5: The 12-bit immediate in a JALR instruction is added to rs1 without being multiplied by two; it is not treated as a multiple of two bytes.
RULE 6: The least-significant bit of the target address of a JALR instruction is always cleared, regardless of the values of rs1 and the immediate.
RULE 7: If the target address computed by a JALR instruction is not aligned to a four-byte boundary, the implementation must raise an instruction-address-misaligned exception.
RULE 8: On implementations where IALIGN = 16, a JALR instruction can never raise an instruction-address-misaligned exception because all instruction addresses are 16-byte aligned.
RULE 9: Encoding a plain unconditional indirect jump (JR) as a JALR with rd = x0 yields the same architectural effect as the JR pseudoinstruction.
RULE 10: JALR with destination register x0 and immediate 0 must set the next PC to the value contained in source register rs1, performing an unconditional jump, provided rs1 ≠ x0.
RULE 11: JALR with destination register x1 and immediate 0 must set the next PC to the value contained in source register rs1 and must write the address of the following compressed instruction (pc + 2) into register x1, provided rs1 ≠ x0.
RULE 12: If rs1 = x0 for a JALR with destination register x1 and immediate 0, the encoding corresponds to the C.EBREAK instruction and the JALR semantics do not apply.
RULE 13: JALR obtains its branch target address by adding the sign-extended 12-bit immediate encoded in the instruction to the value held in the rs1 register.
RULE 14: If a JALR instruction writes to a register other than x0 (rd ≠ x0), then the destination register is updated with the return address (pc+4), so the instruction is treated as a call.
RULE 15: When a JALR instruction generates a software-check exception caused by the Zicfilp extension, that exception has higher priority than an illegal-instruction exception but lower priority than an instruction access-fault exception.

INSTRUCTION: LB
FORMAT: lb rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: LB must compute its effective address by adding the contents of register rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset.
RULE 2: LB must read an 8-bit byte from memory at the effective address.
RULE 3: LB must sign-extend the loaded 8-bit byte to the XLEN width of the processor and write the resulting value to destination register rd.
RULE 4: LB must raise any exceptions that occur during the memory access even when the destination register rd is x0, and the loaded value is discarded.
RULE 5: LB must not raise an address-misaligned exception when its effective address is naturally aligned for a byte access.
RULE 6: In a system where endianness is byte-address invariant, LB must return the exact byte value previously stored to the same address regardless of the current endianness.
RULE 7: If an LB instruction accesses a naturally aligned address, then the load is performed atomically, delivering the byte value to the destination register as a single indivisible operation.
RULE 8: LB must load an 8-bit value from memory, sign-extend that value to the processor's XLEN, and write the sign-extended result into integer register rd.
RULE 9: LB is defined analogously to LW and LH, meaning that LB performs the same sign-extension operation on the loaded 8-bit value to XLEN that LW performs on a 32-bit value and that LH performs on a 16-bit value.
RULE 10: The LB instruction atomically loads a byte from the memory address contained in rs1.
RULE 11: The byte loaded by the LB instruction is sign-extended to XLEN bits before being written to rd.
RULE 12: An LB instruction must correctly handle misaligned address operands, subject to the same architectural limitations that apply when pointer masking is not performed.
RULE 13: When an LB instruction accesses a misaligned address, the byte fetched shall be the same as the byte that would be fetched if the pointer-masking transformation were applied separately to each aligned byte of the access without pointer masking.

INSTRUCTION: LBU
FORMAT: lbu rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: LBU loads an unsigned 8-bit value from the effective address and zero-extends the value to the processor's XLEN width.
RULE 2: When the destination register rd of an LBU is x0, the instruction must still raise any load-related exceptions and must still perform any side effects even though the loaded value is discarded.
RULE 3: LBU must not raise an address-misaligned exception for any effective address because a byte access is naturally aligned for all addresses.
RULE 4: In a system whose endianness is byte-address invariant, an LBU performed at an address that previously stored a byte returns exactly the stored byte value, independent of the system's endianness.
RULE 5: LBU must fetch an 8-bit byte from the effective memory address, zero-extend that byte to 64 bits, and write the resulting 64-bit value into the destination register rd when executed on an RV64I implementation.
RULE 6: LBU must apply the same address-space and access-permission checks as all other load instructions, operating within the 64-bit address space defined by RV64I and respecting the execution environment's definition of legally accessible memory regions.
RULE 7: LBU must be defined for RV64 implementations.
RULE 8: LBU must compute the effective address by adding the sign-extended immediate offset to the base register value.
RULE 9: LBU must read one byte from memory at the effective address.
RULE 10: LBU must zero-extend the loaded byte to the XLEN of the executing ISA.
RULE 11: LBU must exhibit identical architectural behavior (address calculation, memory access, zero-extension) for both the compressed c.lbu form and the regular 32-bit LBU.
RULE 12: LBU (including its compressed c.lbu form) is only present when the Zcb extension is implemented, and the Zcb extension depends on the Zca extension.
RULE 13: LBU is a standard non-compressed load instruction that may cause a transformed instruction to be written to the trap-instruction CSR mtinst or htinst when a synchronous exception occurs.
RULE 14: Because LBU is an explicit memory access, the address used by an LBU must be transformed by pointer masking when pointer masking is enabled for the privilege mode that performs the access.
RULE 15: Pointer masking is excluded only for SFENCE.*, HFENCE.*, SINVAL.*, and HINVAL.*, and since LBU is not listed among these exclusions, pointer masking applies to LBU.
RULE 16: Misaligned LBU accesses are supported and the bytes loaded are identical to those that would be accessed if the pointer-masking transformation were applied individually to each aligned constituent byte of the access.

INSTRUCTION: LD
FORMAT: ld rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: If a LD.{aq,aqrl} instruction loads a value whose size is less than XLEN, then the destination register rd contains the sign‑extended value of the loaded data.
RULE 2: LD loads a 64-bit value from memory into the architectural register rd.
RULE 3: When LD is generated by expanding a C.LDSP instruction, the destination register rd must not be x0; encodings that specify rd = x0 are reserved.
RULE 4: The LD instruction must load a 64-bit value from memory into the destination register rd′.
RULE 5: During the cm.pop operation, the address used by the LD instruction shall be computed as sp plus stack_adj minus bytes.
RULE 6: Each LD instruction executed by the cm.pop operation shall load a double-word (8 bytes) from the memory location given by addr into the destination register x[i]; the initial addr is sp plus stack_adj minus 8 and is decremented by 8 after each load.
RULE 7: In a 64-bit XLEN implementation, the LD instruction reads an 8-byte (doubleword) value from memory.
RULE 8: The LD instruction transfers the 8-byte value stored at the effective address to the integer register specified as the destination operand.
RULE 9: If the LD executed as part of a cm.popret does not load an exact 8‑byte value from the effective memory address, then after cm.popret the destination register contains a value that differs from the 8‑byte word stored at that address.
RULE 10: For the LD instruction used in cm.popret, the effective address is calculated as sp plus stack_adj minus 8 bytes.
RULE 11: The LD instruction writes the loaded doubleword into the architectural integer register identified by its destination field.
RULE 12: LD must not raise an address-misaligned exception when its effective address is a multiple of 8 bytes.
RULE 13: If the effective address of an LD instruction is a multiple of 4 bytes, then the two 32‑bit word accesses that form the 64‑bit load are atomic, so after execution the destination register contains either the entire original 64‑bit memory value or the entire updated 64‑bit memory value—mixed‑half values must not appear.
RULE 14: When the effective address of LD is a multiple of 8 bytes, the word accesses are guaranteed to be atomic and no misaligned trap may be raised.
RULE 15: If LD takes a trap, the architectural value of the base register (rs1) must remain unchanged.
RULE 16: When LD uses a stack-pointer-relative address, the base register that must retain its original value on a trap is x2; otherwise the base register is rs1.
RULE 17: LD instructions with destination x0 must perform the memory load as specified but must discard the loaded data and must not write any value to architectural register x0.
RULE 18: If a split‑address LD instruction raises a memory‑access exception on its later (second) memory operation, then the data read by the earlier (first) memory operation is written to the destination register and the exception is taken (mcause set to the corresponding load exception).
RULE 19: LD loads a 64-bit value from memory into register rd.
RULE 20: If the offset is not zero‑extended before scaling, the effective address for LD is computed with a sign‑extended offset, causing the load to access an incorrect memory location; the destination register will contain the value from that wrong address instead of the value at the correctly zero‑extended address.
RULE 21: LD MUST be defined as a 64-bit load instruction in the RV64I base ISA.
RULE 22: When a standard LD instruction traps, the transformed instruction written to the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst) must have the same funct3, rd, and opcode fields as the original LD.
RULE 23: In the transformed LD stored in mtinst or htinst, the Addr.Offset field (bits 19:15) must equal the positive difference between the faulting virtual address (written to mtval or stval) and the original virtual address.
RULE 24: The two low-order bits (bits 1:0) of the transformed LD in mtinst or htinst must be binary 11 because the original LD is not compressed.
RULE 25: If an LD instruction does not apply the pointer‑masking transformation to its computed address, then the load uses the unmasked address and a load‑access‑fault exception is raised when that unmasked address is illegal (otherwise the destination register receives the value from the unmasked address).
RULE 26: If an LD instruction fails to apply pointer masking to its effective address, then the destination register receives the memory value from the unmasked address instead of the masked address.
RULE 27: If MPRV or SPVP is enabled, LD must apply pointer masking using the pointer-masking settings of the effective privilege mode.
RULE 28: When MXR is set in the effective privilege mode at which an LD is performed, pointer masking must not be applied to that LD.
RULE 29: Even when page-based virtual memory is not in effect, MXR must be consulted to determine whether pointer masking is applied to an LD.
RULE 30: If an LD is executed with a misaligned address that would be illegal when pointer masking is absent, then the load raises a load‑address‑misaligned exception (mcause = 0x5); otherwise, if the misaligned address is permitted by the architectural limits, the destination register receives the correctly assembled 64‑bit value.
RULE 31: The bytes accessed by a misaligned LD must be identical to the bytes that would be accessed if the pointer-masking transformation were applied individually to each byte of the access.

INSTRUCTION: LH
FORMAT: lh rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: The effective address for LH is computed by adding the contents of register rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset.
RULE 2: When the destination register rd of an LH instruction is x0, the load still raises any applicable exceptions and performs any other side effects even though the loaded value is discarded.
RULE 3: If the effective address of an LH instruction is naturally aligned (divisible by 2), the instruction shall not raise an address-misaligned exception regardless of the execution environment implementation.
RULE 4: The LH instruction on an RV64I implementation must read a 16-bit value from the memory address formed by adding its sign-extended immediate offset to the base register.
RULE 5: After reading the 16-bit value, the LH instruction must sign-extend the value to 64 bits before writing the result to the destination register rd.
RULE 6: LH sign-extends the loaded 2-byte value to XLEN when the loaded size is less than XLEN.
RULE 7: Encodings of LH with only the rl bit set (load-release) are not supported.
RULE 8: The effective address used by LH must be processed by the pointer-masking transformation before the memory read is performed.
RULE 9: If an LH instruction accesses a non‑halfword‑aligned address (address bit 0 = 1), then the core must not raise a misaligned‑address exception and the destination register receives the 16‑bit value composed of the two consecutive bytes at that address (according to the current endianness).
RULE 10: If an LH instruction’s effective address has its low bit set (i.e., is not 2‑byte aligned), then the instruction must not raise an exception; instead it reads the two constituent bytes from the aligned addresses obtained after applying pointer‑masking to each byte address, assembles those bytes into a halfword, and writes that halfword into the destination register.
RULE 11: The set of bytes returned by a misaligned LH must be exactly the same as the bytes that would be obtained by individually applying pointer masking to each byte of the halfword and then assembling them.

INSTRUCTION: LHU
FORMAT: lhu rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: LHU copies the loaded value from memory into the destination register rd.
RULE 2: LHU computes its effective address by adding the contents of rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset.
RULE 3: LHU with destination register x0 must still raise any exceptions and perform all side effects even though the loaded value is discarded.
RULE 4: LHU whose effective address is naturally aligned (address divisible by 2) shall not raise an address-misaligned exception.
RULE 5: In a byte-address invariant system, each byte fetched by LHU reflects the value previously stored at its address independent of the system's endianness.
RULE 6: In a little-endian configuration, LHU places the byte from the lower memory address into the least-significant bits of rd and the byte from the higher address into the next higher bits.
RULE 7: In a big-endian configuration, LHU places the byte from the higher memory address into the least-significant bits of rd and the byte from the lower address into the next higher bits.
RULE 8: LHU loads a 16-bit halfword from memory and zero-extends the loaded value to the processor's XLEN before writing the result to register rd.
RULE 9: On an RV64I implementation, the XLEN is 64 bits, therefore LHU zero-extends the 16-bit memory value to a 64-bit result placed in rd.
RULE 10: The LHU instruction adds the XLEN-bit value of source register rs1′ to the zero-extended immediate uimm to compute the effective memory address.
RULE 11: The LHU instruction reads a halfword (bits 15 down to 0) from memory at the effective address.
RULE 12: The LHU instruction zero-extends the loaded halfword to XLEN bits and writes the result to destination register rd′.
RULE 13: If an LHU instruction is executed, then it never raises an exception and the destination register contains the zero‑extended 16‑bit value read from the memory address given by its effective address.
RULE 14: When an LHU instruction traps, the implementation must write a transformed instruction into the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst).
RULE 15: If any high-order bits of the effective address computed for an LHU instruction are non‑zero, the hardware masks those bits to zero before the memory access, so the halfword loaded into the destination register comes from the masked address.
RULE 16: If an LHU instruction accesses an address that is not halfword‑aligned, then the hardware performs two aligned halfword reads with pointer masking and assembles the result into the destination register; if the implementation does not permit misaligned loads, the instruction raises a load‑address‑misaligned exception (mcause = 4).
RULE 17: If an LHU load is generated by a CPU instruction (including those produced by extensions such as the FPU) and the pointer mask is not applied, then the destination register receives the value from the unmasked address; if an LHU load is generated by a non‑CPU source and the pointer mask is applied, then the destination register receives the value from the masked address.
RULE 18: If the MPRV or SPVP privilege-mode settings are active, the pointer-masking behavior of the LHU instruction follows the pointer-masking configuration of the effective privilege mode.
RULE 19: When MXR is active in the effective privilege mode that performs the LHU memory access, pointer masking does not apply to that LHU access.

INSTRUCTION: LPAD
FORMAT: lpad imm20
RULE 1: If the LPL field of an LPAD instruction is zero, the LPAD must not perform the label check and software may omit establishing an expected landing-pad label value in x7.
RULE 2: When the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED and the next instruction, which is expected to be an LPAD, is not 4-byte aligned, the processor must raise a software-check exception with cause 18 and xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".
RULE 3: When the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED and the next instruction is an LPAD whose LPL is non-zero and does not equal bits 31:12 of x7, the processor must raise a software-check exception with cause 18 and xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".
RULE 4: The LPAD instruction must be placed at a 4-byte aligned address; misaligned placement can cause the software-check exception described for the LP_EXPECTED state.
RULE 5: When Zicfilp is enabled and the LPAD instruction is executed while the PC is not 4-byte aligned and the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED, the LPAD instruction MUST raise a software-check exception with xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".
RULE 6: When Zicfilp is enabled and the LPAD instruction is executed while the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED, the LPL field of the instruction is non-zero, and the LPL field does not equal bits 31:12 of the x7 register, the LPAD instruction MUST raise a software-check exception with xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".
RULE 7: When Zicfilp is enabled and the LPAD instruction is executed while the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED, the PC is 4-byte aligned, and either the LPL field is zero or it matches bits 31:12 of the x7 register, the LPAD instruction MUST update the architectural ELP state to NO_LP_EXPECTED and MUST NOT raise a software-check exception.
RULE 8: When Zicfilp is enabled and the ELP state is not LP_EXPECTED, execution of the LPAD instruction MUST have no architectural effect (it is a no-op).
RULE 9: If the LPE field in the menvcfg CSR is 0, thereby disabling the Zicfilp extension in S-mode (or in U-mode when S-mode is not implemented), then execution of the LPAD instruction must produce no architectural effect (it operates as a no-op).
RULE 10: If the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode (the MLPE field in mseccfg is 0), execution of an LPAD instruction must have no architectural effect.
RULE 11: When the LPE field in the senvcfg CSR is 0, the LPAD instruction must operate as a no-op.
RULE 12: While the LPE field in the senvcfg CSR is 0, execution of the LPAD instruction must not update the ELP state, which remains NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 13: A software-check exception that occurs because the fetched instruction is not an LPAD instruction while ELP is LP_EXPECTED, or a software-check exception generated by an LPAD instruction itself, MUST result in a trap being delivered to the same privilege mode or to a higher privilege mode.

INSTRUCTION: LR.D
FORMAT: lr.d rd, (rs1)
RULE 1: LR.D loads a doubleword from the address in rs1, writes the value to rd, and registers a reservation set that subsumes the bytes of the addressed doubleword.
RULE 2: LR.D is defined only for RV64 implementations.
RULE 3: The address in rs1 for LR.D must be naturally aligned to eight bytes; if the address is not naturally aligned, an address-misaligned exception or an access-fault exception will be generated.
RULE 4: Execution of LR.D MUST invalidate any previous reservation held by the hart.
RULE 5: A hart MUST hold at most one reservation at a time.
RULE 6: Setting the aq bit on LR.D gives the LR/SC sequence acquire semantics.
RULE 7: LR.D registers a reservation set that subsumes the addressed doubleword, analogous to the behavior of LR.W.

INSTRUCTION: LR.W
FORMAT: lr.w rd, (rs1)
RULE 1: LR.W MUST load a word from the address in rs1, place the sign-extended value into rd, and register a reservation set that includes at least the bytes of the addressed word.
RULE 2: On RV64 implementations, LR.W MUST sign-extend the loaded 32-bit value before writing it to rd.
RULE 3: LR.W MUST cause an address-misaligned exception or an access-fault exception if the address in rs1 is not naturally aligned to a four-byte boundary.

INSTRUCTION: LUI
FORMAT: lui rd, uimm20
RULE 1: Executing a LUI instruction must write to the destination register a 32-bit value whose upper 20 bits are the instruction's 20-bit immediate and whose lower 12 bits are zero.
RULE 2: After a LUI instruction has loaded a register with its upper-immediate value, a subsequent JALR that uses that register as its base may add the signed 12-bit immediate to the register value to compute the jump target address.
RULE 3: LUI writes to integer register rd the 32-bit value formed by placing the 20-bit U-immediate into bits 31:12 and setting bits 11:0 to zero.
RULE 4: In RV64I, the 32-bit value produced by LUI is sign-extended to 64 bits before being written to rd.
RULE 5: The address offset that can be produced by pairing an LUI instruction with a subsequent load or store instruction in RV64I is limited to the inclusive range [−2^31−2^11, 2^31−2^11−1].
RULE 6: When a call-offset pseudoinstruction expands to a two-instruction sequence using LUI, the LUI writes the 20-bit immediate into bits 31:12 of the link register (x1 or x5) and sets bits 11:0 of that register to zero.
RULE 7: When a tail-offset pseudoinstruction expands to a two-instruction sequence using LUI, the LUI writes the 20-bit immediate into bits 31:12 of register x7 and sets bits 11:0 of x7 to zero.
RULE 8: If an indirect call or jump (e.g., jalr) uses x7 as the target register and bits 11:0 of x7 are not zero, then the PC is set to the full x7 value and, if that address is not 2‑byte aligned, an instruction‑address‑misaligned exception is raised.
RULE 9: The LUI instruction places its 20-bit immediate value into bits 31:12 of the destination register and clears bits 11:0 of that register to zero.

INSTRUCTION: LW
FORMAT: lw rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: LW MUST compute its effective address by adding the value in register rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset.
RULE 2: LW MUST load a 32-bit value from the memory location given by its effective address into the destination register rd.
RULE 3: LW with destination register x0 MUST still raise any applicable exceptions and perform all side effects even though the loaded value is discarded.
RULE 4: In a little-endian configuration, LW MUST transfer the byte from the lowest memory address of the accessed word into the least-significant byte of rd and similarly for the remaining bytes.
RULE 5: In a big-endian configuration, LW MUST transfer the byte from the highest memory address of the accessed word into the least-significant byte of rd and similarly for the remaining bytes.
RULE 6: LW whose effective address is naturally aligned (i.e., divisible by 4) shall not raise an address-misaligned exception regardless of the execution-environment identifier (EEI).
RULE 7: If the effective address of an LW is not divisible by 4, then a load‑address‑misaligned exception is raised (mcause = 4).
RULE 8: If an LW instruction accesses a memory address that is in an EEI‑protected region where reads are not permitted, or if the address is not 4‑byte aligned in a region that only allows word accesses, then the load raises an exception (load‑access‑fault for the permission violation, address‑misaligned for the alignment violation) and sets mcause to the corresponding fault code.
RULE 9: LW MUST load a 32-bit value from memory, sign-extend that value to 64 bits, and store the resulting 64-bit value into register rd on RV64I implementations.
RULE 10: If the effective address computed for an LW is not a 64‑bit value or lies outside the execution environment’s legal load address range, then the instruction raises a load‑access‑fault exception (mcause = 5) and the destination register is not written.
RULE 11: LW encodings that do not have the aq bit set are RESERVED.
RULE 12: If an lw rd, offset(x2) instruction is executed, then rd contains the 32‑bit word read from memory at address x2 + offset, sign‑extended to the processor’s XLEN.
RULE 13: The LW instruction loads a 32-bit value from the memory location whose effective address is the sum of the offset and the value in register x2, and writes that value into register rd.
RULE 14: LW loads a 32-bit value from the memory location whose effective address is computed and places that value into register rd′.
RULE 15: LW s4, 60(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 60 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register s4.
RULE 16: LW s3, 56(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 56 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register s3.
RULE 17: LW s2, 52(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 52 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register s2.
RULE 18: LW s1, 48(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 48 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register s1.
RULE 19: LW s0, 44(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 44 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register s0.
RULE 20: LW ra, 40(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 40 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register ra.
RULE 21: An LW instruction may raise an exception or interrupt if the memory access at its effective address causes a fault.
RULE 22: If an LW instruction raises an exception or interrupt, the load loop is terminated and subsequent adjustments of sp and the return operation are not performed.
RULE 23: LW is valid only when the processor is executing in M-mode, HS-mode, or U-mode with the hstatus.HU bit equal to 1.
RULE 24: LW raises the same architectural exceptions as any other load instruction.
RULE 25: If bits 1:0 of the trap‑instruction register equal 11 and the register’s value encodes a valid standard LW instruction, then the trapped instruction is interpreted as LW and the trap‑instruction register is overwritten with the transformed LW encoding, observable by reading the register after the trap.
RULE 26: If an LW instruction triggers a synchronous exception of type load‑address‑misaligned, load‑access‑fault, or load‑page‑fault, then the trap‑instruction register (e.g., mtval) may contain the transformed LW encoding.
RULE 27: If a transformed LW instruction has any non‑zero immediate offset field, then the processor raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 28: If the effective address used by an LW instruction is not masked by the current address‑mask register, then the load accesses the unmasked address and raises a load‑access‑fault exception (mcause set to the appropriate fault code).
RULE 29: The LW instruction MUST support misaligned accesses, and the same limitations that apply to misaligned accesses without pointer masking also apply when pointer masking is used.
RULE 30: When the LW instruction performs a misaligned access, the bytes loaded MUST be identical to the bytes that would be loaded if the pointer-masking transformation were applied separately to each aligned sub-access that composes the misaligned access.

INSTRUCTION: LWU
FORMAT: lwu rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: An LWU instruction reads a 32-bit word from the effective memory address and writes the zero-extended 64-bit value into the destination register.
RULE 2: If an LWU instruction causes any CSR read, CSR write, hardware‑generated CSR write, or DMA controller access, then the architectural state will show a changed CSR value (the post‑execution CSR readback differs from its pre‑execution value) or a DMA‑access indicator will be set.

INSTRUCTION: MAX
FORMAT: max rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: MAX must write to destination register rd the larger of the two single-precision floating-point operands in rs1 and rs2 according to IEEE-754 ordering.
RULE 2: MAX must produce the canonical NaN as the result when both rs1 and rs2 contain NaN values.
RULE 3: The MAX instruction MUST have the MIN/MAX field set to select the MAX operation.
RULE 4: If a MAX instruction does not write the quad‑precision maximum of its two source operands into rd, then the value read from rd is not that maximum.
RULE 5: The MAX instruction is only available when the Zbb extension is implemented.
RULE 6: Executing the MAX instruction writes to rd the greater of the integer values held in rs1 and rs2.
RULE 7: The MAX instruction does not generate any architecturally defined exceptions or permission checks.
RULE 8: The MAX instruction returns the larger of the two source register values.
RULE 9: The scalar MIN/MAX instruction must not be affected by the Zvkt extension and must perform its defined maximum operation semantics unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: MAXU
FORMAT: maxu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: MAXU computes the unsigned maximum of the values in source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the selected value to destination register rd.
RULE 2: The MAXU instruction MUST read the unsigned integer values from registers rs1 and rs2, compare them, and write the larger of the two values to register rd.
RULE 3: If the unsigned values in rs1 and rs2 are equal, the MAXU instruction MUST write that equal value to rd.
RULE 4: If the MAXU instruction is not implemented for the hart’s native XLEN (RV32 or RV64), then executing MAXU raises an illegal‑instruction exception; otherwise, executing MAXU writes to rd the unsigned maximum of rs1 and rs2, with the result represented in the hart’s native XLEN width.
RULE 5: Execution of the MAXU instruction MUST not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 6: MAXU compares rs1_val and rs2_val using an unsigned less-than comparison.
RULE 7: If the unsigned comparison determines that rs1_val is less than rs2_val, MAXU writes rs2_val into destination register rd.
RULE 8: If the unsigned comparison determines that rs1_val is not less than rs2_val, MAXU writes rs1_val into destination register rd.
RULE 9: MAXU does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: MIN
FORMAT: min rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: In RV64 implementations the MIN instruction is defined and must execute when encoded.
RULE 2: The MIN instruction computes the signed integer minimum of the values in source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes that result to destination register rd.
RULE 3: MIN rd, rs1, rs2 must compute the signed smaller of the two XLEN-bit operand values and write that result to rd.
RULE 4: The MIN instruction MUST write to rd the smaller of the signed integer values read from rs1 and rs2.
RULE 5: The MIN instruction performs the comparison between rs1 and rs2 using signed less-than semantics.
RULE 6: When the signed value in rs1 is less than the signed value in rs2, the MIN instruction MUST write the rs1 value to rd.
RULE 7: When the signed value in rs1 is not less than the signed value in rs2, including the case of equality, the MIN instruction MUST write the rs2 value to rd.
RULE 8: The MIN instruction must exhibit the same architectural behavior as defined for the scalar floating-point MIN instruction in version 2.2 of the RISC-V F/D/Q extension.

INSTRUCTION: MINU
FORMAT: minu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: MINU must interpret the values in registers rs1 and rs2 as unsigned integers and must select the smaller of the two values.
RULE 2: MINU must write the selected unsigned minimum value to the destination register rd.
RULE 3: MINU must perform a full-XLEN operation on its source operands and must produce a full-XLEN result written to rd.
RULE 4: A MINU instruction must write to rd the unsigned integer that is less than or equal to both unsigned values read from rs1 and rs2.
RULE 5: If the unsigned values in rs1 and rs2 are equal, a MINU instruction must write that equal value to rd.
RULE 6: MINU must compare rs1_val and rs2_val using unsigned less-than semantics.
RULE 7: MINU must set the internal result to rs1_val if rs1_val is less than rs2_val under unsigned comparison, otherwise it must set the result to rs2_val.
RULE 8: MINU must write the internal result to architectural register rd.
RULE 9: MINU must not alter any architectural state other than the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: MNRET
FORMAT: mnret
RULE 1: When the MNRET instruction is executed and the privilege mode that becomes active after the instruction is not Machine mode, the MDT bit is set to 0.
RULE 2: When the MNRET instruction is executed and the privilege mode that becomes active after the instruction is User mode, Virtual Supervisor mode, or Virtual User mode, the SDT bit in sstatus is set to 0.
RULE 3: Execution of MNRET MUST cause the processor to resume execution from the RNMI handler.
RULE 4: When IALIGN is 32, a write to bit mnepc[1] updates the stored mnepc value, but the MNRET instruction will still read that bit as 0 because the read is masked.
RULE 5: MNRET MUST be executed only when the current privilege mode is M, and executing MNRET in any other privilege mode is treated as an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 6: If MNRET changes the privilege mode to a mode that is less privileged than M, MNRET clears the MPRV bit in the mstatus CSR.
RULE 7: If the Zicfilp extension is implemented and MNRET selects privilege mode y as the new mode, MNRET writes to the ELP CSR the logical AND of the y LPE CSR and the MNPELP field of mnstatus.
RULE 8: When the MNRET instruction is executed, it must set the program counter to the value stored in the mnepc CSR.

INSTRUCTION: MRET
FORMAT: mret
RULE 1: MRET returns from a trap that was taken into machine mode (M-mode).
RULE 2: When MRET is executed, MIE is set to the value of MPIE.
RULE 3: When MRET is executed, the current privilege mode is changed to the privilege mode stored in MPP.
RULE 4: When MRET is executed, MPIE is set to 1.
RULE 5: When MRET is executed, MPP is set to the least-privileged supported mode, which is U-mode if U-mode is implemented otherwise M-mode.
RULE 6: When MRET is executed and the privilege mode stored in MPP is not M, MRET also sets MPRV to 0.
RULE 7: If the privilege mode entered after MRET is U, VS, or VU, MRET MUST set sstatus.SDT to 0.
RULE 8: When an MRET instruction changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than machine mode, the implementation must set MPRV to 0.
RULE 9: When a machine-mode handler executes the MRET instruction, the trap is redirected back to the privilege level that was active before the trap.
RULE 10: When IALIGN=32, the implementation MUST mask bit mepc[1] on all reads so that the read value of mepc[1] appears as 0, and this masking MUST also apply to the implicit read of mepc performed by the MRET instruction.
RULE 11: When IALIGN=32, the implementation MUST permit writes to bit mepc[1] even though reads of that bit are masked, and the writability of mepc[1] is unaffected by execution of the MRET instruction.
RULE 12: If MRET is executed in a privilege mode that is less privileged than machine, an illegal-instruction exception MUST be raised.
RULE 13: When MRET is executed, after performing the privilege-stack manipulation defined in Section 3.1.6.1, the program counter is set to the value stored in the mepc register.
RULE 14: When an MRET instruction is executed and the MPP field equals 3, the virtualization mode V MUST be set to 0.
RULE 15: MRET MUST determine the new privilege mode by evaluating the MPP and MPV fields in mstatus or mstatush according to their defined encoding.
RULE 16: MRET MUST set MPV to 0 in mstatus or mstatush.
RULE 17: MRET MUST set MPP to 0 in mstatus or mstatush.
RULE 18: MRET MUST set MIE to the previous value of MPIE in mstatus or mstatush.
RULE 19: MRET MUST set MPIE to 1 in mstatus or mstatush.
RULE 20: MRET MUST set the privilege mode to the mode previously determined from MPP and MPV.
RULE 21: MRET MUST set the program counter to the value stored in mepc.
RULE 22: When MRET is executed and the new privilege mode after the return has its LPE bit equal to 1, the effective privilege level (ELP) MUST be set to the value of the machine-mode PELP bit.

INSTRUCTION: MUL
FORMAT: mul rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The binary encoding of the MUL instruction in the Zmmul extension MUST be identical to the binary encoding of the MUL instruction defined in the M extension.
RULE 2: The MUL operation executed by c.mul computes the lower XLEN bits of the product of the values in the source registers and writes that result to the destination register, identical to the architectural effect of the standard MUL instruction.
RULE 3: The MUL instruction is defined only when the implementation provides either the M extension or the Zmmul extension and those extensions are enabled.
RULE 4: The MUL instruction treats the XLEN-bit values in rs1' and rs2' as signed two's complement integers and multiplies them to produce a 2 × XLEN-bit product.
RULE 5: The MUL instruction writes the low XLEN bits of the 2 × XLEN-bit product to the destination register rd'.
RULE 6: The high XLEN bits of the product computed by the MUL instruction are discarded and have no architectural effect.
RULE 7: The result of the MUL instruction is written to the destination register atomically as a single architectural update.

INSTRUCTION: MULH
FORMAT: mulh rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The MULH instruction supplied by the Zmmul extension MUST exhibit the same architectural behavior as the MULH instruction defined in the M extension.

INSTRUCTION: MULHSU
FORMAT: mulhsu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: MULHSU MUST interpret the value in rs1 as a signed XLEN-bit integer and the value in rs2 as an unsigned XLEN-bit integer, multiply them to form a full 2 × XLEN-bit product.
RULE 2: MULHSU MUST write the upper XLEN bits of the full 2 × XLEN-bit product to the destination register, discarding the lower XLEN bits.
RULE 3: When performing multi-word signed multiplication, using MULHSU with the signed most-significant word of the multiplicand in rs1 and an unsigned less-significant word of the multiplier in rs2 yields the upper XLEN bits of the product.

INSTRUCTION: MULHU
FORMAT: mulhu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: MULHU MUST return the upper XLEN bits of the full 2 × XLEN-bit product generated by unsigned multiplication of the values in rs1 and rs2.
RULE 2: MULHU performs an unsigned multiplication of the values in rs1 and rs2 and does not perform division or remainder operations.

INSTRUCTION: MULW
FORMAT: mulw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: In RV64, MULW MUST multiply the low-order 32 bits of the first source register with the low-order 32 bits of the second source register.
RULE 2: MULW MUST ignore all bits above bit 31 of both source registers when performing the multiplication.
RULE 3: MULW MUST produce a 32-bit product from the multiplication of the low-order 32 bits of the source registers.
RULE 4: MULW MUST sign-extend the low-order 32 bits of the product to 64 bits and write that value to the destination register.
RULE 5: MULW MUST not modify any architectural state other than the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: NTL.P1
FORMAT: ntl.p1
RULE 1: Execution of NTL.P1 MUST NOT modify any architectural state except that it advances the program counter to the next instruction and updates any applicable performance counters.
RULE 2: If NTL.P1 is executed, then the program counter is updated to the address of the next sequential instruction.
RULE 3: If NTL.P1 is executed and the permission checks that apply to HINT instructions are not satisfied (e.g., insufficient privilege or the opcode is reserved), then the processor raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2); otherwise the instruction has no architectural effect.
RULE 4: If executing NTL.P1 raises any exception or changes any architectural state (CSR, register, or memory) other than the normal program‑counter update, then the test observes an unexpected exception or a non‑zero difference in that state, violating the specification.

INSTRUCTION: OR
FORMAT: or rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The OR instruction is defined in the RV32I base integer ISA as a register-register (R-type) operation that works on integer registers.
RULE 2: When an OR instruction executes, the architectural registers identified by rs1 and rs2 are read as source operands and the computed result is written to the architectural register identified by rd.
RULE 3: The OR instruction performs a bitwise logical OR of the two source register values, producing a result in which each bit is 1 if the corresponding bit of either source operand is 1, and 0 otherwise.
RULE 4: The OR instruction computes rd = rs1 | rs2, where each bit of the result is 1 if the corresponding bit of rs1 or rs2 is 1, and writes the result to rd.

INSTRUCTION: ORC.B
FORMAT: orc.b rd, rs1
RULE 1: ORC.B MUST read the architectural value of the source register rs and consider only the least-significant 8 bits of that value, ignoring all more-significant bits.
RULE 2: ORC.B MUST perform the defined bitwise OR-Combine operation on the least-significant 8 bits of the source register to produce an 8-bit intermediate result.
RULE 3: ORC.B MUST write an XLEN-wide value to the destination register rd that is either sign-extended or zero-extended from the 8-bit intermediate result, as specified for the instruction.
RULE 4: ORC.B MUST not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 5: ORC.B MUST set each byte of the destination register rd to all zeros when the corresponding byte of the source register rs contains no bits that are set to one.
RULE 6: ORC.B MUST set each byte of the destination register rd to all ones when the corresponding byte of the source register rs contains at least one bit that is set to one.
RULE 7: ORC.B computes an XLEN-bit output where for each byte the output byte is 0x00 if the corresponding byte of the value read from rs is zero, otherwise the output byte is 0xFF.
RULE 8: ORC.B writes the computed XLEN-bit output to destination register rd.
RULE 9: ORC.B does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: ORI
FORMAT: ori rd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: The ORI instruction MUST sign-extend the 12-bit immediate to the processor's XLEN before performing the operation.
RULE 2: The ORI instruction MUST compute the bitwise OR of the sign-extended immediate and the value in source register rs1.
RULE 3: The ORI instruction MUST write the result of that bitwise OR into destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: ORN
FORMAT: orn rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The ORN instruction MUST compute the bitwise OR of the value in source register rs1 with the bitwise complement of the value in source register rs2 and write the resulting value to destination register rd.
RULE 2: The ORN instruction is defined for RV64 implementations and produces a 64-bit result written to rd.
RULE 3: When an ORN instruction executes, the architectural result written to rd equals the bitwise OR of the value in rs1 and the bitwise NOT of the value in rs2.
RULE 4: Executing ORN with source registers rs1 and rs2 produces a destination register value equal to the bitwise OR of rs1 and the bitwise complement of rs2.
RULE 5: ORN rd, rs1, rs2 must compute the bitwise logical OR of the XLEN-bit value in rs1 and the bitwise inversion of the XLEN-bit value in rs2, and must write the XLEN-bit result to rd.
RULE 6: ORN must read the XLEN-bit values from registers rs1 and rs2 and must write the resulting XLEN-bit value to register rd without modifying any other architectural state.
RULE 7: The ORN instruction must compute the bitwise logical OR of the value in source register rs1 and the bitwise inversion of the value in source register rs2, and must write the resulting word to destination register rd.
RULE 8: The ORN instruction MUST compute the bitwise OR of the first source register and the bitwise NOT of the second source register and write the XLEN-wide result to the destination register.
RULE 9: The ORN instruction must read the value from source register rs1, compute the bitwise NOT of the value in source register rs2, perform a bitwise OR of these two values, and write the resulting value to destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: PACK
FORMAT: pack rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: If a PACK instruction is fetched on an implementation that is not RV64 (e.g., RV32), then the processor raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 2: The PACK instruction packs the low 16 bits of rs1 and the low 16 bits of rs2 into the 32-least-significant bits of rd and sign-extends the 32-bit result into the upper bits of rd.
RULE 3: When the PACK instruction executes, it extracts lo_half = X(rs1)[15..0] and hi_half = X(rs2)[15..0] and writes X(rd) = EXTS(hi_half @ lo_half).
RULE 4: On RV64, executing the PACK instruction with rs2 set to x0 yields the same architectural result as the ZEXT.H instruction.
RULE 5: The PACK instruction reads the lower XLEN/2 bits of the source register rs1 and treats them as the low half of the result.
RULE 6: The PACK instruction reads the lower XLEN/2 bits of the source register rs2 and treats them as the high half of the result.
RULE 7: The PACK instruction concatenates the high half taken from rs2 with the low half taken from rs1, forming a bit-vector of length XLEN.
RULE 8: If the PACK instruction writes a value to rd that is not the zero‑extended concatenation of the XLEN‑bit vector, or if it modifies any architectural state other than rd, then after execution rd contains an incorrect value and/or some other register or CSR differs from its value before the instruction.
RULE 9: PACK concatenates the low half of the first source register with the low half of the second source register to form the destination register value.

INSTRUCTION: PACKH
FORMAT: packh rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: An implementation that includes the Zbkb extension MUST implement the PACKH instruction for RV64.
RULE 2: The PACKH instruction packs the low-order bytes of its source registers into the destination register.
RULE 3: PACKH MUST write to bits 15:8 of the destination register rd the byte taken from bits 7:0 of rs2 and write to bits 7:0 of rd the byte taken from bits 7:0 of rs1.
RULE 4: PACKH MUST set all bits of rd above bit 15 (bits XLEN-1:16) to zero, thereby zero-extending the packed 16-bit result.
RULE 5: PACKH concatenates the extracted byte from rs2 as the high half and the extracted byte from rs1 as the low half to form a 16-bit value that becomes the least-significant 16 bits of rd.
RULE 6: PACKH zero-extends the 16-bit result so that all bits of rd above bit 15 are cleared to zero.
RULE 7: The architectural result of PACKH is equivalent to computing EXTZ(hi_half @ lo_half), where hi_half is X(rs2)[7:0] and lo_half is X(rs1)[7:0].
RULE 8: Executing the PACKH instruction packs the low-order byte of each source register into the destination register according to the architecture-defined packing order.
RULE 9: PACKH is defined only for the RV64 ISA variant and its encoding is reserved for implementations that do not support RV64.

INSTRUCTION: PACKW
FORMAT: packw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: PACKW ignores the upper 32 bits of its source registers rs1 and rs2.
RULE 2: If PACKW is executed, the destination register rd contains sign‑extend(rs2[31:0]) in bits 63:32 and sign‑extend(rs1[31:0]) in bits 31:0, so any change to rs1[63:32] or rs2[63:32] does not affect the value written to rd.
RULE 3: PACKW produces a 32-bit signed result that is sign-extended to the machine XLEN.
RULE 4: PACKW packs the low 16 bits of rs1 and the low 16 bits of rs2 into a 32-bit result.
RULE 5: PACKW extracts bits 15-0 of the first source register and writes them into bits 15-0 of the destination register.
RULE 6: PACKW extracts bits 15-0 of the second source register and writes them into bits 31-16 of the destination register.
RULE 7: PACKW sets bits 63-32 of the destination register to zero on RV64 implementations.
RULE 8: PACKW on RV64 forms a 32-bit intermediate value by concatenating the low 16 bits of rs2 as bits 31–16 and the low 16 bits of rs1 as bits 15–0.
RULE 9: PACKW on RV64 sign-extends the 32-bit intermediate produced from rs2 and rs1 to the full XLEN width and writes the result to rd.
RULE 10: When rs2 is x0, PACKW on RV64 produces the same architectural result as the ZEXT.H instruction.
RULE 11: PACKW on an RV64 implementation writes to rd a 64-bit value whose lower 32 bits are formed by concatenating the low 16 bits of rs2 as the high half and the low 16 bits of rs1 as the low half, and sign-extends bit 31 of that 32-bit result into the upper 32 bits of rd.
RULE 12: PACKW is available only when the Zbkb extension is implemented; if the Zbkb extension is not present, the PACKW encoding is reserved.
RULE 13: When executed on an RV64 implementation, PACKW packs the low 16-bit portions of the source registers into the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: PREFETCH.I
FORMAT: prefetch.i imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: Execution of PREFETCH.I must not alter any architecturally visible integer registers, floating-point registers, or memory state; any state change other than a possible instruction-cache prefetch is not architecturally defined.
RULE 2: If bits [4:0] of the immediate in a PREFETCH.I instruction are non‑zero, the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2); otherwise the effective address used for the prefetch is guaranteed to be a multiple of 32 bytes.

INSTRUCTION: REM
FORMAT: rem rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The REM instruction must produce as its result the remainder of the signed integer division of the value in rs1 by the value in rs2.
RULE 2: If the remainder produced by the REM instruction is nonzero, the sign of that remainder must equal the sign of the dividend (the value in rs1).
RULE 3: For signed division, when the division does not overflow, the relationship dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder must hold, where the remainder is the value returned by the REM instruction.
RULE 4: If rs2 is zero, the REM instruction must return a remainder equal to the dividend (the value in rs1).
RULE 5: Signed division overflow occurs only when the dividend is the most-negative integer and the divisor is –1; in that overflow case the REM instruction must return a remainder of zero.
RULE 6: When a signed division overflow occurs, the remainder produced by the REM instruction must be zero.

INSTRUCTION: REMU
FORMAT: remu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: REMU computes the remainder of the unsigned integer division of the value in rs1 (the dividend) by the value in rs2 (the divisor).
RULE 2: For the unsigned division underlying REMU, the dividend equals the divisor multiplied by the unsigned quotient plus the remainder produced by REMU.
RULE 3: If the divisor supplied to REMU (the value in rs2) is zero, REMU produces a remainder equal to the dividend (the value in rs1).
RULE 4: Because unsigned division overflow cannot occur, REMU never encounters an overflow condition and therefore does not raise an overflow exception.
RULE 5: The architectural result of REMU is defined solely by its remainder semantics and is unaffected by any microarchitectural fusion with a preceding DIVU instruction.

INSTRUCTION: REMUW
FORMAT: remuw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: REMUW uses the lower 32 bits of rs1 as the unsigned dividend and the lower 32 bits of rs2 as the unsigned divisor.
RULE 2: REMUW produces a 32-bit remainder that is sign-extended to a full 64-bit register value, regardless of the divisor value.
RULE 3: For all executions of REMUW where unsigned division does not overflow, the remainder value satisfies the identity dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder.
RULE 4: When the unsigned divisor supplied to REMUW is zero, REMUW returns the unsigned dividend as the remainder.
RULE 5: REMUW must not raise an exception or trap for a divide-by-zero condition; it returns the defined remainder value instead.
RULE 6: If the divisor operand of a REMUW instruction is zero, then the instruction raises a divide‑by‑zero exception; for any non‑zero divisor, no overflow exception can occur.

INSTRUCTION: REMW
FORMAT: remw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: REMW is an RV64 instruction that computes the signed remainder of the lower 32-bit value of rs1 divided by the lower 32-bit value of rs2, treating both 32-bit values as signed integers.
RULE 2: REMW always sign-extends the 32-bit remainder result to a full 64-bit value before writing it to the destination register.
RULE 3: When the lower 32-bit divisor (rs2) of a REMW execution is zero, REMW produces a remainder equal to the sign-extended lower 32-bit dividend (rs1).
RULE 4: When the lower 32-bit dividend of a REMW execution equals –2³¹ and the lower 32-bit divisor equals –1, REMW produces a zero remainder that is sign-extended to 64 bits.
RULE 5: For all other cases of REMW execution, the produced remainder satisfies dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder, where dividend and divisor are the signed lower 32-bit values of rs1 and rs2 respectively.

INSTRUCTION: REV8
FORMAT: rev8 rd, rs1
RULE 1: Executing REV8 rd, rs on an RV64 hart must write to rd the 64-bit value whose eight bytes are the bytes of rs in reverse order.
RULE 2: REV8 produces a result that is the byte-reversed representation of the architectural value contained in source register rs.
RULE 3: REV8 writes the byte-reversed result into destination register rd.
RULE 4: REV8 leaves the bit pattern within each individual byte unchanged while reversing the order of the bytes in the register.
RULE 5: The REV8 instruction MUST read the XLEN-bit value of the source register rs, reverse the order of its bytes across the full XLEN, and write the resulting XLEN-bit value to the destination register rd.
RULE 6: The REV8 instruction MUST operate on the full XLEN of the source register; no defined architectural behavior exists for reversing only a subset of the register width.
RULE 7: The REV8 instruction MUST NOT modify any architectural state other than writing the reversed byte result to rd.
RULE 8: Software may obtain a 32-bit byte-reversed result by executing REV8 followed by an arithmetic right shift (SRAI) of the destination register by XLEN-32, and may obtain a 16-bit byte-reversed result by executing REV8 followed by SRAI with shift amount XLEN-16.
RULE 9: REV8 MUST produce a destination register value whose XLEN-bit content consists of the bytes of the source register taken in reverse order, so that the most-significant byte of the source becomes the least-significant byte of the destination and vice-versa.
RULE 10: REV8 MUST operate on the full XLEN bits of the source register, reversing exactly XLEN/8 bytes regardless of whether the processor is RV32 or RV64.
RULE 11: REV8 MUST leave all architectural state unchanged except for writing the computed byte-reversed value to the destination register.
RULE 12: The REV8 instruction reads a source integer register, reverses the order of its bytes, and writes the resulting value to a destination integer register.
RULE 13: In an RV64 implementation, REV8 reverses the order of the eight bytes of the 64-bit source register so that byte 0 becomes byte 7, byte 1 becomes byte 6, byte 2 becomes byte 5, byte 3 becomes byte 4, byte 4 becomes byte 3, byte 5 becomes byte 2, byte 6 becomes byte 1, and byte 7 becomes byte 0 in the destination register.
RULE 14: The REV8 instruction reverses the order of the eight-bit bytes within the XLEN-bit value in rs1 and writes the resulting XLEN-bit value to rd.
RULE 15: When the vs1 field of a vector instruction encoding has the value 01001, the instruction must be decoded as REV8.

INSTRUCTION: ROL
FORMAT: rol rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: ROL rd, rs1, rs2 rotates left the bit pattern of the value in register rs1 by the amount specified in register rs2 and writes the resulting value to register rd.
RULE 2: ROL MUST compute the rotate distance as the value in rs2 masked to the low log2(XLEN) bits (i.e., rs2 modulo XLEN).
RULE 3: ROL MUST produce a destination value that is the bits of rs1 rotated left by the rotate distance, where each bit is taken from the position (i − rotate_distance) modulo XLEN of the original rs1.
RULE 4: ROL MUST not shift in zeros; bits that are shifted out of the most-significant side of the value must reappear at the least-significant side, implementing a left circular shift.
RULE 5: The ROL instruction rotates the bits of the source register left, with bits shifted out of the most-significant position reappearing in the least-significant position, and writes the rotated value to the destination register.
RULE 6: ROL rd, rs1, rs2 MUST rotate the XLEN-bit value in register rs1 left by a shift amount equal to the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of register rs2.
RULE 7: For a ROL instruction the shift amount shamt MUST be taken as bits [4:0] of rs2 when XLEN = 32 and as bits [5:0] of rs2 when XLEN = 64.
RULE 8: ROL MUST compute the result as (X(rs1) << shamt) OR (X(rs1) >> (XLEN − shamt)) using XLEN-bit unsigned arithmetic.
RULE 9: ROL MUST write the computed result into register rd, overwriting rd's previous architectural value.
RULE 10: The ROL instruction must compute the shift amount shamt from the least-significant log₂(XLEN) bits of rs2, using bits [4:0] when XLEN = 32 and bits [5:0] when XLEN = 64.
RULE 11: The ROL instruction must produce a result equal to (X(rs1) << shamt) | (X(rs1) >> (XLEN - shamt)).
RULE 12: The ROL instruction must write the computed result into the destination register rd.
RULE 13: The ROL instruction performs a left rotation of the bits of a register value.
RULE 14: ROL applied to a 32-bit operand produces a 32-bit result whose bits are rotated left by the shift amount reduced modulo 32.
RULE 15: ROL does not modify any architectural state other than delivering its result and does not raise an exception.
RULE 16: The shift operand of ROL is interpreted as an unsigned integer and only its lower 5 bits determine the effective rotation amount.
RULE 17: When the operand of ROL is a 32-bit value, the result is defined to be a 32-bit value regardless of the implementation's native register width.
RULE 18: ROL is defined for any integer shift amount; shift amounts outside the range 0-31 are handled by the modulo 32 reduction rather than being illegal or reserved.

INSTRUCTION: ROLW
FORMAT: rolw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The ROLW instruction MUST ignore the upper 32 bits of rs1 and rs2, using only the least-significant 32 bits of each operand.
RULE 2: The ROLW instruction MUST interpret the least-significant 32 bits of rs1 as a signed 32-bit value for the rotation operation.
RULE 3: The ROLW instruction MUST use the least-significant 32 bits of rs2 as the rotate amount.
RULE 4: The ROLW instruction MUST rotate left the 32-bit signed value from rs1 by the amount specified in the lower 32 bits of rs2.
RULE 5: The ROLW instruction MUST produce a 32-bit signed result that is sign-extended to the processor's XLEN.
RULE 6: If execution of a ROLW instruction raises any privilege check or exception that would not be raised by a normal w‑suffixed instruction, then the core takes an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause set to illegal‑instruction).
RULE 7: The ROLW instruction rotates the bits of the 32-bit word in rs1 to the left by the amount specified in rs2, inserting into the low-order positions the bits that were shifted out of the high-order positions instead of zeros.
RULE 8: The architectural result of executing ROLW rd, rs1, rs2 is equivalent to the result produced by the four-instruction sequence "neg …; sll …; srl …; or …" that would otherwise be used to achieve the same rotation.
RULE 9: The ROLW instruction is defined only for RV64 implementations and its encoding is reserved on RV32 implementations, providing no defined architectural effect on RV32.
RULE 10: The ROLW instruction rotates left the low 32 bits of the rs1 register by a rotate amount equal to the low 5 bits of the rs2 register.
RULE 11: The rotate amount used by the ROLW instruction is computed as the value of rs2 modulo 32 (i.e., the lower 5 bits of rs2).
RULE 12: During the ROLW operation, bits shifted out of the low 32-bit field are re-introduced on the right side of that field, effecting a circular left shift.
RULE 13: After completing the 32-bit rotation, the ROLW instruction writes the 32-bit result to the destination register rd and sign-extends the result to 64 bits.
RULE 14: If the rotate amount for the ROLW instruction is zero, rd receives the sign-extended low 32 bits of rs1 unchanged.
RULE 15: ROLW rotates left the least-significant 32-bit word of rs1 by a shift amount equal to the value of the least-significant five bits of rs2.
RULE 16: ROLW obtains the shift amount by extracting bits 4 down to 0 of rs2 and ignoring all higher bits.
RULE 17: ROLW computes the rotated value as (rs1_word << shamt) | (rs1_word >> (32-shamt)), where rs1_word is the zero-extended low 32 bits of rs1 and shamt is the extracted shift amount.
RULE 18: When the extracted shift amount for ROLW is zero, the low-order word of rs1 is left unchanged before sign extension.
RULE 19: ROLW sign-extends the 32-bit rotation result by copying bit 31 into all more-significant bits of the destination register.
RULE 20: ROLW writes the sign-extended 32-bit result into rd, replacing the entire XLEN-bit destination register.
RULE 21: ROLW does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 22: The ROLW instruction rotates left the least-significant 32-bit word of the source register rs1 by a rotate amount equal to the value of the least-significant 5 bits of the source register rs2.
RULE 23: For the ROLW instruction the rotate amount shamt is obtained by extracting bits 4 down to 0 of X(rs2).
RULE 24: For the ROLW instruction the 32-bit value rs1 used in the rotation is obtained by zero-extending bits 31 down to 0 of X(rs1).
RULE 25: For the ROLW instruction the rotation result is computed as (rs1 << shamt) | (rs1 >> (32 − shamt)) where the shifts are performed on the 32-bit zero-extended rs1 value.
RULE 26: The ROLW instruction writes to rd the 32-bit rotation result sign-extended to XLEN bits by replicating bit 31 of the result into all more-significant bits of X(rd).
RULE 27: In RV64 the ROLW instruction is defined, whereas in RV32 the ROLW encoding is not defined and its behavior is reserved.
RULE 28: The ROLW instruction uses the value read from source register rs2 as the rotation amount.
RULE 29: The ROLW instruction writes the result of rotating the rs1 value left by the rs2 amount into destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: ROR
FORMAT: ror rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The ROR instruction rotates right the bits of the value held in register rs1 by the amount specified in the low XLEN bits of register rs2, with the rotation count taken modulo XLEN, and writes the resulting value to register rd.
RULE 2: ROR rd, rs1, rs2 rotates the bits of the value in rs1 to the right by the amount specified in rs2 and writes the resulting value to rd.
RULE 3: ROR inserts into the vacated high-order bits the bits that were shifted out of the low-order side during the rotation.
RULE 4: ROR performs a circular shift, so bits that exit one end of the operand re-enter at the opposite end.
RULE 5: ROR provides the same architectural effect as a four-instruction sequence that uses neg, shift-left logical, shift-right logical, and bitwise OR to compute the rotated value.
RULE 6: The ROR instruction rotates the bits of a source register value to the right and writes the rotated result to the destination register.
RULE 7: ROR computes a rotate-right of the XLEN-bit value in rs1 by a shift amount taken from the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of rs2.
RULE 8: When XLEN is greater than 32, ROR extracts bits 5 down to 0 of rs2 to form the shift amount shamt.
RULE 9: ROR calculates the rotation result as (X(rs1) logical right-shifted by shamt) OR (X(rs1) logical left-shifted by (XLEN minus shamt)).
RULE 10: ROR writes the computed rotation result into rd, replacing the previous architectural value of rd.
RULE 11: If the extracted shamt for ROR is zero, the value written to rd is identical to the original value of rs1.
RULE 12: ROR treats the source registers rs1 and rs2 as XLEN-bit unsigned integers for all shift and logical operations.
RULE 13: ROR MUST extract bits 4 down to 0 of rs2 and use that 5-bit value as the rotation amount (shamt).
RULE 14: ROR MUST compute the rotation result as (rs1 >> shamt) OR (rs1 << (32 - shamt)), where the shifts are logical and rs1 is the zero-extended 32-bit word.
RULE 15: ROR MUST produce a result equal to the original 32-bit word of rs1 when the extracted rotation amount shamt is zero.
