RULE 16: The ROR instruction MUST compute the rotate amount shamt from the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of rs2, using bits 4..0 when XLEN = 32 and bits 5..0 when XLEN = 64.
RULE 17: The ROR instruction MUST produce a result equal to (X(rs1) >> shamt) OR (X(rs1) << (XLEN – shamt)), where the shifts are performed on XLEN-bit values.
RULE 18: The ROR instruction MUST write the computed result into destination register rd.
RULE 19: The ROR instruction MUST rotate right the least-significant 32-bit word of the value in source register rs1 by a rotate amount equal to the unsigned value of the least-significant five bits of source register rs2.
RULE 20: The ROR instruction MUST compute the 32-bit rotation result as (operand >> shamt) OR (operand << (32 - shamt)), where operand is the zero-extended low 32 bits of rs1 and shamt is the low-5-bit value of rs2.
RULE 21: The ROR instruction MUST be architecturally defined only when the Zbb or Zbkb extensions are implemented; otherwise the encoding is reserved.
RULE 22: ROR rotates a 64-bit value taken from a source register right by the immediate rotation amount imm, with bits shifted out on the right re-entering on the left, and produces a 64-bit result.
RULE 23: Executing ROR rd, rs1, rs2 rotates the bits of the value in rs1 right by the distance given by the low XLEN bits of rs2, with bits shifted out on the right re-entering on the left.
RULE 24: The result of the ROR operation is written to the destination register rd.
RULE 25: In the vector-scalar form of the ROR instruction, the rotate amount for all elements is taken from the low bits of integer register rs1.
RULE 26: For any form of the ROR instruction, only the low log2(SEW) bits of the rotate-amount value are used and all higher bits are ignored.
RULE 27: For any form of the ROR instruction, the rotation is performed on each element using an element width EEW equal to SEW.
RULE 28: For any form of the ROR instruction, the instruction completes without raising an exception and retires successfully.

INSTRUCTION: RORI
FORMAT: rori rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: RORI is an instruction that is defined only when either the Zbb extension or the Zbk extension is implemented.
RULE 2: RORI operates on the full XLEN-wide values of its registers because it is not a w-suffixed or .uw-suffixed instruction.
RULE 3: RORI rotates the bits of the XLEN-wide value in rs1 to the right by the amount specified in the immediate field shamt and writes the XLEN-wide result to rd.
RULE 4: RORI does not cause any architectural side effects other than writing the rotation result to rd and does not raise any defined exceptions.
RULE 5: The RORI instruction rotates the XLEN-bit value in register rs1 to the right by the amount given in shamt, inserting into the vacated most-significant bits the bits that were shifted out of the least-significant side, thereby performing a circular right shift.
RULE 6: The RORI instruction writes the result of the rotation into the destination register rd.
RULE 7: RORI rotates the bits of the source register right by the immediate amount and writes the rotated value to the destination register.
RULE 8: RORI performs a rotate-right of the value in rs1 by the amount given by the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt field.
RULE 9: When XLEN is greater than 32, RORI uses shamt[5:0] as the effective rotate amount.
RULE 10: RORI computes the result as (X(rs1) >> shamt) OR (X(rs1) << (XLEN – shamt)).
RULE 11: RORI writes the computed result into register rd, replacing the previous contents of rd.
RULE 12: RORI determines the effective shift amount by extracting the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt immediate field.
RULE 13: RORI uses shamt[5:0] as the effective shift amount when XLEN ≠ 32.
RULE 14: RORI computes the result as (X(rs1) >> shamt) | (X(rs1) << (XLEN - shamt)).
RULE 15: RORI writes the computed result into integer register rd.
RULE 16: RORI rotates the bits of the source register right by the immediate amount and writes the rotated result to the destination register.
RULE 17: RORI operates on the native XLEN width of the hart, performing a 32-bit rotation on RV32 implementations and a 64-bit rotation on RV64 implementations.
RULE 18: RORI instruction MUST be defined for RV64 implementations.

INSTRUCTION: RORIW
FORMAT: roriw rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: RORIW must ignore the upper 32 bits of its source register operand when executing.
RULE 2: RORIW must treat the least-significant 32 bits of its source operand as a signed 32-bit integer during the rotation operation.
RULE 3: RORIW must produce a 32-bit signed result and then sign-extend that result to the processor's XLEN.
RULE 4: An implementation that supports the Zbkb extension on RV64 must provide the RORIW instruction, and the RORIW instruction is not defined for RV32.
RULE 5: The architectural effect of the RORIW instruction is to rotate right a 32-bit word operand by an immediate amount.
RULE 6: RORIW must take the rotate amount from the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt operand.
RULE 7: RORIW must operate only on the least-significant 32-bit word of the source register rs1, ignoring all more-significant bits.
RULE 8: RORIW must zero-extend the selected 32-bit word of rs1 to XLEN bits before performing the rotation.
RULE 9: RORIW must compute the rotated 32-bit word as (rs1_data >> shamt) | (rs1_data << (32-shamt)), where rs1_data is the zero-extended 32-bit value and shamt is the effective rotate amount.
RULE 10: RORIW must sign-extend the 32-bit rotation result by copying bit 31 into all more-significant bits and write the resulting XLEN-bit value to rd.
RULE 11: RORIW must treat a rotate amount of zero as producing the original 32-bit word (after sign-extension) in rd.
RULE 12: When RORIW rd, rs1, shamt is executed, the processor extracts bits 31-0 of register rs1, zero-extends that word to XLEN bits, and uses the resulting value as rs1_data.
RULE 13: For RORIW the rotate amount shamt is taken from the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of the immediate; any higher bits of shamt are ignored.
RULE 14: RORIW computes result = (rs1_data >> shamt) | (rs1_data << (32 - shamt)), where the shifts are performed on the XLEN-bit rs1_data and the shamt value is defined by RULE 2.
RULE 15: RORIW writes the lower 32 bits of result into rd and sign-extends bit 31 of that word to all more-significant bits, so that X(rd) = sign-extend(result[31:0]).
RULE 16: RORIW rotates a 32-bit word taken from the source register right by an immediate shift amount.
RULE 17: The immediate shift amount used by RORIW is a 5-bit value and the rotation is performed modulo 32.
RULE 18: On RV64, execution of the RORIW instruction rotates the bits of the source register rs1 right by the immediate amount imm and writes the rotated value to the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: RORW
FORMAT: rorw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The RORW instruction ignores the upper 32 bits of its source registers and uses only the low 32 bits as input.
RULE 2: The RORW instruction produces a 32-bit signed result that is sign-extended to the processor's XLEN before being written to the destination register.
RULE 3: The RORW instruction does not cause any architectural side effects, permission checks, or defined exceptions beyond writing its result.
RULE 4: In an RV64 implementation the RORW instruction is defined, while in an RV32 implementation the RORW encoding is not defined.
RULE 5: Execution of the RORW rd, rs1, rs2 instruction rotates the low-word (bits 0-31) of the value in register rs1 right by the amount given by the value in register rs2, with bits shifted out on the right re-entering on the left, and writes the resulting 32-bit value to register rd.
RULE 6: The RORW instruction performs a bitwise rotation rather than a logical shift, so no zero bits are introduced during the operation.
RULE 7: When executed, RORW rotates right the low 32 bits of the first source register by the amount specified in the low 5 bits of the second source register and writes the 32-bit rotated value to the destination register.
RULE 8: If the core is RV64 and executes a RORW instruction, then the destination register contains the 32‑bit rotate‑right result zero‑extended to 64 bits; if the core is RV32 and executes the RORW encoding, then it raises an illegal‑instruction exception (undefined architectural behavior).
RULE 9: RORW MUST take the lower 32 bits of the value in rs1, zero-extend them to the XLEN width, and use that as the operand for the rotation.
RULE 10: RORW MUST extract bits 4 down to 0 of the value in rs2 to form a 5-bit rotation amount that determines how many positions the operand is rotated right.
RULE 11: RORW MUST compute the rotated value as (operand >> shamt) OR (operand << (32 - shamt)), where operand is the zero-extended 32-bit value and shamt is the extracted rotation amount.
RULE 12: RORW MUST write to rd the sign-extended 32-bit rotated result, replicating bit 31 of the rotated value into all higher bits of the XLEN-wide destination register.
RULE 13: RORW MUST ignore all bits of rs1 above bit 31 when performing the rotation, operating solely on the least-significant word.
RULE 14: RORW MUST treat the rotation amount as modulo 32 because only the low 5 bits of rs2 are used.
RULE 15: RORW MUST, when the rotation amount is zero, produce a result equal to the sign-extended original least-significant word of rs1.
RULE 16: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 rotates right the least-significant 32-bit word of register rs1 by a shift amount equal to the least-significant 5 bits of register rs2.
RULE 17: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 determines the shift amount shamt by extracting bits 4-0 of rs2 and ignoring all higher bits.
RULE 18: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 treats the least-significant 32 bits of rs1 as a zero-extended 32-bit value before performing the rotation.
RULE 19: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 computes the 32-bit rotation result as (rs1 >> shamt) | (rs1 << (32 - shamt)), where both shifts are logical and the width is exactly 32 bits.
RULE 20: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 sign-extends the 32-bit rotation result by copying bit 31 of the result into all more-significant bits of the destination register.
RULE 21: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 writes the sign-extended 32-bit result into register rd, replacing the previous contents of rd.
RULE 22: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 with a shift amount of zero leaves the least-significant word of rs1 unchanged before sign-extension.
RULE 23: RORW rotates right a 32-bit word taken from a source register and writes the rotated result to the destination register.
RULE 24: RORW is only implemented on RV64 implementations and is not available on RV32 implementations.

INSTRUCTION: SB
FORMAT: sb rs2, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: SB computes its effective memory address by adding the contents of register rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset.
RULE 2: SB stores the low-order 8 bits of register rs2 into the memory byte at the effective address.
RULE 3: SB shall never raise an address-misaligned exception because an 8-bit store is naturally aligned on any address.
RULE 4: If a load‑byte (LB) from the address targeted by an SB observes a byte value that is neither the original memory content nor the value written by the SB, then the SB did not execute atomically.
RULE 5: In a system where endianness is byte-address invariant, a byte stored by SB at a particular address is returned unchanged by any byte-sized load from the same address regardless of the endianness in effect.
RULE 6: SB must store the low-order 8-bit value from register rs2 into the memory byte at the effective address.
RULE 7: The SB (c.sb) instruction does not modify any architectural integer register.
RULE 8: SB instruction must form a 2-bit immediate uimm where uimm[1] is bit 5 of the instruction encoding, uimm[0] is bit 6 of the instruction encoding, and all higher bits uimm[31:2] are zero.
RULE 9: SB instruction must replace bits [7:0] of the addressed memory location with the stored byte while leaving all other bits of that byte-aligned location unchanged.
RULE 10: SB instruction has no architectural prerequisites and may be executed in any privilege mode.
RULE 11: When an SB instruction causes a Store/AMO address misaligned exception, the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst) shall be written with the transformed SB encoding rather than zero.
RULE 12: When an SB instruction causes a Store/AMO access fault exception, the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst) shall be written with the transformed SB encoding rather than zero.
RULE 13: When an SB instruction causes a Store/AMO page fault exception, the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst) shall be written with the transformed SB encoding rather than zero.
RULE 14: Because SB has a defined transformed encoding, a synchronous trap caused by SB for any of the listed exception causes shall result in the trap-instruction register containing the transformed SB rather than zero or a pseudoinstruction.
RULE 15: SB MUST be subject to pointer masking because pointer masking applies to all explicit memory accesses.
RULE 16: When MPRV is in effect, pointer masking for SB MUST use the pointer-masking configuration of the effective privilege mode.
RULE 17: If MXR is in effect at the effective privilege mode where an SB access occurs, pointer masking MUST NOT be applied to that SB access.
RULE 18: Misaligned SB accesses MUST be supported.
RULE 19: If a misaligned SB stores a byte to an address whose low bits are non‑zero, then the byte written to memory must equal the byte that would result from applying the pointer‑masking transformation to the aligned address of that byte; otherwise the stored byte differs from the expected masked value, indicating a violation.
RULE 20: If a SB instruction accesses an address that is not aligned and pointer masking is not present, then the core raises a store‑address‑misaligned exception (mcause = 0x7), exactly as for any other misaligned load or store.

INSTRUCTION: SC.D
FORMAT: sc.d rd, rs2, (rs1)
RULE 1: SC.D is defined only for RV64 implementations and operates on doubleword-sized operands analogously to SC.W.
RULE 2: The address held in rs1 for an SC.D must be eight-byte naturally aligned, and if it is not naturally aligned the implementation must raise either an address-misaligned exception or an access-fault exception.
RULE 3: An SC.D may succeed only when the address in rs1 is within the reservation set of the most recent LR instruction in program order.
RULE 4: An SC.D must fail when the address in rs1 is not within the reservation set of the most recent LR instruction in program order.
RULE 5: An SC.D must fail if a store from another hart to any byte of the reservation set can be observed to occur between the LR and the SC.D.
RULE 6: An SC.D must fail if any other SC instruction, to any address, appears in program order between the LR and the SC.D.
RULE 7: An SC.D must fail if a write from another device to any byte accessed by the LR can be observed to occur between the LR and the SC.D.
RULE 8: If an external device write updates the reservation set without modifying any byte accessed by the preceding LR, then executing an SC.D may either succeed (the destination register is written with 0) or fail (the destination register is written with 1).
RULE 9: The effects of an SC.D can never be observed by another RISC-V hart before the LR instruction that established the reservation is observed.
RULE 10: Executing an SC.D invalidates any reservation held by the executing hart.
RULE 11: When an SC.D succeeds, it writes the doubleword contained in rs2 to the memory address in rs1 and writes zero to rd.
RULE 12: When an SC.D fails, it does not modify memory at the address in rs1 and writes a non-zero value to rd.

INSTRUCTION: SC.W
FORMAT: sc.w rd, rs2, (rs1)
RULE 1: SC.W succeeds only if the reservation is still valid and the reservation set contains the bytes being written.
RULE 2: When SC.W succeeds, it writes the word from rs2 to the memory address in rs1 and writes zero to rd.
RULE 3: When SC.W fails, it does not write to memory and writes a nonzero value to rd.
RULE 4: SC.W must not retire unless it passes memory permission checks.
RULE 5: Executing SC.W invalidates any reservation held by the executing hart, regardless of success or failure.
RULE 6: On RV64, SC.W sign-extends the value placed in rd.
RULE 7: SC.W requires the address in rs1 to be naturally aligned to a four-byte boundary; otherwise an address-misaligned exception or an access-fault exception is generated.
RULE 8: SC.W fails if its address is not within the reservation set of the most recent LR in program order.
RULE 9: SC.W fails if a store to the reservation set from another hart can be observed to occur between the LR and the SC.W.
RULE 10: SC.W fails if a write from another device to the bytes accessed by the LR can be observed to occur between the LR and the SC.W.
RULE 11: SC.W fails if another SC (to any address) occurs between the LR and the SC.W in program order.
RULE 12: SC.W may succeed only if no store from another hart to the reservation set is observed between the LR and the SC.W and no other SC occurs between them in program order.
RULE 13: SC.W may succeed only if no write from a non-hart device to the bytes accessed by the LR is observed between the LR and the SC.W.

INSTRUCTION: SCTRCLR
FORMAT: sctrclr
RULE 1: Execution of the SCTRCLR instruction must zero all CTR Entry Registers for every DEPTH value.
RULE 2: Execution of the SCTRCLR instruction must set the CTR CCV field to zero.
RULE 3: After an SCTRCLR instruction and before the next qualified control transfer, a read of the ctrsource register must return zero.
RULE 4: After an SCTRCLR instruction and before the next qualified control transfer, a read of the ctrtarget register must return zero.
RULE 5: After an SCTRCLR instruction and before the next qualified control transfer, a read of the ctrdata register must return zero.
RULE 6: The first control transfer recorded after an SCTRCLR instruction must have the ctrdata.CCV field equal to zero.
RULE 7: If the SCTRCLR instruction is executed in U-mode and CTR state enable access restrictions do not apply, the implementation must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 8: If CTR state enable access restrictions apply, execution of the SCTRCLR instruction must not raise the illegal-instruction or virtual-instruction exception specified for U-mode or VU-mode.
RULE 9: If Smstateen is implemented and mstateen0.CTR = 1, then SCTRCLR must behave as described in Section 11.3.1.
RULE 10: If Smstateen is implemented, mstateen0.CTR = 0, and the current privilege mode is less privileged than M-mode, then execution of SCTRCLR must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 11: If Smstateen is implemented and the CSR bits mstateen0.CTR = 1 and hstateen0.CTR = 1, then an SCTRCLR write clears the enabled counters as specified in Section 11.3.1, causing subsequent reads of those counters to return zero.
RULE 12: Execution of SCTRCLR MUST reset the CtrCycleCounter.
RULE 13: If the Cycle Count Valid (CCV) field of the record generated immediately after executing SCTRCLR is not 0, then the architectural state shows a non‑zero CCV field (the CCV readback ≠ 0), indicating a violation.
RULE 14: Execution of SCTRCLR MUST have the same architectural effect as a write to the xctrctl CSR that resets the CtrCycleCounter and clears CCV.
RULE 15: The 32-bit encoding of SCTRCLR must have funct7 = 0b0001000, rs2 = 0b00100, rs1 = 0b00000, funct3 = 0b000, rd = 0b00000, and opcode = 0b1110011.

INSTRUCTION: SD
FORMAT: sd rs2, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: SD stores the low 64 bits of register rs2 into the memory location addressed by the instruction.
RULE 2: SD MUST atomically store a doubleword (2 × XLEN/8 bytes) to the memory address computed from the rs1 base register.
RULE 3: An SD instruction generated by the RV64C C.SDSP encoding MUST store the 64-bit value from source register rs2 into the memory location whose effective address is the stack pointer x2 plus the zero-extended offset scaled by eight.
RULE 4: In RV64 implementations (XLEN ≠ 32), the SD instruction stores the 8-byte double-word value from the specified integer register to the memory address formed by the base address with a zero offset.
RULE 5: The cm.push pseudocode for RV64 defines the number of bytes transferred by each SD as 8, reflecting that SD stores a double-word.
RULE 6: SD stores a 64-bit doubleword composed of the contents of register pair rs2 into memory at the effective address computed as rs1 plus the signed offset.
RULE 7: When the effective address of an SD instruction is a multiple of 8, the implementation must not raise an address-misaligned exception.
RULE 8: When the effective address of an SD instruction is a multiple of 4, each 32-bit word access performed by the store must be performed atomically.
RULE 9: When the effective address of an SD instruction is a multiple of 8, the implementation must not raise a misaligned-address trap.
RULE 10: If the source register specified for an SD (or C.SDSP) instruction is x0, the 64-bit value stored is zero and the source register is not architecturally accessed.
RULE 11: For RVWMO ordering and exception handling, an SD instruction is treated as a misaligned store.
RULE 12: If an exception is taken after the first of the two 4-byte operations of an SD, the architectural effect of the first operation remains visible.
RULE 13: SD sign-extends the 12-bit offset to the XLEN width before adding it to the value in rs1 to form the effective address.
RULE 14: SD must store the 64-bit value held in register rs2 to the memory location whose effective address is computed by adding the instruction's offset to the contents of register x2.
RULE 15: SD stores a 64-bit doubleword value from a register to memory.
RULE 16: SD obtains the 64-bit value from the architectural register rs2′, and when the instruction is the expansion of the compressed form c.sd the upper 32 bits are taken from the register rs2′+1.
RULE 17: If a misaligned SD store does not satisfy the same limitations that apply when pointer masking is not used, then the processor raises a store/AMO address‑misaligned exception (mcause = 6).
RULE 18: The effect of a misaligned SD store is identical to applying the pointer masking transformation to each aligned memory access that composes the store.
RULE 19: If MXR is enabled in the effective privilege mode at the time an SD instruction executes, pointer masking MUST NOT be applied to the store address.

INSTRUCTION: SEXT.B
FORMAT: sext.b rd, rs1
RULE 1: SEXT.B extracts bits 7:0 of the source register, treats bit 7 as a sign bit, and creates an XLEN-wide value by replicating bit 7 into all higher-order bits.
RULE 2: SEXT.B stores the XLEN-wide sign-extended value into the destination register, replacing its previous contents.
RULE 3: For the SEXT.B instruction, the .b suffix causes the operation to consider only the least-significant 8 bits of the source register.
RULE 4: The SEXT.B instruction extracts the least-significant 8 bits of the source register rs, sign-extends that 8-bit value to the processor's XLEN, and writes the XLEN-wide result to the destination register rd.
RULE 5: SEXT.B MUST sign-extend the least-significant 8 bits of the source register and write the XLEN-bit result to the destination register.
RULE 6: SEXT.B is architecturally equivalent to executing the sequence "slli rd, rs, (XLEN-8); srai rd, rd, (XLEN-8)".
RULE 7: The SEXT.B instruction must write to the destination register a value whose low-order 8 bits are identical to the source register's low-order 8 bits and whose remaining bits (bits XLEN-1 down to 8) are all equal to bit 7 of the source register's low-order byte, thereby sign-extending that byte to XLEN.

INSTRUCTION: SEXT.H
FORMAT: sext.h rd, rs1
RULE 1: SEXT.H rd, rs MUST sign-extend the least-significant 16 bits of the value in register rs and write the resulting XLEN-bit value to register rd.
RULE 2: SEXT.H MUST replicate bit 15 of rs into all bits of the result above bit 15.
RULE 3: SEXT.H MUST read rs, write the sign-extended result to rd, and MUST NOT modify any other architectural state.
RULE 4: When SEXT.H specifies x0 as rd, the sign-extended result MUST be discarded and no architectural state change occurs.
RULE 5: SEXT.H is defined for both RV32 and RV64 implementations and MUST produce an XLEN-bit result appropriate to the current XLEN.
RULE 6: SEXT.H rd, rs MUST read the value in integer register rs, extract bits 15 down to 0, and produce a result in which bits 15 down to 0 equal the extracted halfword and bits XLEN-1 down to 16 are all set to the value of bit 15 of rs.
RULE 7: SEXT.H rd, rs MUST write the sign-extended result into integer register rd, replacing the previous contents of rd.
RULE 8: SEXT.H rd, rs MUST NOT modify any integer register other than rd nor affect any memory location.
RULE 9: SEXT.H rd, rs MUST perform the sign-extension operation for the implementation's XLEN (the width of integer registers), replicating the half-word sign bit into all more-significant bits of the XLEN-bit result.

INSTRUCTION: SFENCE.INVAL.IR
FORMAT: sfence.inval.ir
RULE 1: Executing SFENCE.INVAL.IR while the hart is in U-mode MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 2: Execution of SFENCE.INVAL.IR in S-mode or VS-mode is always permitted regardless of the values of the mstatus.TVM and hstatus.VTVM fields.
RULE 3: When mstatus.TVM=1 or hstatus.VTVM=1, execution of SFENCE.INVAL.IR does NOT cause a trap because it has only ordering effects and no visible side effects.

INSTRUCTION: SFENCE.VMA
FORMAT: sfence.vma rs1, rs2
RULE 1: If the TVM bit is 1, executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction in S-mode must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 2: If the TVM bit is 0, executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction in S-mode is permitted and must not raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: If the value held in rs1 is not a valid virtual address, SFENCE.VMA has no effect and does not raise an exception.
RULE 4: When rs2≠x0, software should zero bits SXLEN-1:ASIDMAX of the rs2 value used by SFENCE.VMA, and current implementations must ignore those bits because they are reserved for future standard use.
RULE 5: Executing SFENCE.VMA in user mode must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 6: Executing SFENCE.VMA in supervisor mode or hypervisor-supervisor mode when the mstatus.TVM field is 1 must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 7: SFENCE.VMA requires the same execution permissions and raises the same exceptions as SINVAL.VMA, HINVAL.VVMA, and HINVAL.GVMA.

INSTRUCTION: SFENCE.W.INVAL
FORMAT: sfence.w.inval
RULE 1: Executing SFENCE.W.INVAL while the hart is in user mode MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 2: When the hart is in supervisor mode or virtual supervisor mode, SFENCE.W.INVAL is not affected by the mstatus.TVM or hstatus.VTVM fields and therefore is always permitted.

INSTRUCTION: SH1ADD
FORMAT: sh1add rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SH1ADD computes the value of rs1 shifted left by one bit, adds the value of rs2, and writes the XLEN-wide result to rd.
RULE 2: Because SH1ADD has no w or .uw suffix, the shift-left-by-1 and add operation is performed on the full XLEN-wide values of rs1 and rs2 without truncating the result to 32 bits.
RULE 3: SH1ADD MUST shift the value in rs2 left by 1 bit, add the shifted value to the value in rs1, and write the resulting sum to rd.
RULE 4: SH1ADD rd, rs1, rs2 produces rd equal to the XLEN‑bit result of (rs1 logically left‑shifted by one, inserting a zero into the least‑significant bit and discarding the most‑significant bit) added to rs2.
RULE 5: SH1ADD rd, rs1, rs2 adds the intermediate result of the left shift of rs1 to the value in rs2, performing XLEN-bit addition modulo 2^XLEN.
RULE 6: SH1ADD rd, rs1, rs2 writes the XLEN-bit sum to the destination register rd, replacing its previous architectural value.
RULE 7: SH1ADD sets X(rd) to the XLEN-bit sum of X(rs2) and the value obtained by zero-extending the low 32 bits of X(rs1) and shifting that value left by one bit.
RULE 8: SH1ADD treats the least-significant word of X(rs1) as an unsigned value by applying zero-extension before any further operation.
RULE 9: SH1ADD performs the addition defined in its operation modulo 2^XLEN, so any overflow is discarded.
RULE 10: SH1ADD updates only the destination register rd and leaves all other integer registers and architectural state unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: SH1ADD.UW
FORMAT: sh1add.uw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: If SH1ADD.UW is executed, then the destination register contains ((zero‑extend of the low‑32 bits of the shifted‑operand register) << 1) + value of the first source register, ignoring any upper‑32‑bit bits of the shifted operand.
RULE 2: SH1ADD.UW left-shifts the extracted unsigned 32-bit value by one bit.
RULE 3: SH1ADD.UW adds the one-bit-left-shifted unsigned 32-bit result to the full-XLEN value contained in the other source register.
RULE 4: SH1ADD.UW writes the full-XLEN sum produced by the addition into the destination register rd.
RULE 5: SH1ADD.UW performs the addition and result write as a full-XLEN operation, despite the shifted operand being derived from a 32-bit unsigned extraction.
RULE 6: If a SH1ADD.UW instruction is executed on an RV32 implementation, then the processor raises an illegal‑instruction exception (the architectural effect is otherwise undefined).
RULE 7: The SH1ADD.UW instruction left-shifts the unsigned value obtained from rs1 by one bit, inserting a zero into the least-significant bit.
RULE 8: The SH1ADD.UW instruction adds the shifted unsigned value from rs1 to the unsigned value obtained from rs2, producing an XLEN-bit result.
RULE 9: The SH1ADD.UW instruction writes the XLEN-bit result to destination register rd, overwriting its previous contents.
RULE 10: The SH1ADD.UW instruction performs the shift and addition modulo 2^XLEN, discarding any overflow beyond the XLEN-bit result.
RULE 11: SH1ADD.UW forms its second addend by extracting bits 31 down to 0 of the value in rs1, zero-extending that 32-bit word to XLEN bits, and then left-shifting the zero-extended value by one bit.
RULE 12: SH1ADD.UW adds the XLEN-wide value contained in rs2 to the second addend produced as specified for SH1ADD.UW, performing the addition modulo 2^XLEN.
RULE 13: SH1ADD.UW writes the XLEN-wide result of the addition to the destination register rd, leaving all other architectural state unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: SH2ADD
FORMAT: sh2add rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SH2ADD computes the value of rd by adding the result of a logical left shift of rs1 by two bits to the value in rs2.
RULE 2: SH2ADD shifts rs1 left by two bits using a logical shift that inserts zeros into the least-significant bits and discards any bits shifted out of the XLEN width.
RULE 3: SH2ADD adds the shifted rs1 value to rs2 using standard XLEN-wide integer addition that wraps around on overflow.
RULE 4: SH2ADD performs a left shift of the second source register (rs2) by 2 bits, adds the shifted value to the first source register (rs1), and writes the resulting sum to the destination register (rd).
RULE 5: The acceleration provided by SH2ADD works by adding a shifted index to a base address.
RULE 6: SH2ADD rd, rs1, rs2 must compute rd as the XLEN-bit result of adding rs2 to the value obtained by shifting rs1 left by two bit positions, discarding any overflow beyond XLEN bits.
RULE 7: SH2ADD rd, rs1, rs2 performs a logical left shift of rs1 by two bits, inserting zeros into the least-significant positions.
RULE 8: SH2ADD rd, rs1, rs2 adds the shifted rs1 value to rs2 using two's-complement addition modulo 2^XLEN and writes the lower XLEN bits to rd.
RULE 9: SH2ADD adds the first addend (the value of rs2) to the second addend (the zero-extended, left-shifted low word of rs1) using XLEN-bit modular arithmetic and writes the result to destination register rd.
RULE 10: SH2ADD writes the computed sum to rd and does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: SH2ADD.UW
FORMAT: sh2add.uw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SH2ADD.UW extracts an unsigned 32-bit value from the least-significant 32 bits of the register specified as rs2 and zero-extends that value to the processor's XLEN.
RULE 2: SH2ADD.UW left-shifts the zero-extended 32-bit value from rs2 by exactly 2 bits, producing an XLEN-wide result.
RULE 3: SH2ADD.UW adds the 2-bit-left-shifted XLEN-wide value to the XLEN-wide value contained in the register specified as rs1, performing the addition modulo 2^XLEN.
RULE 4: SH2ADD.UW writes the XLEN-wide result of the addition to the destination register rd.
RULE 5: SH2ADD.UW is defined only for implementations with XLEN = 64; the encoding is reserved for implementations where XLEN is not 64.
RULE 6: SH2ADD.UW is defined only for RV64 implementations and is not defined for RV32 implementations.
RULE 7: For SH2ADD.UW the value in source register rs2 is interpreted as an unsigned 32-bit word.
RULE 8: For SH2ADD.UW the unsigned word value from rs2 is left-shifted by 2 bits.
RULE 9: For SH2ADD.UW the shifted result is added to the value contained in source register rs1.
RULE 10: For SH2ADD.UW the sum produced by adding the shifted rs2 value to rs1 is written to destination register rd.
RULE 11: SH2ADD.UW does not define any architecturally specified exceptions or permission checks beyond the illegal-instruction exception for an undefined encoding.
RULE 12: SH2ADD.UW MUST read the XLEN-bit value of register rs2 and use it as the first addend (base) for the addition.
RULE 13: SH2ADD.UW MUST extract bits 31 down to 0 of register rs1, zero-extend the extracted word to XLEN bits, and treat the result as the second addend (index) before shifting.
RULE 14: SH2ADD.UW MUST left-shift the zero-extended index by two bit positions.
RULE 15: SH2ADD.UW MUST add the shifted index to the base value using XLEN-wide modular arithmetic and write the resulting XLEN-bit value to destination register rd.
RULE 16: SH2ADD.UW MUST not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: SH3ADD
FORMAT: sh3add rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SH3ADD rd, rs1, rs2 must compute rd = rs1 + (rs2 << 3) using XLEN-wide integer arithmetic appropriate to the current ISA (32-bit for RV32 and 64-bit for RV64).
RULE 2: If an SH3ADD instruction is executed on a system where XLEN ≠ 64, then it raises an illegal‑instruction exception (mcause = 2).
RULE 3: SH3ADD shifts the value in source register rs2 left by three bits as part of its operation.
RULE 4: SH3ADD adds the three-bit-left-shifted value of rs2 to the value in source register rs1 and writes the sum to destination register rd.
RULE 5: SH3ADD rd, rs1, rs2 computes the sum of rs2 and the value of rs1 shifted left by 3 bits, and writes the XLEN-bit result to register rd.
RULE 6: SH3ADD performs a logical left shift of rs1 by 3 positions, inserting zeros into the vacated low-order bits and discarding any bits shifted out beyond XLEN.
RULE 7: SH3ADD adds the shifted rs1 value to rs2 using XLEN-bit modular arithmetic, so any overflow wraps around and only the low XLEN bits are written to rd.
RULE 8: SH3ADD does not perform any memory accesses, does not read or write any CSR, and does not modify the program counter except for the normal sequential advancement.
RULE 9: When the Zba extension is supported, execution of SH3ADD must complete without raising an exception unless a preceding trap or interrupt occurs.
RULE 10: SH3ADD rd, rs1, rs2 performs an XLEN-wide addition of the value in rs2 and the unsigned value obtained by extracting the low word of rs1 and shifting it left three bits.
RULE 11: If SH3ADD does not use the value in rs2 as the first XLEN‑sized addend, then the destination register rd will not contain rs2 + (rs1 << 2).
RULE 12: SH3ADD forms the second addend by zero-extending the least-significant 32-bit word of rs1 and then shifting that value left by three positions.
RULE 13: SH3ADD writes the XLEN-wide sum of the two addends to register rd, with the result reduced modulo 2^XLEN.
RULE 14: SH3ADD does not raise any architectural exception for the addition operation, even if the sum overflows XLEN bits.
RULE 15: SH3ADD permits rs1, rs2, and rd to refer to the same architectural register, and the defined addition semantics are applied without additional restrictions.

INSTRUCTION: SH3ADD.UW
FORMAT: sh3add.uw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SH3ADD.UW is defined only for RV64 implementations and its encoding is reserved (behavior not defined) on RV32 implementations.
RULE 2: For SH3ADD.UW the operand taken from rs2 is an unsigned 32-bit value extracted from the least-significant 32 bits of rs2.
RULE 3: SH3ADD.UW performs its arithmetic on the full XLEN width (64 bits on RV64) using the zero-extended 32-bit unsigned values from rs1 and rs2.
RULE 4: SH3ADD.UW computes the value (zero_extend(rs1[31:0]) << 3) + zero_extend(rs2[31:0]) and writes the 64-bit result to rd.
RULE 5: The architecture does not define any additional exception or permission conditions for SH3ADD.UW beyond the general illegal-instruction handling.
RULE 6: SH3ADD.UW is defined only for RV64 processors and its encoding is reserved on RV32 implementations.
RULE 7: SH3ADD.UW reads the lower 32 bits of integer register rs2, zero-extends them to 64 bits, and treats that as an unsigned word operand.
RULE 8: SH3ADD.UW left-shifts the zero-extended unsigned word operand by three bits using an unsigned logical shift, discarding any bits that overflow beyond the XLEN width.
RULE 9: SH3ADD.UW adds the shifted value to the 64-bit base operand from rs1 using 64-bit integer addition modulo 2^64.
RULE 10: SH3ADD.UW writes the 64-bit result of the addition to integer register rd.
RULE 11: SH3ADD.UW effectively computes rd = rs1 + ((rs2[31:0] << 3)), which is equivalent to adding rs1 to eight times the unsigned word index in rs2.
RULE 12: SH3ADD.UW MUST form the second addend by extracting bits 31 down to 0 of the XLEN-wide value in rs1, zero-extending that 32-bit field to XLEN bits, and then shifting the zero-extended result left by three bit positions.
RULE 13: SH3ADD.UW MUST take the first addend as the XLEN-wide architectural value contained in rs2.
RULE 14: SH3ADD.UW MUST add the first addend to the second addend using XLEN-wide modular arithmetic and write the XLEN-bit sum to the destination register rd.
RULE 15: SH3ADD.UW MUST not modify any architectural state other than updating rd with the addition result.

INSTRUCTION: SH
FORMAT: sh rs2, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: SH stores the low 16 bits of integer register rs2 to the memory location identified by the instruction's effective address.
RULE 2: If the effective address of an SH instruction is naturally aligned (divisible by 2), the SH shall not raise an address-misaligned exception.
RULE 3: If the EEI does not guarantee invisible handling of misaligned accesses, then executing a misaligned SH may either complete without raising an exception (the halfword is stored at the target address) or raise a misaligned‑address exception.
RULE 4: If the EEI does not guarantee invisible handling of misaligned accesses and a misaligned SH instruction is executed, then the processor raises a trap that must be either a contained (resumable) trap or a fatal (non‑resumable) trap as defined by the EEI.
RULE 5: If an SH instruction accesses a naturally aligned address (address % 2 == 0), then the halfword store is performed atomically—any concurrent load sees either the original halfword or the new halfword, never a mixed value; if the SH address is misaligned (address % 2 != 0), then the store is not guaranteed to be atomic and may be broken into multiple byte writes, allowing another hart to observe a partially updated halfword.
RULE 6: SH stores the low-order 16 bits of the value in register rs2 to the memory address specified by the instruction.
RULE 7: SH performs an explicit memory access that stores a half-word to the address computed by adding a signed offset to the contents of its base register.
RULE 8: SH MUST atomically store a 2-byte value taken from the low bits of register rs2 to the memory address calculated from register rs1.
RULE 9: SH MUST store the least-significant halfword of the source register rs2' into memory.
RULE 10: SH MUST form the immediate uimm such that uimm[31:2] = 0, uimm[1] = encoding[5], and uimm[0] = 0.
RULE 11: SH MUST compute the effective address by adding the zero-extended immediate uimm to the value held in the base register rs1'.
RULE 12: SH MUST write the 16-bit value to memory at the effective address, updating bits 15..0 of that memory location.
RULE 13: In a transformed SH instruction written to mtinst or htinst, the rs2, funct3, and opcode fields are identical to those of the trapping SH instruction.
RULE 14: During normal execution of an SH instruction without exception, mtinst or htinst is not written.
RULE 15: If an exception other than Store/AMO address‑misaligned, access‑fault, or page‑fault is taken on an SH instruction, then reading mtinst (or htinst) may return zero or any other permitted value and is not required to contain the transformed SH encoding.
RULE 16: If SH fails to apply pointer masking to its effective address, then the half‑word is stored at the unmasked address (observable by loading from that unmasked address and seeing the stored value, while loading from the masked address returns the original memory contents).
RULE 17: When SH is executed, the pointer-masking settings of the effective privilege mode, as possibly altered by MPRV or SPVP, are applied to the address used by SH.
RULE 18: If a SH instruction accesses an address whose least‑significant bit is 1 (misaligned), then the hardware splits the operation into two byte stores: it writes the low‑order byte of the source register to the aligned address (addr & ~1) and the high‑order byte to the next byte address ((addr & ~1)+1), leaving all other memory bytes unchanged.
RULE 19: Software reads or writes to CSRs are not subject to pointer masking, but the address subsequently used by SH after being obtained from a CSR is subject to pointer masking.
RULE 20: If an SH‑induced exception causes hardware to write a transformed address to a CSR and the SH pointer‑masking conditions are satisfied, then the value written to the CSR equals the transformed address with the pointer mask applied (low bits cleared); otherwise the CSR receives the unmasked transformed address.

INSTRUCTION: SHA256SIG0
FORMAT: sha256sig0 rd, rs1
RULE 1: Execution of the SHA256SIG0 instruction shall produce a result equal to the SHA-2-256 Sigma0 transformation applied to the instruction's input word, as specified in the NIST SHA-2 specification (2015).
RULE 2: If the value written to rd by SHA256SIG0 for source rs1 = X is not equal to ROTR(X, 2) ⊕ ROTR(X, 13) ⊕ ROTR(X, 22), then the instruction fails to implement the Sigma0 transformation defined in the SHA‑2‑256 specification.
RULE 3: On an RV64 implementation, SHA256SIG0 operates on the low 32 bits of the source register and sign-extends the 32-bit result to XLEN bits.
RULE 4: The functional result of SHA256SIG0 is defined as the bitwise XOR of a 7-bit rotate-right of the low 32-bit input, an 18-bit rotate-right of the same input, and the input shifted right logically by 3 bits.
RULE 5: The destination register written by SHA256SIG0 receives the 32-bit result sign-extended to the processor's XLEN.

INSTRUCTION: SHA256SIG1
FORMAT: sha256sig1 rd, rs1
RULE 1: SHA256SIG1 computes the SHA2-256 Sigma1 function on its input operands.
RULE 2: When SHA256SIG1 is executed on an RV64 implementation, the instruction extracts the low 32 bits of the source register rs1, applies the Sigma1 transformation to those bits, and then sign-extends the 32-bit result to 64 bits before writing it to the destination register rd.
RULE 3: If the SHA256SIG1 instruction is executed with source operand x, then the destination register receives the 32‑bit value rotr(x, 17) ⊕ rotr(x, 19) ⊕ (x >> 10).
RULE 4: The 32-bit result produced by the Sigma1 computation in SHA256SIG1 is sign-extended to the XLEN width of the executing architecture and written to the destination register rd.
RULE 5: Upon successful execution, SHA256SIG1 updates rd with the sign-extended result and then retires normally without raising an exception.

INSTRUCTION: SHA256SUM0
FORMAT: sha256sum0 rd, rs1
RULE 1: In an RV64 implementation, the SHA256SUM0 instruction extracts the low 32 bits of the source register rs1 and uses that 32-bit value as the input to the Sum0 transformation, ignoring the upper 32 bits.
RULE 2: The SHA256SUM0 instruction computes the result as the bitwise XOR of the 32-bit input rotated right by 2 bits, rotated right by 13 bits, and rotated right by 22 bits.
RULE 3: The SHA256SUM0 instruction writes the sign-extended 32-bit result into the destination register rd, filling the upper XLEN-32 bits with copies of the result's most-significant bit.
RULE 4: When executed with a valid destination register, the SHA256SUM0 instruction retires successfully without causing an exception.

INSTRUCTION: SHA256SUM1
FORMAT: sha256sum1 rd, rs1
RULE 1: When executed on an RV64 implementation, SHA256SUM1 takes the low 32 bits of source register rs1 as its input value and sign-extends the 32-bit result to XLEN bits before writing to rd.
RULE 2: SHA256SUM1 computes a 32-bit result equal to the bitwise XOR of ror32(inb, 6), ror32(inb, 11) and ror32(inb, 25), where inb is the 32-bit input taken from rs1.
RULE 3: SHA256SUM1 writes the sign-extended 32-bit result to the destination register rd.
RULE 4: SHA256SUM1 completes with normal retirement (RETIRE_SUCCESS) and does not raise an exception for any defined operand values.
RULE 5: SHA256SUM1 MUST compute the SHA-256 Sum1 function on its source operands and write the result to the destination register as defined by the SHA-2 specification.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG0
FORMAT: sha512sig0 rd, rs1
RULE 1: When the SHA512SIG0 instruction is executed on an RV64 implementation, it performs the SHA-2-512 Sigma0 transformation on the 64-bit source operand and writes the 64-bit result to the destination register.
RULE 2: If SHA512SIG0 is executed with source register rs1 holding a value V, then the destination register rd must contain (V rotr 28) xor (V rotr 34) xor (V rotr 39); otherwise rd holds an incorrect value, indicating the Sigma0 transformation is not implemented as specified.
RULE 3: SHA512SIG0 is defined for the RV64 base ISA and is not defined for other integer register widths.
RULE 4: SHA512SIG0 writes to the destination register the 64-bit value equal to the bitwise XOR of the source register rotated right by 1 bit, the source register rotated right by 8 bits, and the source register logically shifted right by 7 bits.
RULE 5: SHA512SIG0 causes a successful retirement of the instruction after updating the destination register as defined.
RULE 6: SHA512SIG0 is defined only for RV64 implementations; on RV32 implementations the encoding of SHA512SIG0 is reserved.
RULE 7: SHA512SIG0 performs the SHA-2-512 Sigma0 transformation on a 64-bit operand as specified by the RVK extension.
RULE 8: SHA512SIG0 must be recognized and executed only when the RVK extension is implemented on an RV64 core.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG0H
FORMAT: sha512sig0h rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: RV64 implementations must not define the SHA512SIG0H instruction.
RULE 2: SHA512SIG0H is defined only for RV32 implementations and is not defined for implementations with an XLEN other than 32.
RULE 3: SHA512SIG0H computes the high 32-bit half of the SHA-512 Sigma0 transformation using the low-half operand in rs1 and the high-half operand in rs2.
RULE 4: For SHA512SIG0H the 64-bit input value is formed by concatenating the contents of rs2 as the high 32 bits and rs1 as the low 32 bits, and the instruction produces the high-half result in rd.
RULE 5: SHA512SIG0H writes to rd the sign-extended value of ((X(rs1) >> 1) XOR (X(rs1) >> 7) XOR (X(rs1) >> 8) XOR (X(rs2) << 31) XOR (X(rs2) << 24)).
RULE 6: SHA512SIG0H completes execution with RETIRE_SUCCESS and does not raise an exception under normal operation.
RULE 7: SHA512SIG0H computes the SHA-2-512 Sigma0 high function on a 32-bit source operand and writes the 32-bit result to the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG0L
FORMAT: sha512sig0l rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: If a core that does not implement the Zknh extension or is not RV32 executes the SHA512SIG0L instruction, then it raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 2: The SHA512SIG0L instruction is not defined for any RV64 implementation, regardless of extension support.
RULE 3: Execution of the SHA512SIG0L instruction computes the low part of the SHA-512 Σ0 function as specified for the RV32 architecture.
RULE 4: SHA512SIG0L computes the low 32-bit half of the 64-bit SHA-512 σ0 function using the low 32 bits of registers rs1 and rs2 as inputs and writes the result to the low 32 bits of rd.
RULE 5: SHA512SIG0L writes to rd the sign-extended value of the 32-bit expression ((X(rs1) >> 1) ^ (X(rs1) >> 7) ^ (X(rs1) >> 8) ^ (X(rs2) << 31) ^ (X(rs2) << 25) ^ (X(rs2) << 24)).
RULE 6: If after executing SHA512SIG0L the destination register does not contain the correct sigma‑0 function result, then the instruction fails to retire successfully, causing an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 7: The SHA512SIG0L instruction computes the low 32-bit result of the SHA-512 Sigma0 function as defined by the SHA-2-512 algorithm.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG1
FORMAT: sha512sig1 rd, rs1
RULE 1: SHA512SIG1 computes the Sigma1 transformation defined by the SHA-512 algorithm, setting X(rd) to the bitwise XOR of ror64(X(rs1),19), ror64(X(rs1),61), and X(rs1) right-shifted by 6 bits.
RULE 2: SHA512SIG1 always retires successfully and does not raise any architectural exception.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG1H
FORMAT: sha512sig1h rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SHA512SIG1H is defined only for RV32 implementations and is not defined for RV64.
RULE 2: SHA512SIG1H must compute the high 32-bit half of the SHA-512 Sigma1 transformation using the specified bitwise operations on the values in rs1 and rs2.
RULE 3: SHA512SIG1H writes the sign-extended 32-bit result of the transformation to the destination register rd.
RULE 4: In SHA512SIG1H the source register rs1 supplies the low 32-bit half and rs2 supplies the high 32-bit half of the 64-bit input value to the Sigma1 transformation.
RULE 5: In SHA512SIG1H the destination register rd receives the high 32-bit half of the 64-bit Sigma1 output.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SIG1L
FORMAT: sha512sig1l rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SHA512SIG1L is defined only for RV32 implementations and must not be provided on RV64 or RV128.
RULE 2: SHA512SIG1L writes the low 32 bits of the computed expression, sign-extended to 64 bits, into the destination register rd, thereby updating rd with the low half of the Sigma1 transformation result.
RULE 3: SHA512SIG1L must retire successfully without raising any architectural exception when executed with valid operand registers.
RULE 4: SHA512SIG1L is not defined for RV64 processors, even if the K extension is present.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SUM0
FORMAT: sha512sum0 rd, rs1
RULE 1: SHA512SUM0 computes X(rd) = ror64(X(rs1),28) ^ ror64(X(rs1),34) ^ ror64(X(rs1),39) and writes this 64-bit result to the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SUM0R
FORMAT: sha512sum0r rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SHA512SUM0R computes the SHA-2-512 Sum0 transformation on a 64-bit input value formed from the two 32-bit source registers rs1 and rs2.
RULE 2: SHA512SUM0R treats each source register as a zero-extended 64-bit operand when performing the shift and XOR operations specified for the Sum0 function.
RULE 3: SHA512SUM0R produces a 64-bit result, discards the upper 32 bits, and writes the low 32 bits of the result to the destination register rd.
RULE 4: SHA512SUM0R executes the computation rd = EXTS(((X(rs1) << 25) ^ (X(rs1) << 30) ^ (X(rs1) >> 28) ^ (X(rs2) >> 7) ^ (X(rs2) >> 2) ^ (X(rs2) << 4))) where X(reg) denotes the zero-extended 64-bit value of the register.
RULE 5: In an RV64 implementation the SHA512SUM0R instruction is not defined, and its behavior is reserved.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SUM1
FORMAT: sha512sum1 rd, rs1
RULE 1: SHA512SUM1 performs the SHA-2 512-bit Sum1 operation as specified in the SHA-2 standard.
RULE 2: SHA512SUM1 must compute the SHA-512 Sum1 transformation by writing to the destination register the bitwise XOR of three 64-bit right rotations of the source register by 14, 18, and 41 bits.
RULE 3: SHA512SUM1 must retire successfully without raising any architectural exception when executed with a legal encoding.
RULE 4: SHA512SUM1 is defined only for RV64 implementations and is not defined for RV32 implementations.
RULE 5: If executing SHA512SUM1 rd, rs1, rs2 yields a destination register value that is not equal to ((rotr(rs1,14) XOR rotr(rs1,18) XOR rotr(rs1,41)) + rs2) modulo 2^64, then the processor does not implement the SHA‑512 Σ1 function as specified.

INSTRUCTION: SHA512SUM1R
FORMAT: sha512sum1r rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: An RV64 implementation must not define the SHA512SUM1R instruction.
RULE 2: SHA512SUM1R implements the Sum1 transformation as defined in the SHA-2-512 hash function (NIST 2015).
RULE 3: SHA512SUM1R is defined only for RV32 implementations and its encoding is reserved on RV64.
RULE 4: SHA512SUM1R completes execution and retires successfully without raising an exception when its encoding is defined.
RULE 5: If SHA512SUM1R is executed on an RV64 implementation, then it raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 6: SHA512SUM1R performs the SHA-2 512 Sum1 operation as its architectural effect.

INSTRUCTION: SINVAL.VMA
FORMAT: sinval.vma rs1, rs2
RULE 1: If the TVM bit in mstatus is 1 and the processor is executing in S-mode, an attempt to execute the SINVAL.VMA instruction MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 2: If the TVM bit in mstatus is 0, execution of the SINVAL.VMA instruction while in S-mode is permitted and must not raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: Executing SINVAL.VMA in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 4: Executing SINVAL.VMA in S-mode or HS-mode while mstatus.TVM=1 raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 5: Executing SINVAL.VMA in any other case does not raise an exception and the instruction executes with the same permissions as SFENCE.VMA.
RULE 6: When VTVM = 1, the condition that raises a virtual-instruction exception for SINVAL.VMA also raises a virtual-instruction exception for SFENCE.VMA.
RULE 7: SINVAL.VMA has its destination register field rd fixed to x0, so the instruction never writes an architectural register.
RULE 8: SINVAL.VMA is a privileged instruction and therefore must be executed only in a privileged privilege level.

INSTRUCTION: SLL
FORMAT: sll rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The SLL instruction must read the registers rs1 and rs2 as source operands and must write the resulting value into register rd.
RULE 2: The SLL instruction must shift the value contained in rs1 left logically by the amount specified in the lower five bits of rs2, and must store the shifted result in rd.
RULE 3: SLL MUST perform a logical left shift on the value in register rs1 by the shift amount held in register rs2.
RULE 4: In RV64I, SLL MUST consider only the low 6 bits of rs2 as the shift amount.
RULE 5: If a vector logical‑shift‑left instruction (VSLL.VV or VSLL.VX) is executed, then each destination element contains the corresponding source element shifted left by the element‑width‑masked shift amount with zeros shifted into the least‑significant bits and any bits shifted out discarded; if the shift amount is ≥ the element width, the destination element is zero.

INSTRUCTION: SLLI
FORMAT: slli rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: For an SLLI instruction, bits 11 through 5 of the immediate field (imm[11:5]) must be zero.
RULE 2: For an SLLI instruction, bit 30 of the encoding is not used and does not affect the operation.
RULE 3: An SLLI instruction performs a logical left shift of the value in rs1 by the amount specified in shamt[4:0].
RULE 4: An SLLI instruction inserts zeros into the vacated low-order bits of the result.
RULE 5: An SLLI instruction writes the shifted result to the destination register rd.
RULE 6: In RV64I, SLLI must use the lower 6 bits of the I-immediate field as the shift amount.
RULE 7: SLLI must perform a logical left shift, inserting zeros into the lower bits of the result.
RULE 8: SLLI performs a logical left shift of the value in the source register by the immediate amount, producing a shifted result.

INSTRUCTION: SLLI.UW
FORMAT: slli.uw rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: SLLI.UW extracts the least-significant 32 bits of the rs1 register, treats that field as an unsigned 32-bit value, zero-extends it to the processor's XLEN, shifts the XLEN-wide value left by the immediate amount, and writes the XLEN-wide result to rd.
RULE 2: The SLLI.UW instruction shifts the unsigned word in rs1 left by the immediate imm and writes the resulting value to rd.
RULE 3: SLLI.UW computes its result by extracting bits 31 down to 0 of the source register rs1, zero-extending that 32-bit word to the XLEN width, shifting the extended value left by the immediate shamt, and writing the final XLEN-wide value to destination register rd.
RULE 4: SLLI.UW zero-extends the least-significant word of rs1, meaning that after extracting bits 31..0 all higher bits of the intermediate value are set to zero before any shift is performed.
RULE 5: SLLI.UW uses the encoded immediate field as the shift amount shamt for a logical left shift, and the shift amount is applied directly to the zero-extended word.
RULE 6: The architectural effect of SLLI.UW is defined by the equation X(rd) = (EXTZ(X(rs)[31..0]) << shamt).
RULE 7: SLLI.UW produces the same architectural result as an slli instruction applied to the zero-extended 32-bit value of rs1.

INSTRUCTION: SLLIW
FORMAT: slliw rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: SLLIW operates on the low 32 bits of the source register, performs a logical left shift by the amount specified in the lower 5 bits of the immediate, and sign-extends the 32-bit result to 64 bits before writing it to the destination register.
RULE 2: When the immediate field bit imm[5] of a SLLIW encoding is zero, the instruction is defined and executes according to its specified operation.
RULE 3: Encodings of SLLIW with imm[5] not equal to zero are no longer defined to raise illegal-instruction exceptions; they are instead reserved.

INSTRUCTION: SLLW
FORMAT: sllw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: If the implementation does not support the RV64I base ISA and an SLLW instruction is executed, then an illegal‑instruction exception is raised (mcause = 2).
RULE 2: When an SLLW instruction is executed, the shift amount used by the operation MUST be the value of bits 4-0 of the second source register rs2.
RULE 3: When an SLLW instruction is executed, the low 32 bits of the first source register rs1 MUST be logically left-shifted by the shift amount, discarding any bits that are shifted out.
RULE 4: The 32-bit result of the logical left shift performed by SLLW MUST be sign-extended to 64 bits before being written to the destination register.
RULE 5: The destination register rd of an SLLW instruction MUST be written with the sign-extended 64-bit result, replacing its previous architectural value.

INSTRUCTION: SLT
FORMAT: slt rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The destination register for SLT is rd.
RULE 2: SLT does not cause arithmetic exceptions.
RULE 3: SLT performs a signed comparison of the values in rs1 and rs2 and writes the integer 1 to rd if the signed value in rs1 is less than the signed value in rs2.
RULE 4: SLT writes the integer 0 to rd if the signed value in rs1 is not less than the signed value in rs2.
RULE 5: SLT places the 1-or-0 result in the low XLEN bits of rd and any overflow bits are ignored.

INSTRUCTION: SLTI
FORMAT: slti rd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: SLTI writes its result to the destination register rd.
RULE 2: SLTI does not cause any arithmetic exception.
RULE 3: SLTI must write the integer 1 to register rd when the signed value in register rs1 is less than the sign-extended immediate.
RULE 4: SLTI must write the integer 0 to register rd when the signed value in register rs1 is not less than the sign-extended immediate.

INSTRUCTION: SLTIU
FORMAT: sltiu rd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: SLTIU must sign-extend the immediate field to XLEN bits before performing the comparison.
RULE 2: SLTIU must treat the sign-extended immediate as an unsigned integer for the comparison.
RULE 3: SLTIU must treat the value read from register rs1 as an unsigned integer for the comparison.
RULE 4: SLTIU must write the integer 1 to destination register rd if the unsigned value in rs1 is strictly less than the unsigned sign-extended immediate; otherwise SLTIU must write the integer 0 to rd.
RULE 5: When the immediate encoded in SLTIU equals 1, SLTIU must set rd to 1 if and only if the unsigned value in rs1 is zero, and must set rd to 0 otherwise.

INSTRUCTION: SLTU
FORMAT: sltu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: An SLTU instruction must write the result of the comparison into the architectural register specified as rd.
RULE 2: An SLTU instruction must treat the values in rs1 and rs2 as unsigned integers and must set rd to the integer 1 if the unsigned value in rs1 is less than the unsigned value in rs2, otherwise it must set rd to the integer 0.
RULE 3: When the rs1 operand of an SLTU instruction is the zero register (x0), the SLTU instruction must set rd to the integer 1 if the unsigned value in rs2 is not equal to zero, otherwise it must set rd to the integer 0.
RULE 4: SLTU MUST operate on XLEN-bit values.
RULE 5: In RV64I, SLTU MUST compare its two source operands as 64-bit unsigned integers.
RULE 6: When 32-bit unsigned values are held sign-extended in 64-bit registers, SLTU performed as a 64-bit unsigned comparison MUST produce the same result as a comparison of the original 32-bit unsigned values.

INSTRUCTION: SM3P0
FORMAT: sm3p0 rd, rs1
RULE 1: If the processor does not implement the SM3P0 extension (i.e., it is not an RV32 or RV64 base implementation), then executing an SM3P0 instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.
RULE 2: SM3P0 computes the result as r1 XOR rol32(r1, 9) XOR rol32(r1, 17).
RULE 3: If SM3P0 does not retire with RETIRE_SUCCESS, then a trap is taken and mcause is set to a non‑zero value.
RULE 4: When executed, SM3P0 must perform the SM3 P0 transform on its source operand and produce the transformed value as defined by the SM3 algorithm.

INSTRUCTION: SM3P1
FORMAT: sm3p1 rd, rs1
RULE 1: Executing SM3P1 performs the SM3 P1 transform as specified by the SM3 algorithm.
RULE 2: An implementation that includes the Zksh or Zks extension must provide the SM3P1 instruction for both RV32 and RV64 base architectures.
RULE 3: If SM3P1 rd, rs1 uses bits outside the low‑32 bits of rs1 as the operand r1, then the destination register rd will not contain the correct SM3P1 result (i.e., rd’s value will differ from the value computed when r1 is the low‑32 bits of rs1).
RULE 4: The SM3P1 instruction computes a 32-bit result equal to r1 XOR rol32(r1,15) XOR rol32(r1,23).
RULE 5: The SM3P1 instruction writes the sign-extended 32-bit result to the destination register rd.
RULE 6: The SM3P1 instruction completes with a normal successful retirement (RETIRE_SUCCESS) when executed with valid registers.
RULE 7: Execution of the SM3P1 instruction must perform the SM3 P1 transform as defined by the SM3 algorithm.

INSTRUCTION: SM4ED
FORMAT: sm4ed rd, rs1, rs2, bs
RULE 1: If an SM4ED instruction executes, then the destination register contains the SM4 block‑cipher encryption (or decryption) of the source register’s data.
RULE 2: If the shift amount used by an SM4ED instruction is not the 5‑bit value formed by concatenating the bs field with three zero bits (i.e., not bs × 8), then the destination register will contain a result that does not match the expected value computed with the correct shamt.
RULE 3: If the SM4ED instruction does not compute sb_in as the least‑significant byte of ((rs2 & 0xffffffff) >> shamt), then the destination register rd will contain a value different from the correct SM4ED result.
RULE 4: SM4ED applies the SM4 S-Box to sb_in and forms a 32-bit word x whose most-significant 24 bits are zero and whose least-significant byte is the S-Box output.
RULE 5: If the SM4ED instruction does not set the destination register to the 32‑bit value y = x ⊕ (x<<8) ⊕ (x<<2) ⊕ (x<<18) ⊕ ((x & 0x0000003F) << 26) ⊕ ((x & 0x000000C0) << 10), then the destination register contains a value different from that expression.
RULE 6: SM4ED rotates the 32-bit value y left by shamt bits (32-bit rotate) to produce the 32-bit value z.
RULE 7: On RV64 implementations, SM4ED writes the 32-bit result to rd sign-extended to XLEN bits.
RULE 8: If SM4ED is executed while any architecturally defined condition is violated, then the processor raises an exception (the instruction does not retire); otherwise, when all conditions hold, the instruction retires successfully with no exception.

INSTRUCTION: SM4KS
FORMAT: sm4ks rd, rs1, rs2, bs
RULE 1: SM4KS extracts an 8-bit byte from the low 32 bits of source register rs2 by right-shifting those bits by (bs × 8) positions and selecting the least-significant 8 bits of the shifted value.
RULE 2: SM4KS applies the SM4 S-box to the extracted byte and forms a 32-bit intermediate value x whose low 8 bits are the S-box output and whose upper 24 bits are zero.
RULE 3: If an SM4KS instruction reads source register x, then the destination register must be written with y = x ⊕ ((x & 0x00000007) << 29) ⊕ ((x & 0x000000FE) << 7) ⊕ ((x & 0x00000001) << 23) ⊕ ((x & 0x000000F8) << 13); any other value in the destination register indicates a violation.
RULE 4: SM4KS rotates the 32-bit value y left by (bs × 8) bits to produce the 32-bit value z.
RULE 5: When an SM4KS instruction executes, the destination register is written with the XOR of the rotated 32‑bit value z and the low 32 bits of rs1.
RULE 6: On RV64 implementations SM4KS sign-extends the 32-bit result to XLEN bits before writing it into destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: SRA
FORMAT: sra rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SRA produces as its result the arithmetic right-shift of the value in integer register rs1 by a shift amount equal to the low-order five bits of integer register rs2.
RULE 2: SRA must shift the value in register rs1 right by the shift amount contained in register rs2, performing an arithmetic right shift that replicates the sign bit into the vacated high-order bits.
RULE 3: In RV64I, SRA must consider only the low 6 bits of register rs2 when determining the shift amount.
RULE 4: The SRA narrowing right-shift extracts a smaller field from a wider operand and sign-extends the result.

INSTRUCTION: SRAI
FORMAT: srai rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: In SRAI the shift amount is taken from the lower five bits of the immediate field (shamt[4:0]).
RULE 2: SRAI must produce a result equal to an arithmetic right shift of the rs1 value by shamt bits, with the original sign bit copied into all vacated upper bits.
RULE 3: SRAI reads the value to be shifted from register rs1 and writes the resulting value to register rd.
RULE 4: In an RV64I implementation, SRAI takes the shift amount from the low-order six bits (imm[5:0]) of the immediate field.
RULE 5: SRAI performs an arithmetic right shift, copying the original sign bit of the operand into all vacated upper bits of the result.

INSTRUCTION: SRAIW
FORMAT: sraiw rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: SRAIW is defined only in RV64I implementations and operates on a 32-bit source operand.
RULE 2: SRAIW sign-extends the 32-bit result of the shift to produce a 64-bit destination register value.
RULE 3: The shift amount for SRAIW is taken from the lower six bits of the immediate field, and any encoding with imm[5] ≠ 0 is reserved.
RULE 4: SRAIW encodings with imm[5] ≠ 0 must not raise an illegal-instruction exception; their behavior is reserved and not defined by the architecture.
RULE 5: SRAIW performs an arithmetic right shift, copying the original sign bit of the 32-bit operand into all vacated upper bits of the 32-bit result before sign-extension.

INSTRUCTION: SRAW
FORMAT: sraw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SRAW uses the low 5 bits of rs2 (rs2[4:0]) as the shift amount for the arithmetic right shift.
RULE 2: SRAW applies an arithmetic right shift to the 32-bit value held in rs1 using the shift amount from rs2[4:0].
RULE 3: SRAW produces a 32-bit result from the shift and then sign-extends that result to 64 bits before writing it to the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: SRET
FORMAT: sret
RULE 1: Executing SRET sets the SIE bit to the value of the SPIE bit.
RULE 2: Executing SRET changes the current privilege mode to the mode encoded in the SPP field.
RULE 3: Executing SRET sets the SPIE bit to 1.
RULE 4: Executing SRET sets the SPP field to the least-privileged supported mode, which is U if U-mode is implemented, otherwise M.
RULE 5: If the mode restored by SRET is not M, then SRET clears the MPRV bit to 0.
RULE 6: When SRET is executed, the SPP field is a WARL field that may hold only S-mode and any implemented lower privilege mode.
RULE 7: When SRET is executed while the processor is in M-mode, the implementation MUST set the MDT bit to 0.
RULE 8: When the execution of SRET results in a new privilege mode of U, VS, or VU, the implementation MUST set sstatus.SDT to 0.
RULE 9: If an SRET instruction changes the privilege mode to a mode that is less privileged than M, then the instruction must set MPRV to 0.
RULE 10: The TSR field is a WARL field that determines whether execution of the SRET instruction in S-mode is intercepted.
RULE 11: When TSR = 1, an attempt to execute SRET while executing in S-mode MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 12: When TSR = 0, an attempt to execute SRET while executing in S-mode MUST NOT raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 13: SRET must be provided when supervisor mode is supported, and should raise an illegal-instruction exception when supervisor mode is not supported.
RULE 14: SRET should raise an illegal-instruction exception when the TSR bit in mstatus is 1.
RULE 15: Executing SRET in user mode (a privilege level lower than supervisor) must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 16: SRET may be executed in privilege mode S or in privilege mode M.
RULE 17: When SRET is executed, it sets the program counter to the value stored in the sepc register after performing the privilege-stack manipulation.
RULE 18: The Smrnmi extension MUST NOT change the behavior of the SRET instruction.
RULE 19: When an SRET instruction is executed, the current privilege level is set to user mode if the SPP bit is 0, and to supervisor mode if the SPP bit is 1.
RULE 20: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SPP bit is cleared to 0.
RULE 21: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SIE bit is loaded with the value of the SPIE bit.
RULE 22: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SPIE bit is set to 1.
RULE 23: The implementation must evaluate interrupt-trap conditions immediately after the execution of an SRET instruction.
RULE 24: Following an SRET instruction, any explicit write to a CSR on which interrupt-trap conditions depend (including sip, sie, or sstatus) must cause the interrupt-trap conditions to be evaluated immediately.
RULE 25: When IALIGN = 32, the implicit read of sepc performed by the SRET instruction must mask bit sepc[1] to zero, so that the read value appears with sepc[1] = 0.
RULE 26: If SRET is executed while the V bit equals 0, the implementation MUST set V to the value of SPV.
RULE 27: SRET MUST cause a return from a trap that was taken into HS-mode or VS-mode.
RULE 28: When SRET is executed while the current virtualization mode V field is 0 (i.e., in M-mode or HS-mode), SRET MUST set hstatus.SPV to 0.
RULE 29: When SRET is executed while the current virtualization mode V field is 0, SRET MUST set sstatus.SPP to 0.
RULE 30: When SRET is executed while the current virtualization mode V field is 0, SRET MUST copy the previous value of sstatus.SPIE into sstatus.SIE.
RULE 31: When SRET is executed while the current virtualization mode V field is 0, SRET MUST set sstatus.SPIE to 1.
RULE 32: When SRET is executed while the current virtualization mode V field is 0, SRET MUST restore the current privilege mode to the mode saved in sstatus.SPP prior to the trap.
RULE 33: When SRET is executed while the current virtualization mode V field is 0, SRET MUST set the program counter to the value contained in sepc.
RULE 34: SRET is recognized when bits [19:15] of the instruction are 00000.
RULE 35: SRET is recognized when bits [11:7] of the instruction are 00000.

INSTRUCTION: SRL
FORMAT: srl rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: SRL MUST read the contents of source registers rs1 and rs2 and write the computed result into destination register rd.
RULE 2: SRL performs a logical right shift on the value in register rs1 by the shift amount specified in register rs2, inserting zeros into the vacated high-order bits.
RULE 3: In RV64I, SRL considers only the low 6 bits of register rs2 as the shift amount and ignores all higher bits.
RULE 4: SRL MUST perform a logical right shift that zero-extends the vacated bits.
RULE 5: The SRL form may obtain its shift amount from a scalar x register.

INSTRUCTION: SRLI
FORMAT: srli rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: SRLI computes the result by logically shifting the value in rs1 right by shamt positions, inserting zeros into the vacated high-order bits.
RULE 2: SRLI writes the logical-right-shift result into the destination register rd.
RULE 3: SRLI does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 4: In RV64I implementations SRLI takes the shift amount from the lower 6 bits of the immediate field.
RULE 5: SRLI performs a logical right shift, inserting zeros into the upper bits of the destination register.
RULE 6: SRLI, when executed immediately after an SLLI that shifts a 16-bit value left, produces a zero-extended 16-bit result.

INSTRUCTION: SRLIW
FORMAT: srliw rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: SRLIW operates on the 32-bit value in rs1, logically right-shifts it by the specified amount, and then sign-extends the 32-bit result to produce a 64-bit result written to rd.
RULE 2: The shift amount for SRLIW is taken from the lower six bits of the I-immediate field, with imm[5] representing the most-significant bit of the amount.
RULE 3: SRLIW performs a logical right shift, inserting zeros into the upper bits of the 32-bit intermediate result before sign-extension.

INSTRUCTION: SRLW
FORMAT: srlw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: If an SRLW instruction executes, then the destination register contains the lower 32 bits of rs1 logically right‑shifted by the amount given in the low 5 bits of rs2, and the upper XLEN‑32 bits of the destination are cleared to zero.
RULE 2: SRLW uses the low five bits of register rs2 (rs2[4:0]) as the shift amount.
RULE 3: SRLW behaves analogously to the SRL instruction except that its operand and result are 32 bits and the result is sign-extended to 64 bits.

INSTRUCTION: SSAMOSWAP.D
FORMAT: ssamoswap.d rd, rs2, (rs1)
RULE 1: SSAMOSWAP.D atomically swaps a doubleword value with the contents of a shadow stack location.
RULE 2: If the current privilege mode is not Machine mode and the menvcfg.SSE field is zero, execution of SSAMOSWAP.D must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: If the current privilege mode is User mode and the senvcfg.SSE field is zero, execution of SSAMOSWAP.D must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 4: Otherwise, SSAMOSWAP.D atomically loads the 64-bit value from the memory address in rs1 into rd and stores the 64-bit value from rs2 to the same memory address.
RULE 5: The address held in rs1 for SSAMOSWAP.D must be naturally aligned to eight bytes, and if it is not, execution of SSAMOSWAP.D must raise the same exception that would be raised by the privilege-mode checks (illegal-instruction or virtual-instruction as appropriate).
RULE 6: When rd is x0, SSAMOSWAP.D still performs the atomic load and store but discards the loaded value.
RULE 7: If the SSE field in the menvcfg CSR is 0, execution of SSAMOSWAP.D must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 8: When menvcfg.SSE is 1, execution of SSAMOSWAP.D in U-mode must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 9: If the menvcfg.SSE field is set to one, executing the SSAMOSWAP.D instruction must raise a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 10: If satp.MODE (or vsatp.MODE when V=1) is Bare and the effective privilege mode is less than M, execution of SSAMOSWAP.D must raise a store/AMO access-fault exception.
RULE 11: If the effective privilege mode is M, execution of SSAMOSWAP.D must raise a store/AMO access-fault exception.
RULE 12: Writes to a memory region mapped as an SS page by any instruction other than SSAMOSWAP.D, SSPUSH, or C.SSPUSH must raise a store/AMO access-fault exception.
RULE 13: If SSAMOSWAP.D accesses a page whose pte.xwr field is neither 010b (shadow-stack) nor 001b (read-only), a store/AMO access-fault exception must be raised.
RULE 14: If the memory location accessed by SSAMOSWAP.D is not idempotent, execution of SSAMOSWAP.D must raise a store/AMO access-fault exception.
RULE 15: If the PMA attributes for the address accessed by SSAMOSWAP.D do not provide AMOSwap level support, SSAMOSWAP.D must raise a store/AMO access-fault exception.
RULE 16: If PMP configuration does not grant read-write permission for the address accessed by SSAMOSWAP.D, execution of SSAMOSWAP.D must raise a store/AMO access-fault exception.
RULE 17: For SSAMOSWAP.D memory accesses, the U and SUM bits are enforced according to the standard privilege rules.
RULE 18: For SSAMOSWAP.D accesses to a shadow-stack page, the MXR bit does not affect read permission.
RULE 19: When SSAMOSWAP.D triggers an exception, the reported exception cause code must be store/AMO access-fault (7), store/AMO page-fault (15), or store/AMO guest-page-fault (23) as appropriate, and the instruction type reported must be treated as a store/AMO operation.

INSTRUCTION: SSAMOSWAP.W
FORMAT: ssamoswap.w rd, rs2, (rs1)
RULE 1: If SSAMOSWAP.W is executed with a shadow‑stack address, then the word at that address is atomically replaced by the value in rs2 and the original word is written to rd.
RULE 2: SSAMOSWAP.W must raise an illegal-instruction exception when the current privilege mode is not Machine mode and the menvcfg.SSE bit is zero.
RULE 3: SSAMOSWAP.W must raise an illegal-instruction exception when the current privilege mode is User mode and the senvcfg.SSE bit is zero.
RULE 4: SSAMOSWAP.W must atomically load a 32-bit word from the address in rs1, sign-extend the loaded value to 64 bits, write the sign-extended value to rd, and store the lower 32 bits of rs2 to the same address when all privilege-mode and configuration checks succeed on an RV64 implementation.
RULE 5: SSAMOSWAP.W must require that the address in rs1 be naturally aligned to a four-byte boundary, and if it is not, the same exception behavior defined for AMO instructions applies.
RULE 6: If the menvcfg.SSE field is 0 and the current privilege mode is less privileged than M, execution of SSAMOSWAP.W MUST raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 7: If the menvcfg.SSE field is 1 and the current privilege mode is less privileged than M, execution of SSAMOSWAP.W MUST NOT raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 8: If the current privilege mode is M, execution of SSAMOSWAP.W MUST NOT raise an illegal-instruction exception regardless of the value of menvcfg.SSE.
RULE 9: If the SSE field in menvcfg is set to 1 and the processor is executing in U-mode, an execution of the SSAMOSWAP.W instruction must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
